It further argued that, given the energy- and labour-intensive production process and the limited annual demand, Union producers considered the product commercially unattractive. As regards the impact of the duties, Czech Blades claimed that blades made from the material in question account for a big share of its production by value.

- (139) Following disclosure, the Union industry supported the rejection of the request. It argued that the Commission had already found that such products are produced in the Union, that Union producers do supply cold rolled feedstock which Union re-rollers process into the narrow strips used for razor blades, that the measures do not prohibit imports but restore a level playing field, and that the cost impact is negligible.

- (140) The Commission first noted that it is for the party requesting exclusion to demonstrate, on the basis of complete, consistent and verifiable evidence submitted in a timely manner, that all of the relevant conditions for exclusion are cumulatively met. It is not sufficient for a party to establish one element in isolation; the request must be assessed as a whole against each of the five elements identified in recital (137).

- (141) As regards characteristics and interchangeability, the Commission noted that, while the additional information provides a more detailed description of the material imported by Czech Blades, it does not, in itself, establish that the Union industry does not manufacture products having the same or equivalent technical, chemical and physical characteristics. The material specification reflects the requirements applicable to Czech Blades’ own purchases and does not constitute evidence that Union producers are unable to meet those specifications. The fact that stainless steel may not constitute a suitable alternative does not establish that other carbon-steel products meeting equivalent technical requirements are unavailable. Czech Blades did not provide any evidence addressing specifically whether products manufactured by Union producers can meet the technical requirements identified in its specification.

- (142) As regards alternative sources of supply, the Commission noted that the evidence submitted by Czech Blades was not exhaustive and was based on statements rather than on verifiable data. The Commission also noted, as claimed by the Union industry (see recital (139)), that the narrow strip used for razor blades is obtained by Union re-rollers from cold-rolled feedstock supplied by Union producers, which confirms that the relevant production chain exists in the Union. Czech Blades therefore failed to demonstrate that the product cannot be sourced from Union producers

- (143) As regards the impact of an exemption on duties, the additional submission does not provide a specific quantification of the duties that would be avoided if the requested exclusion were granted. The information submitted focuses on the expected increase in the user’s input costs and does not quantify the corresponding impact of the exemption on the duties.

- (144) Overall, the additional information submitted after disclosure provides more detailed and specific evidence than was available when the Commission previously assessed the request. This additional information, however, does not relieve Czech Blades of the burden of proof described in recital (137), which requires positive evidence, not merely assertions, that each condition for exclusion is met. The additional information does not, on its own, establish that the products concerned have characteristics which distinguish them from cold-rolled products manufactured by the Union industry, or that products meeting the required characteristics, or technically interchangeable products, cannot be sourced from Union producers. The information concerning the steelmakers approached must therefore be assessed in conjunction with the technical specification in order to determine whether the alleged lack of alternative supply is based on technical limitations or on commercial considerations. Furthermore, the additional submission does not quantify the impact of the requested exemption on duties, while the estimates concerning the impact of the measures on Czech Blades require substantiation as regards the underlying assumptions and the causal link between the duties and the claimed loss of sales and employment. These are necessary elements of a properly substantiated exclusion request, and their absence independently justifies rejection of the request, irrespective of the assessment of the technical and supply-related evidence.

- (145) On this basis, the Commission therefore concluded that, Czech Blades failed to demonstrate that the conditions for exclusion of the products concerned from the scope of the measures were met. The request for exclusion was therefore rejected.

- (146) Following disclosure, Proterial Ltd., a cooperating non-sampled Japanese exporting producer requested the Commission to adjust the application of the definitive anti-dumping duty to their exports in light of the alleged unique nature of their product and its role in automotive applications.

- (147) The Commission noted that the product described by the party remains within the scope of the investigation. The arguments presented were not supported by evidence and therefore could not justify any product specific consideration. The Union interest was already assessed in the disclosure document, and no further data that would alter the Commission’s conclusions was provided. The claim was therefore rejected.

### 3. **DUMPING**

#### 3.1.   **Particular market situation: push-out effect of Chinese overcapacity**

##### 3.1.1.   *The push-out effect as a particular market situation under Article 2(3) of the basic Regulation*

- (148) For the purposes of this investigation, the Commission relied on the definition of overcapacity employed by the Global Forum on Steel Excess Capacity [^ntr46-L_202602547EN.000101-E0046] (‘GFSEC’): overcapacity is the gap between production capacity and demand. The complainant also followed such definition.

- (149) The complainant submitted that the CRF overcapacity in the People’s Republic of China (‘PRC’) pushed Chinese exports of CRF at low prices into the markets of all the countries concerned. The pressure of such exports would depress the prices of the domestic producers in the countries concerned to unsustainable levels. Therefore, sales prices of CRF in the countries concerned would be artificially low. In turn, such prices would lead to artificially low profits for the domestic CRF producers in the countries concerned when selling in their domestic markets.

- (150) As a consequence, the complainant argued that the normal value should be constructed in line with Article 2(3) of the basic Regulation and the actual profit data should be replaced to remove the effect of the particular market situation.

##### 3.1.2.   *Steel overcapacity in the PRC in general*

- (151) According to the complaint, while steel overcapacity is a global issue, the PRC is the original and most important source of overcapacity. As of 2024, the PRC held 47 % of the world’s crude steel capacity, corresponding to 1 173 million of metric tonnes [^ntr47-L_202602547EN.000101-E0047].

- (152) The complainant explained that the steel overcapacity in the PRC is not of a short-term nature due to a drop in demand, but rather of structural nature whereby, following the governmental policies, the creation of new production capacity in the PRC is disconnected from the expected demand. According to the OECD, while the former results from normal fluctuations in the business cycle, the latter refers to excess capacity that is due to subsidies and other forms of government support [^ntr48-L_202602547EN.000101-E0048].

- (153) More broadly, the GFSEC noted that overcapacity leads to oversupply of steel on international markets and depressed steel prices, as well as lower market shares and capacity utilisation rates for domestic steel producers in third countries that operate under market conditions [^ntr49-L_202602547EN.000101-E0049].

- (154) Against this background, the Commission own analysis of the Chinese steel industry confirmed the existence of a significant overcapacity in the steel sector in the PRC at least since 2014, thereby confirming its structural nature [^ntr50-L_202602547EN.000101-E0050]. Moreover, past Commission investigations showed that the intervention of the Government of the People’s Republic of China (‘GOC’) in the steel sector created significant market distortions [^ntr51-L_202602547EN.000101-E0051]. In addition, publicly available research also shows that the support granted at various levels by the GOC to steel companies indicates that the steel market in China does not operate under normal market forces [^ntr52-L_202602547EN.000101-E0052].

##### 3.1.3.   *CRF overcapacity in the PRC in particular*

- (155) With specific regard to CRF, the complainant showed that the Chinese CRF capacity was out of step with domestic demand. Indeed, as Chinese CRF demand between 2022 and 2024 remained stable and constantly below capacity, the tandem mill capacity consistently increased [^ntr53-L_202602547EN.000101-E0053].

- (156) The Commission, in its anti-dumping investigation on imports of CRF originating, inter alia, in the PRC [^ntr54-L_202602547EN.000101-E0054], had already found that in 2020 the total Chinese capacity of CRF was more than 120 million tonnes, while production and Chinese consumption in 2020 were both estimated at 100 million tonnes, resulting in a spare capacity estimated at 20 million tonnes in 2020. Based on the data in the complaint, in 2024 Chinese capacity increased further by 12 % compared to 2019 and by 9 % compared to 2021 [^ntr55-L_202602547EN.000101-E0055]. At the same time, domestic demand increased by only 3 % compared to 2019 and even decreased by 2 % compared to 2021 [^ntr56-L_202602547EN.000101-E0056]. No interested party challenged such data.

- (157) This increase in overcapacity was largely due to the financing plans of the GOC in response to the global financial crisis of 2008-2009 [^ntr57-L_202602547EN.000101-E0057]. Since Chinese overcapacity is supported extensively by GOC’s interventions, when domestic demand stalls, as for example in the aftermath of the Covid-19 pandemic due to the crisis of the construction sector, the Chinese market is affected by oversupply [^ntr58-L_202602547EN.000101-E0058].

##### 3.1.4.   *The consequence of Chinese overcapacity on prices*

- (158) The complainant showed that the combined result of increasing CRF capacity and decreasing CRF demand was a decrease in prices of CRF in the PRC. In CNY, the price of cold-rolled carbon steel in the PRC decreased between 2022 and 2024 [^ntr59-L_202602547EN.000101-E0059].

- (159) Therefore, Chinese steel producers were forced to export their excess production at the low prices resulting from overcapacity [^ntr60-L_202602547EN.000101-E0060].

- (160) Indeed, overcapacity in China has caused a surge of exports and depression of steel prices world-wide, and hence destabilised global steel markets. As a consequence, measures, including trade defence investigations, against Chinese steel imports, continued to increase in recent years [^ntr61-L_202602547EN.000101-E0061].

- (161) The Commission collected evidence in this respect in the course of the investigation, notably through the questionnaire reply and the verification of the authorities of the countries concerned. Such evidence showed that also the countries concerned were aware of the harmful effects of Chinese overcapacity and many of them took measures to counter it, as explained in the relevant sections.

- (162) While an analysis of the import volume and prices in each country concerned is included in the relevant sections, at this stage the Commission noted that, since Chinese domestic prices were affected by the overcapacity, Chinese import prices into the country concerned represented the externalisation of such Chinese overcapacity.

- (163) More specifically, in its anti-dumping investigation on imports of CRF originating, inter alia, in the PRC [^ntr62-L_202602547EN.000101-E0062], the Commission concluded that domestic sales prices of CRF in the PRC were affected by significant distortions within the meaning of Article 2(6a) of the basic Regulation [^ntr63-L_202602547EN.000101-E0063].

- (164) This conclusion was reached also based on the fact that ‘[t]he market of CRF [in the PRC] is served to a significant extent by enterprises which operate under the ownership, control or policy supervision or guidance of the PRC authorities’ [^ntr64-L_202602547EN.000101-E0064].

- (165) With regard to the prices in the markets of the countries concerned, which would be depressed due to the pressure of Chinese prices affected by overcapacity, the complainant explained that they would be artificially low because the level of the Chinese import prices is not based on market considerations. According to the complainant, since companies are so profoundly subsidised, they can accumulate years of losses and not reduce capacity.

- (166) The Commission, in the relevant sections below, reached a conclusion in this respect for each of the countries concerned, in view of the respective imports from the PRC and their level of prices.

##### 3.1.5.   *The consequence of Chinese overcapacity on profits*

- (167) The complainant explained that artificially low prices imply lower profits. In this respect, it reported evidence of the deteriorating profitability of Chinese steel producers in 2024 [^ntr65-L_202602547EN.000101-E0065] and of the fact that domestic steel prices and Chinese export prices were lower than the cost of production [^ntr66-L_202602547EN.000101-E0066].

- (168) As a consequence of the entry of Chinese imports affected by overcapacity, according to the complainant, prices in the markets of the countries concerned would be pushed down and domestic steel producers could no longer sell the same volumes, thereby reducing capacity utilisation. As a result, fixed costs skyrocket, which further depresses profits. Ultimately, domestic producers would no longer be able to invest, becoming less innovative and less efficient [^ntr67-L_202602547EN.000101-E0067].

- (169) In conclusion, the complainant submitted that domestic steel producers outside the PRC achieved profits lower than the profits they would have achieved had the Chinese overcapacity not surged the way it did [^ntr68-L_202602547EN.000101-E0068]. The complainant also provided profitability data of CRF producers in the countries concerned. The findings of the investigation in this regard are presented in the relevant sections below.

- (170) The Commission, in the relevant sections below, reached a conclusion in this respect for each of the countries concerned, in view of the respective imports from the PRC and their level of prices.

#### 3.2.   **India**

##### 3.2.1.   *Particular market situation due to the push-out effect caused by Chinese imports*

- (171) In order to assess the existence of a PMS in the Indian market due to the push-out effect caused by Chinese imports of CRF, the Commission first analysed the degree of penetration of Chinese imports in the Indian domestic market for CRF in terms of volume.

- (172) The Commission compared the volume of Chinese imports of CRF with India’s consumption. The Commission relied on production data, import data and export data submitted by the GOI as part of the reply to the questionnaire and which the Commission had verified on spot. The Commission estimated the consumption of CRF in India as total production plus imports and minus exports.

*Table 1*

**Consumption in India**

|  |  |  |  |  |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
|  | 2022 | 2023 | 2024 | Investigation period |
| Consumption in India (estimated) (tonnes) | 18 322 098 | 19 390 835 | 21 708 857 | 21 178 934 |
| All imports (tonnes) | 537 448 | 509 192 | 525 511 | 440 782 |
| Imports from China (tonnes) | 17 681 | 23 269 | 63 775 | 52 992 |
| Market share all imports (%) | 2,93 | 2,63 | 2,42 | 2,08 |
| Market share imports from China (%) | 0,10 | 0,12 | 0,29 | 0,25 |
| *Source*: Government of India, own calculations. | *Source*: Government of India, own calculations. | *Source*: Government of India, own calculations. | *Source*: Government of India, own calculations. | *Source*: Government of India, own calculations. |

- (173) Between 2022 and the investigation period, the market share of Chinese imports increased from 0,10 % to 0,25 %. On this basis, the Commission considered that the Chinese import volumes into India were insignificant in relation to consumption and too low to have caused a push-out effect.

- (174) Therefore, the Commission concluded that the Indian domestic market was not affected by a particular market situation resulting from Chinese imports due to overcapacity in the steel sector in the PRC.

- (175) Following the final disclosure, EUROFER asked the Commission to reconsider its findings summarised in recitals (171) to (174). The Commission noted that EUROFER did not submit any new information or evidence that would alter the Commission's assessment. Therefore, the Commission dismissed the claim and confirmed the findings and conclusions set out in recitals (171) to (174).

##### 3.2.2.   *Particular market situation due to measures on inputs*

###### 3.2.2.1.   Introduction

- (176) The complainant alleged that there was a PMS pursuant to Article 2(3) of the basic Regulation on the Indian steel market. The allegations were made in relation to:

- — iron ore including captive mining,

- — coal including captive mining,

- — steel scrap,

- — vertical integration of Indian steel producers.

- (177) The Commission investigated the allegations in the complaint and the comments received on initiation from JSW, the only sampled Indian exporting producer, which were summarised in recitals (58) to (66). The Commission also assessed the replies to the questionnaires submitted by GOI and by JSW, which had been verified on spot, as indicated in recital (103).

- (178) On the basis of the replies to the questionnaires, verification visits and submissions received, the Commission dismissed the allegations in the complaint on the existence of a particular market situation regarding coal, steel scrap and vertical integration of Indian steel producers because there were insufficient elements on file confirming these allegations in the context of this investigation. By contrast, the Commission investigated whether the allegations in the complaint concerning iron ore, including captive mining, were well founded and whether there was a particular market situation affecting the domestic prices of CRF as a result.

###### 3.2.2.2.   Existence of distortions in the iron ore market

###### 3.2.2.2.1.   Introduction

- (179) The Commission established in this investigation that HRF was the main input for producing CRF which required iron ore for its production. The Commission also established that, since JSW had a vertically integrated manufacturing process, the CRF produced from HRF had been produced using iron ore. Iron ore used in the production of HRF originated from one or several of the following three sources: i) domestic purchase of iron ore; ii) iron ore directly mined by the exporting producer and iii) imported iron ore.

- (180) The Commission established that the following measures implemented by the GOI were artificially lowering the price of iron ore sourced domestically for the production of the product concerned:

- — GOI interference in the domestic iron ore market;

- — GOI induced distortions in captive mining of iron ore for steelmaking;

- — Export tax on iron ore with an iron content above 58 %;

- — Restrictions to domestic rail transport of iron ore aimed at exports.

- (181) The Commission analysed each of these measures and their impact on the price of iron ore used for the production of CRF.

###### 3.2.2.2.2.   GOI interference in the domestic Indian iron ore market

- (182) The Commission investigated the role of the GOI in the domestic iron ore market and the effects of the GOI legislation and policies on the supply and price of iron ore. The Commission noted that one of the objectives of the 2017 National Steel Policy [^ntr69-L_202602547EN.000101-E0069], as officially communicated by the Government of India after approving it [^ntr70-L_202602547EN.000101-E0070], was to make available ample supply of iron ore domestically for the benefit of the downstream production of steel, which therefore included CRF. The Commission analysed how these GOI policies affected the price formation of iron ore for domestic sales in the Indian market and how they impacted the domestic market structure and the supply of iron ore for the steel producers.

- (183) The GOI explained, as confirmed by the 2016 Mineral Concession Rules [^ntr71-L_202602547EN.000101-E0071], that the Indian Bureau of Mines (IBM), which was a subordinate office under the Ministry of Mines, published monthly benchmark prices for several minerals including all sales of iron ore, which comprised those used for the production of CRF. The GOI added that companies selling iron ore must report monthly to IBM the volume and value of iron ore sold to all their customers. The IBM collected all the data and published a benchmark price resulting from the weighted average of all the sales reports for each type and grade of iron ore for each relevant region in India.

- (184) With regard to the market structure, two State Owned Enterprises (‘SOEs’), namely the National Mineral Development Corporation (‘NMDC’) and the Odisha Mining Corporation ‘(OMC’), made up for a substantial share of all the iron ore sold on the Indian market. Iron ore mines in India are owned by the State which may in turn grant a concession to operators to exploit them. The Commission investigated how the pricing of these mining rights for the different companies worked, and whether the mechanism of collecting prices for all sales via the Indian Bureau of Mines (‘IBM’) [^ntr72-L_202602547EN.000101-E0072] affected the domestic price of iron ore. The GOI explained that a reform in 2015 of the Law on Mines and Minerals Development and Regulation [^ntr73-L_202602547EN.000101-E0073] required the State to grant exploitation rights of iron ore mines through auctioning. The GOI further indicated that NMDC and OMC were exploiting mines that had not been subject to auctioning as they had acquired their mining rights well before the 2015 reform had been implemented, thereby called ‘legacy mines’.

- (185) The Commission sought to gather all other relevant information concerning the legacy mines, including their ownership and management, the pricing and the conditions of their mining rights. The GOI submitted only very limited information and provided little clarification on the information sought by the Commission. In particular, the GOI did not submit the cost reports for NMDC and OMC despite repeated requests from the Commission, as well as detailed information regarding the remuneration, duration and conditions for legacy mines and hence the impact of their market dominance on the formation of domestic prices. The Commission sent a letter to the GOI based on Article 18(4) of the basic Regulation on 15 June 2026 detailing all the information missing and the intention to base its decision on facts available.

- (186) The GOI replied on 25 June 2026 and claimed that it had cooperated fully with the investigation. Furthermore, the GOI alleged that if the Commission considered the information insufficient, it should have addressed it during the verification visit. Finally, the GOI submitted additional information on the issues raised in the Commission’s letter.

- (187) The Commission disagreed and considered that the information that the GOI had submitted prior to the Article 18(4) letter was insufficient to carry out its assessment. Furthermore, the Commission considered that the purpose of the verification visit was not to obtain information requested that was not submitted but instead to verify the information that had actually been submitted [^ntr74-L_202602547EN.000101-E0074]. As regards the additional substantive information submitted by the GOI, which had all been marked by the GOI as sensitive, the Commission did not consider that it changed its assessment and concluded that GOI should assess the Commission’s findings following disclosure to establish if its comments were relevant and applicable.

- (188) Pursuant to Article 18 of the basic Regulation, in light of the non-cooperation by the GOI, the Commission had to resort to facts available to draw conclusions on the price formation of domestic iron ore, namely with regard to the pricing and conditions for the mining rights under the system prior to 2015, their costs and prices of iron ore also as compared to the remaining market players other than the legacy mines, including JSW’s own production of iron ore.

- (189) On 31 July 2023 the Institute for Studies in Industrial Development (‘ISID’) as a part of the advocacy initiative of the Competition Commission of India published a study on competition in the mining sector with a focus on iron ore [^ntr75-L_202602547EN.000101-E0075]. The report confirmed that the two biggest players (NMDC and OMC) were largely the price makers of the IBM price and that the rest of the market players were price takers. The information submitted by GOI indicated that most of the mines operated by these two players were legacy mines. Furthermore, legacy mines operated by State companies such as NMDC and OMC had to pay 150 % of the royalty payable, that is, 22,5 % of the IBM price (150 % of 15 %). The Commission noted that this rate was significantly lower than the rates paid by JSW for the mines they were allocated after the year 2015 via auctioning. The Commission established on the basis of these sources that taking into account the significant market share and the low prices offered by the legacy mines, mainly due to the granting of mining rights by the GOI not subject to auctioning and the fact that these companies are owned and managed by the GOI, NMDC and OMC were the price makers of the IBM price and the other companies had to follow this price. The Commission concluded that, if NMDC and OMC had had to compete with other operators through auctioning for the right to exploit their legacy mines, these two companies would have had significantly higher costs and therefore would have had to sell at significantly higher prices to remain profitable.

- (190) Following the final disclosure, the GOI claimed that the evidence used by the Commission to state that NMDC and OMC were the predominant suppliers and therefore the price setters of the IBM price was flawed. First, the GOI claimed that the data used in the ISID report was from two years before the IP and therefore the 65 % market share attributed to NMDC and OMC could not be used. Second, the GOI submitted data labelled as sensitive which it claimed proved that the market share of NMDC and OMC was substantially lower than 65 %. Third, the GOI noted that the ISID report stated that iron ore was a deregulated sector and that the government did not have a direct role in fixing iron ore prices.

- (191) The Commission could not verify some the sensitive data submitted by the GOI, since it did not contain sufficiently granular information, in particular, the breakdown showing which companies had been grouped under captive and which ones under non-captive. In any case, the Commission would still consider a combined share of around 45-50 % as significant. More importantly, the Commission considered that the market share was not the only parameter to establish that NMDC and OMC were price setters. In this investigation, the cost advantage that NMDC and OMC benefitted from, as explained in recital (193), was also a key element that allowed these companies to set iron ore prices. In addition, the Commission explained in recital (193) that certain domestic suppliers had an incentive to keep IBM prices low to lower the amount of taxes paid. Furthermore, the Commission noted that the statement in the ISID report quoted by the GOI indicated that the government did not have a ‘direct’ role in fixing iron ore prices which did not mean that the government did not have an indirect role through the mechanism it established for legacy and auctioned mines. In that regard, the Commission concluded in paragraph (198), after the analysis set out in recitals (182) to (197), that the government did interfere in iron ore prices. Therefore, the Commission rejected the claim.

- (192) Following the final disclosure, the GOI submitted information labelled as sensitive to claim that the legacy lease terms did not result in below-market sale prices.

- (193) The Commission noted that information available on file, which was of a sensitive nature, showed, as stated in recital (189), that legacy mines paid significantly lower royalties than JSW for the mines that they had been allocated after the year 2015 via auctioning. Therefore, the Commission dismissed the claim. The Commission confirmed these findings on the basis of additional elements contained in the file. First, the data provided by JSW and verified by the Commission confirm the finding that IBM prices were artificially low. Due to confidentiality reasons, the Commission’s reasoning was disclosed specifically to JSW. Second, since levies such as concession rights and royalties were pegged to the IBM price, the pricing mechanism contained an inherent incentive for all domestic suppliers of iron ore to conform their prices to the IBM price (and therefore that of legacy mines) to minimise the amount paid in levies such as royalties and concession rights, thereby keeping the price of domestic iron ore artificially low.

- (194) In addition, the Controller and Auditor General of India of the Government of Odisha, which is an Indian public body, recommended in 2024 to the GOI, following an audit [^ntr76-L_202602547EN.000101-E0076], to investigate the reporting of low ex-mine prices by lessees, to ascertain whether this was being done deliberately in order to reduce the average sale price and, consequently, the royalty and premium payable.

- (195) Following the final disclosure, the GOI claimed that the 2024 report referred to in recital (194) did not establish underpricing. First, the report was limited to six audited lessees. Second, even if the lessees had under-reported prices, it would not prove that the IBM price was underpriced. Third, the report covered the period from 2020-2022 and was therefore not relevant for the IP. Fourth, the GOI claimed that the government has since taken measures aimed at preventing misreporting.

- (196) The Commission noted that it had further sensitive evidence on file as regards the interest of market players to keep a low IBM price. Regarding the report, first, it confirmed the reason why companies had an interest in keeping IBM prices low and, second, it provided actual examples of this practice. Since the functioning of the IBM price mechanism and its role to serve as an index for paying taxes has not changed, the Commission did not consider it relevant that the examples had occurred before the IP. The Commission also noted that the GOI did not submit evidence on the actual changes introduced by the GOI to reduce the under-reporting practice or an evaluation that the changes had been effective. Therefore, the Commission dismissed the claim.

- (197) Finally, the Commission noted two additional indicators showing that GOI policies were delivering on its objective to make iron ore domestically available for steel making including CRF. First, import quantities were negligible. Second, despite India being the fourth largest producer of iron ore in the world, the exported quantities were very small compared to the production quantities.

- (198) On the basis of all this information, as well as considering the other GOI measures on iron ore detailed in this section, the Commission therefore concluded that: (i) the GOI interference in the domestic iron ore market created a structure where the State-owned legacy mines were the predominant suppliers and hence the price setters; (ii) these legacy mines obtained their mining rights before the 2015 reform, and in the absence of GOI cooperation it was inferred that given their State ownership and that there was no auctioning at the time, their mining rights were granted at very favourable non-market conditions, allowing them to set the iron ore price at a much lower level than their competitors rather than at a fair market value; (iii) the mechanism of the IBM price implemented by GOI, which is used as an index for certain fees and taxes, led to an artificially low price for all types and grades of iron ore including for the iron ore supplied to JSW for the production of CRF.

###### 3.2.2.2.3.   The iron ore captive market in steelmaking

- (199) JSW owned the rights from the GOI to exploit some iron ore mines. Due to confidentiality reasons, additional details were disclosed specifically to JSW.

- (200) The Commission investigated whether the conditions and pricing for the mines used by JSW captively for the production of CRF were distorted as a result of GOI policies. The Commission established that the 2015 reform of the Mining Law referred to in recital (184) introduced the requirement for State governments to auction the allocation of mining rights of minerals, including iron ore. Pursuant to rule 6 of the Mineral (Auction) rules from 2015 [^ntr77-L_202602547EN.000101-E0077], applicants must meet the requirements in Section 5 of the Mines and Minerals Law [^ntr78-L_202602547EN.000101-E0078] which stated that a State Government could not grant a mineral concession unless the person was an Indian national or company. Therefore, by excluding foreign companies from the bidding process, the Commission did not consider the bidding process genuinely open as it was restricted to a limited number of potential domestic market players and excluded all the foreign iron ore operators that could potentially submit more competitive bids than domestic operators.

- (201) Following the final disclosure, the GOI claimed that the Commission had failed to demonstrate that excluding non-Indian bidders from concessions resulted in the distortion of iron ore captively sold.

- (202) The Commission considered that reducing the pool of eligible bidders effectively reduced competition and therefore prevented the possibility of receiving higher bids from non-Indian participants, a point that the GOI did not contest. The Commission considered this as a distortion and considered it irrelevant to speculate how the prices would have been if non-Indian bidders would have been allowed to participate. Therefore, the Commission dismissed the claim.

- (203) Furthermore, the Commission analysed the pricing conditions of the mining rights granted to JSW. Since the bidding rate for the mining rights and consequently the actual price for the mining rights is pegged to the IBM price, the ensuing royalties paid on the iron ore mined are directly affected by the IBM price level. As a result, the distortions stemming from GOI’s interventions in the domestic market of iron ore detailed in section 3.2.2.2.2 also directly affected the pricing of JSW’s iron ore mining rights. On this basis, the Commission concluded that the price for the iron ore extracted by JSW was also distorted.

- (204) On the basis of the legislative restrictions to grant mining rights according to the Mines and Minerals Law referred to in recital (200), as well as of the link between the royalties and concession rights granted to JSW with the distorted IBM price formation, the Commission concluded that also the iron ore captively sourced by JSW was affected by distortions on the Indian market.

###### 3.2.2.2.4.   Export tax on iron ore with an iron content above 58 %

- (205) In addition to the distortions concerning the price formation and the captive mining applicable to all grades of iron ore used by JSW, the GOI has had in place at least since 2012 and including the IP, an export tax on iron ore with iron content above 58 %. Due to confidentiality reasons, additional details on the use of that grade by the cooperating exporting producer were disclosed specifically to JSW.

- (206) During the IP the measure was in force pursuant to Notification No. 58/2022-Customs [^ntr79-L_202602547EN.000101-E0079] for iron ore with an iron content above 58 %. The Commission had already assessed this measure in the 2022 expiry review investigation concerning imports of tubes and pipes of ductile cast iron from India [^ntr80-L_202602547EN.000101-E0080] (‘ductile pipes’) where it had concluded in recital (147) that the measure entrusted or directed the domestic iron ore mining companies to sell iron ore locally at a cheap price. The Commission also concluded that the measure had achieved the desired policy effect to depress the domestic price of iron ore to an artificially low level to the advantage of the downstream industry including the producers of the product concerned, thereby distorting the domestic market of iron ore in India. In those decisions, the Commission took into account an overwhelming body of evidence, including the preparatory works of the relevant legislation and regulation, the proactive monitoring of the implementation of the measure by the GOI to ensure that the intended policy objective was accomplished, as well as the clear economic effects created by this restriction, leading to a clear domestic price decrease at the time of introduction of the export tax also reflecting its level.

- (207) Following the final disclosure, the GOI claimed that the export tax was applicable only to iron ore with an iron content above 58 %, which is one out of the seven grades tracked under the IBM pricing system and that the Commission did not have information on the iron ore grades procured by JSW. Furthermore, the GOI claimed that the Commission’s finding that the measure achieved the desired policy effect corresponded to a previous investigation related to a different product and different IP and therefore that it could not be used. The GOI further claimed that the substantial export volumes recorded in the IP contradicted the Commission’s findings.

- (208) The Commission noted that the sensitive information on file included sufficiently granular data on the iron ore grades procured by JSW to carry out the analysis. The Commission also considered that since the finding related to the same export tax related to the same grade of iron ore and which had not changed since the previous investigation, such finding remained valid. As regards the volumes of exports, the Commission found this claim irrelevant since an export tax is by nature an export restriction. In any case, as noted in recital (197), the overall volume of iron ore exports was very small compared with the production level, noting that, as indicated in page xi of the ISID report, India was among the world’s largest producers of iron ore. Therefore, the Commission dismissed the claim.

###### 3.2.2.2.5.   Restrictions to transport iron ore aimed at exports

- (209) The GOI adopted in 2021 the so-called ‘Iron Ore Policy from 2021’ [^ntr81-L_202602547EN.000101-E0081]. The stated aim of the measure was to promote domestic manufacturing of steel as stated in the first paragraph of its introduction. To that end, the policy set out four categories of customers ranked according to the priority to get allocated wagons for transporting iron ore. The customers that comprised exports of iron ore were placed in the fourth category of customers, that is, the category with the lowest priority. This measure created a clear bias in favour of domestic supply of iron ore, in line with the overarching GOI policies to provide an ample supply of iron ore at cheap prices for domestic downstream producers. Iron ore producers willing to export iron ore face an additional hurdle from this measure if they would consider exporting rather than supplying domestically the iron ore. This is also confirmed by the very low level of iron ore exports from India. A report published in January 2026 by the Indian Ministry of Ports, Shipping and Waterways [^ntr82-L_202602547EN.000101-E0082] flagged this restriction as the first issue that needed to be addressed for increasing the shipping of iron ore. The report called in its Table 18 to address the rake shortfall for the evacuation of iron ore from mines to Ports under Category D allotment by the Ministry of Railways. The report concluded that this constraint results in residual and uncertain rake allocation, particularly during periods of high demand from higher-priority commodities, leading to systemic delays in rake placement for iron ore evacuation to ports. The report gives as an example the Paradip Port which has a handling capacity of 18 rakes per day for iron ore, but rake supply averaged only 3,83 rakes/day in the financial year 2022–2023 and 4,09 rakes/day in the financial year 2023–2024.

- (210) The Commission had also assessed this measure in the ductile pipes investigation and had noted in recital (151) that it prioritised the domestic transport of iron ore over the transport of iron ore for export. The Commission also noted that the purpose of the policy was to promote the domestic manufacturing of steel. The Commission therefore considered that the policy constituted a measure in favour of the domestic steel industry showing its objective to lower the price of iron ore.

- (211) Following the final disclosure, the GOI claimed that the measure did not regulate prices and that allocating a lower priority to export cargoes did not demonstrate that domestic iron ore prices were distorted. The GOI added that the Commission had failed to demonstrate a causal link between the measures and the investigated producers. Furthermore, the GOI claimed that the statistics referred to by the Commission suggested a constraint limited to a specific port and which did not serve to establish a causal link with the investigated exporting producer.

- (212) The Commission considered the claim that the measure did not regulate prices directly as irrelevant. The Commission considered that a measure that prioritised the transport of domestic iron ore over exports of iron ore was an export restriction effectively aimed at favouring the domestic steel industry, including the investigated exporting producer. Furthermore, in recitals (216) to (219), the Commission quantified the resulting benefit for the investigated exporting producer of all the measures listed in recital (180). The Commission noted that objective of the reported statistics was to provide an actual example of how the measure was indeed hampering the exports of iron ore and therefore that the measure contributed to distorting iron ore prices as concluded in recital (213). Therefore, the Commission dismissed the claim.

###### 3.2.2.2.6.   Conclusion

- (213) The Commission concluded that all the above measures implemented by the GOI resulted in a stable iron ore supply at distorted prices for the benefit of domestic steel producers in India.

###### 3.2.2.3.   Existence of a Particular Market Situation affecting domestic CRF Prices

- (214) The Commission assessed whether the identified measures in Section 3.2.2.2 enacted by the GOI and which resulted in artificially low input costs of iron ore for the domestic steel producers constituted a PMS affecting the domestic prices of CRF according to Article 2(3) of the basic Regulation. For this purpose, the Commission considered how these measures had an impact on the costs of producing CRF in India.

- (215) The Commission analysed whether the domestic iron ore prices in the Indian market were indeed lower than undistorted market prices not affected by these distortions. The Commission based its analysis on the data collected from JSW given that it was the *de facto* sole exporting producer of the product under investigation and it also represented a substantial share of the domestic production and sales of CRF. The Commission checked that the JSW domestic prices paid to purchase iron ore used in the CRF production were in line with the relevant IBM published prices. As the IBM price mechanism ensured that all domestic sales prices of iron ore were substantially aligned to this price, including the purchases by JSW, the Commission focused its detailed price analysis on the JSW data.

- (216) To check the impact of the distortions brought by the GOI measures on the domestic iron ore prices, the Commission considered a benchmark that could be used to represent undistorted prices of iron ore in normal market conditions in the domestic market. The Commission carried out the assessment for JSW.

- (217) The cost of iron ore for JSW is the weighted cost of the relevant three sources for iron ore indicated in recital (179). For the reasons provided in recitals (182) to (198) the Commission considered that all the domestic prices of iron ore were affected by the said distortions and thus there existed no suitable undistorted domestic prices to compare the effect of the GOI policies as the four main sources of distortions affected all iron ore sold domestically. Therefore, the Commission had to compare the iron ore domestic prices paid by JSW and the IBM prices with a benchmark as representative and close as possible to an otherwise undistorted domestic price. Since, as explained in recitals (199) to (204), the Commission had also concluded that the captively sourced iron ore was distorted, the Commission had to compare the captively sourced iron ore costs with the same representative benchmark.

- (218) In this light, the Commission considered, as appropriate benchmark, the export price of Indian iron ore to all destinations. Given the limited exports of iron ore due to all the policies enacted by the GOI, the Commission relied on export prices of iron ore with two different groups of representative iron content and constructed the benchmark price for all other iron ore grades on the basis of the differences in domestic price of each grade. This comparison showed significantly lower domestic prices of iron ore and captively sourced iron ore costs for the corresponding grades in India. The adjusted iron ore price, comprised of the weighted averages of the prices or costs of iron from the domestic sources, was also significantly higher than the unadjusted iron ore price.

- (219) Due to confidentiality reasons, additional details concerning the share of iron ore sources in the production of CRF were disclosed specifically to JSW. Using the adjusted iron ore cost and taking into account the share of iron ore costs into the total cost of producing CRF, the Commission estimated that the cost of CRF would have been higher in the absence of the artificially low iron ore costs, and therefore that there is a particular market situation affecting the prices of the CRF in the domestic market affecting JSW. This conclusion also took into account the domestic structure of the CRF market, where there are a few dominant producers of CRF and very few imports. Therefore, the low iron ore prices must have had an immediate direct effect on lowering the domestic CRF prices for these few predominant market suppliers of CRF, confirming the existence of a particular market situation within the meaning of Article 2(3) of the basic Regulation.

- (220) Following the final disclosure, the GOI claimed that the Commission had not demonstrated why the IBM price was unreliable and why it could not be a good price benchmark. The GOI added that the Commission had not demonstrated why the differences between the IBM price and the benchmark chosen by the Commission were due to a domestic distortion as the GOI claimed that the differences could be due to differences between the domestic and export markets such as that they involved different markets, points of sales and competitive conditions. Furthermore, the GOI claimed that since the Commission did not prove that the measures listed in recital (180) had caused price distortions, it did not prove that they had a cumulative effect to distort the domestic price of iron ore. The GOI also alleged that the Commission did not prove that a concentrated market structure of CRF and low imports was caused by the said iron ore measures or that those measures resulted in lower CRF prices. For all these reasons, the GOI concluded claiming that the Commission did not have sufficient evidence to find a PMS.

- (221) The Commission noted that it had demonstrated in recitals (182) to (198) why the IBM price was distorted, and therefore unreliable, and has proposed in recital (218) an undistorted benchmark. The GOI did not explain how the alleged factors could have explained the difference between the IBM price and the benchmark. In that regard, the Commission noted that in competitive markets, that is, undistorted markets, the domestic price and the export price would tend to converge. Furthermore, the Commission noted that the GOI did not submit alternative benchmarks to the one proposed by the Commission. The Commission considered for the reasons provided in recitals (182) to (213) that the measures identified in recital (180) had distorted iron ore domestic prices and the Commission also quantified in recitals (218) to (219) the distortion by comparing it to an undistorted benchmark, noting that the actual figure was confidential but had been disclosed to the investigated exporting producer. The Commission therefore considered that it had provided sufficient evidence and arguments to justify the existence of a PMS. Therefore, the Commission dismissed the claim.

- (222) Following the final disclosure, the GOI claimed that two elements proved that the domestic iron ore market was market-based. First, that most of the production originated in non-captive, commercial mines and second, that the financial statements of NMDC and OMC showed that they were profitable. The GOI further claimed that the Commission had not found in a previous investigation regarding HRF any iron ore price distortion that would lead to establish a PMS and therefore the GOI claimed that it was not possible to establish a PMS for CRF, which was a downstream product of HRF. Finally, the GOI claimed that the Commission should have used the actual iron ore procurement costs incurred by JSW to analyse iron ore prices.

- (223) The Commission had set out in recitals (182) to (204) how NMDC and OMC were price setters of the IBM price. The Commission did not consider that the two elements claimed by the GOI were relevant to disproof the distortion. On the first element, the Commission had already referred in recital (182) to the market shares. On the second element, the Commission considered that being profitable does not prevent the ability to be a price setter. Furthermore, the Commission noted that the findings in this investigation stemmed from the substantial data and information gathered for the purpose of this investigation and did not consider relevant comparing the findings from other investigations. Finally, the Commission had already explained and quantified in recitals (218) to (219) the distortion in domestic iron prices and explained in recitals (233) to (234) why it had to adjust the normal value. Therefore, the Commission dismissed the claim.

###### 3.2.2.3.1.   Conclusion

- (224) In view of the above, the Commission concluded that a Particular Market Situation affecting domestic CRF Prices existed in India.

###### 3.2.2.4.   Proper comparison

- (225) The Commission assessed if a proper comparison within the meaning of Article 2(3) of the basic Regulation could be made between the normal value and the export price of JSW because of the existence of the particular market situation. To that end, the Commission assessed the relative effect of the PMS on domestic and export sales.

- (226) The Commission found that the conditions of competition in India and in the Union were significantly different.

- (227) As indicated in Table 1, the Indian CRF market, with an estimated consumption of 21 178 934 tonnes, was dominated by Indian producers with imports amounting to only around 2 %. This low level of imports had been observed for at least three years according to the information on file. The Commission therefore noted that the Indian CRF market is characterised by an insignificant level of imports. In this market, all the Indian producers benefit from the existence of the particular market situation, artificially lowering their costs. On this basis, the Commission concluded that there is a significant impact of the particular market situation on the domestic prices of CRF.

- (228) By contrast, the Union CRF market is characterised by a mix of Union producers and imports from several countries as indicated in sections 4.1, 4.4 and 5.2. Therefore, the potential impact of the existence of the particular market situation on the export price to the Union would in any event be significantly attenuated by the existence of a series of other factors leading to the formation of such export prices. These factors include *inter alia* competition from Union producers which do not benefit from a similar particular market situation on the supply of iron ore, the fact that there is only one Indian exporting producer rather than more Indian companies on the EU market or the existence of all other exporting producers from the investigated countries as well as from other countries.

- (229) On the basis of all of the above elements, the Commission concluded that the particular market situation affected differently the domestic prices as compared to the export prices. Therefore, the PMS did not allow for a proper comparison between the domestic Indian prices and the export prices according to Article 2(3) of the basic Regulation.

- (230) Following the final disclosure, JSW and the GOI claimed that the Commission had failed to demonstrate that the alleged PMS had affected the domestic and export prices differently and therefore, that it would not allow a proper comparison. JSW claimed that it had the same cost of production regardless of whether the product concerned had been sold in the domestic market or had been exported. Furthermore, JSW and the GOI claimed that the Commission had not explained sufficiently how the factors listed in recitals (227) and (228) had interplayed with the PMS to render the comparison of the domestic and export prices impossible. The GOI also claimed that the Commission’s finding was based on an ‘unevidenced’ prediction that export prices would be depressed rather than on a grounded finding and that the Commission had not identified the magnitude of the alleged differential effect. The GOI further claimed that if a generic difference in the market structure was sufficient, the second limb of Article 2(3) of the basic Regulation would be satisfied automatically, which the GOI did not consider as sufficient. Furthermore, JSW claimed that the Commission’s reasoning was contradictory since less competition on the Indian market should have led to higher prices and more competition in the EU market should have led to lower prices regardless of how costs were structured. JSW added that this finding would be in line with JSW dumping margin without a PMS adjustment.

- (231) The Commission disagreed that it had failed to demonstrate that the PMS had affected differently the domestic and export prices. The Commission noted that regardless of whether the costs of manufacturing were the same for products sold domestically or for export, the Commission had reasoned in recitals (227) to (229) how the PMS found in India did not permit a proper comparison due the different relative prevailing conditions of competition in India and the EU. In that regard, the panel report in DS529 considered that the conditions of competition were a factor that affected domestic and import prices differently [^ntr83-L_202602547EN.000101-E0083].

- (232) Concretely, while in India price formation was affected by the PMS found because it benefitted domestic producers and there is almost no competition from foreign producers, in the Union the PMS had a very different impact (if at all) due to the fact that the prevailing conditions of competition, and consequently price formation, are the result of supply by producers from many countries that did not benefit from such measures creating a PMS. Such conclusions were grounded on the factual circumstances established in this investigation as explained in recitals (227) and (228) and JSW did not provide any evidence to the contrary. The Commission considered sufficient, under Article 2(3) of the basic Regulation to establish that the PMS found had an impact on the price formation of domestic sales, whereas given the different prevailing market conditions and hence formation of prices in the EU it resulted in a different impact (if at all) that did not allow domestic and export prices to be properly compared. Furthermore, the Commission did not consider relevant JSW’s claim on the relation of the degree of competition with the level of prices in the context of the comparison between domestic prices and EU prices, because it only referred to prices within India on the one hand and to prices within the EU on the other hand. Therefore, the Commission confirmed the findings and conclusions set out in recitals (225) to (229).

##### 3.2.3.   *Normal value*

- (233) As set out in recitals (179) to (229) regarding the existence of a particular market situation in the CRF market in India, the Commission noted that all domestic sales of JSW were affected because the set of measures implemented by the GOI resulted in an artificially low cost of iron ore necessary for all the production of CRF. As a consequence, the Commission constructed the normal value on the basis of the cost of production in the country of origin plus a reasonable amount for selling, general and administrative costs and for profits in accordance with Article 2(3) of the basic Regulation.

- (234) Pursuant to Article 2(5) of the basic Regulation, all the costs, as recorded by JSW, were used in the construction of normal value, with the exception of iron ore sourced domestically. For these iron ore costs, the Commission relied on the representative undistorted iron ore prices as described in recitals (216) to (219). With regard to SG & A expenses, the Commission used the overall verified SG & A expenses as reported by JSW on domestic sales for the product concerned. As for profits, the Commission used a reasonable profit margin based on the sales by JSW of the different CRF product types on its domestic market.

- (235) Following the final disclosure, JSW claimed that WTO Panels and the Appellate Body had found that Article 2.2.1.1 of the WTO Agreement did not allow cost adjustments such as the one made by the Commission. Furthermore, JSW disagreed with the method used by the Commission to establish a reasonable profit margin. In particular, JSW claimed that, since the Commission had no evidence that JSW had the ability to reflect the cost increase in its domestic prices, it should have calculated the profit on the basis of the sales that would be in the ordinary cost of trade using the increased cost of manufacturing resulting from the PMS in line with previous investigations where the Commission disregarded costs of production on the basis of Article 2(5) of the basic Regulation. Finally, JSW claimed that in quantifying the PMS, the Commission should not have introduced an export tax correction since it had led to a constructed price inconsistent with the international market price.

- (236) The Commission noted that JSW failed to explain in what way the methodology used by the Commission was not in line with WTO law. The interpretation that seems to be suggested by JSW would deprive Article 2(3) of the Basic Regulation, and Article 2.2 of the WTO ADA of any meaning. The Commission recalls that in DS529 [^ntr84-L_202602547EN.000101-E0084], the panel expressly recognized that a particular market situation can derive from low-priced inputs. Therefore, Article 2(5) of the basic Regulation (and 2.2.1.1 of the WTO ADA) cannot be read as depriving an investigating authority from any remedy to address such cost-based particular market situation. It is self-evident that in cases of cost-based PMS, the cost affected by the PMS needs to be replaced. In this regard, the Commission also recalls that in the panel report in DS529 [^ntr85-L_202602547EN.000101-E0085], mentioned by JSW, the panel clearly stated that there can be ‘compelling reasons’ other than those specifically mentioned under Article 2.2.1.1 WTO ADA that allow an investigating authority not to use the cost in the records kept by the exporting producer. The existence of a cost-based PMS clearly represents such a ‘compelling reason’.

- (237) On the method to obtain a reasonable profit following Article 2(3) of the basic Regulation because of the existence of a PMS, the Commission constructed the normal value as indicated in recital (233), on the basis of the cost of production in the country of origin plus a reasonable amount for selling, general and administrative costs and for profits. The Commission considered that basing that profit on all transactions made in the ordinary course of trade using the data submitted by JSW by product type resulted in a reasonable profit meeting all the requisites of Article 2(6). JSW did not provide any evidence why such method would not be reasonable as required by Article 2(6)(c) of the basic Regulation. Indeed, given that the measures analysed in the investigation benefitted all the domestic CRF industry in India as concluded in recital (213) and the domestic structure of the Indian CRF market, where there are a few dominant producers of CRF and very few imports, the method used by the Commission adequately resulted in a profit that did not exceed the profit normally realised by other exporters or producers on sales of products of the same general category in the domestic market of the country of origin.

- (238) As regards the adjustment due to the export tax, the Commission noted that it had only applied the export tax to iron ore grades above 58 %. The Commission also noted that JSW did not contest that those grades were indeed subject to such export tax. Therefore, even if there was a difference between the Indian iron ore of grades above 58 % and the iron ore originating from other countries, this must have been caused by reasons other than the export tax.

##### 3.2.4.   *Export price*

- (239) The sampled exporting producer exported to the Union directly to independent customers. Therefore, the export price was the price actually paid or payable for the product concerned when sold for export to the Union, in accordance with Article 2(8) of the basic Regulation.

##### 3.2.5.   *Comparison*

- (240) Article 2(10) of the basic Regulation requires the Commission to make a fair comparison between the normal value and the export price at the same level of trade and to make allowances for differences in factors which affect prices and price comparability. In the case at hand the Commission chose to compare the normal value and the export price of the sampled exporting producers at the *ex-works* level of trade. As further explained below, where appropriate, the normal value and the export price were adjusted in order to: (i) net them back to the *ex-works* level; and (ii) make allowances for differences in factors which were claimed, and demonstrated, to affect prices and price comparability.

###### 3.2.5.1.   Adjustments made to the normal value

- (241) As explained in recital (234), the normal value was established at the ex-works level of trade by using costs of production together with amounts for SG & A expenses and for profit, which were considered to be reasonable for that level of trade. Therefore, no adjustments were necessary to net the normal value back to the ex-works level. The Commission found no reasons for making any allowances to the normal value, nor were such allowances claimed by any of the sampled exporting producers.

###### 3.2.5.2.   Adjustments made to the export price

- (242) In order to net the export price back to the ex-works level of trade, adjustments were made on the account of: customs duty, other import charges, freight, insurance, handling loading and ancillary expenses.

- (243) Allowances were made for the following factors affecting prices and price comparability: packing, bank charges, commissions and credit costs.

##### 3.2.6.   *Dumping margins*

- (244) For the sampled cooperating exporting producer, the Commission compared the weighted average normal value of each type of the like product with the weighted average export price of the corresponding type of the product concerned, in accordance with Article 2(11) and (12) of the basic Regulation. On this basis, the definitive dumping margin expressed as a percentage of the CIF Union frontier price, duty unpaid, is 9,5 %.

- (245) Following the final disclosure, JSW claimed that the CIF price for sales carried out through unrelated traders should be increased to account for the profit and SG & A costs of those companies. JSW added that the Commission has followed that approach in previous investigations.

- (246) The Commission noted that JSW did not submit any evidence regarding the CIF Union frontier price applicable for transactions involving unrelated traders. Furthermore, the Commission considered that each investigation is case specific and that the findings and conclusions reached in this investigation were based on the actual data and information collected and verified. Therefore, the Commission dismissed the claim.

- (247) For the cooperating exporting producers outside the sample, the Commission calculated the weighted average dumping margin, in accordance with Article 9(6) of the basic Regulation. Therefore, that margin was established on the basis of the margin of the sampled exporting producer. On this basis, the definitive dumping margin of the cooperating exporting producers outside the sample is 9,5 %.

- (248) For all other exporting producers in India, the Commission established the dumping margin on the basis of the facts available, in accordance with Article 18 of the basic Regulation. To this end, the Commission determined the level of cooperation of the exporting producers. The level of cooperation is the volume of exports of the cooperating exporting producers to the Union expressed as proportion of the total imports from the country concerned to the Union in the IP, that were established on the basis of Eurostat.

- (249) The level of cooperation in this case is high because the exports of the cooperating exporting producers constituted around 100 % of the total imports during the IP. On this basis, the Commission decided to establish the dumping margin for non-cooperating exporting producers at the level of the cooperating sampled company.

- (250) The definitive dumping margins, expressed as a percentage of the CIF Union frontier price, duty unpaid, are as follows:

| Company | Definitive dumping margin (%) |
| --- | --- |
| — JSW Steel Limited — JSW Steel Coated Products Limited | 9,5 |
| Other cooperating companies | 9,5 |
| All other imports originating in country concerned | 9,5 |

#### 3.3.   **Japan**

##### 3.3.1.   *Related companies and arm’s length*

- (251) After the sampling decision, since the Commission had deemed them to be related in the *HRF* investigation [^ntr86-L_202602547EN.000101-E0086], Nippon Steel claimed for the purposes of this investigation that it was not related to the trader Marubeni-Itochu Steel Inc. (‘MISI’), a company involved both in domestic and in export sales of the product concerned manufactured by Nippon Steel. In this respect, Nippon Steel recalled the criteria to identify related parties included in Article 127(1) of Commission Implementing Regulation (EU) 2015/2447 [^ntr87-L_202602547EN.000101-E0087] (‘UCC Implementing Act’) and provided details on the corporate links between the two companies. Based on this, Nippon Steel claimed that the simple fact that Nippon Steel held shares in a third entity in parallel to MISI was insufficient to conclude that MISI was related to Nippon Steel. In support, Nippon Steel recalled that in the anti-subsidy investigation on new battery electric vehicles for passengers originating in the PRC [^ntr88-L_202602547EN.000101-E0088], the Commission did not treat the SAIC Group and China FAW Corporation Limited as being related despite the fact that they had joint ventures with Volkswagen, as it would be clear from the annex to the definitive regulation in that investigation [^ntr89-L_202602547EN.000101-E0089], and, similarly, the Commission did not treat as related the producers who had joint ventures with battery producer CATL.

- (252) The Commission, based on the details provided, considered that there was a relationship between Nippon Steel and MISI, based on a joint ownership of a third entity that the companies controlled together pursuant to Article 127(1)(g) of the UCC Implementing Act and which established their legally recognised business partnership pursuant to Article 127(1)(b) of the UCC Implementing Act. In this respect, at the outset the Commission noted that Nippon Steel itself, in the anti-dumping investigation on certain hot-rolled flat steel products from Japan, declared on its own motion MISI as a related entity precisely because of the fact that Nippon Steel and MISI together held almost 100 % of the shares of this third entity [^ntr90-L_202602547EN.000101-E0090]. In any case, based on the documents provided by Nippon Steel in the current investigation, the situation was the same during the investigation period, when Nippon Steel and MISI had the powers to appoint almost all directors, including the president. The Commission further noted in respect of its findings in the anti-subsidy investigation on battery electric vehicles from the PRC, that it is evident from the operative part and the annex to Implementing Regulation (EU) 2024/2754 that SAIC Group and China FAW Corporation Limited had different joint ventures with Volkswagen. On the contrary, the situation in the present case is the joint ownership of the same third entity, that MISI and Nippon Steel control together. Besides, the Commission considered a producer as related to CATL [^ntr91-L_202602547EN.000101-E0091]. Regardless, any determination of a relationship between parties should be based on the facts and circumstances of each case. The case on battery electric vehicles from the PRC concerned an anti-subsidy investigation, with specific facts and circumstances that were taken into account. The current investigation, on the other hand, is an anti-dumping investigation concerning a different country, economic reality, product/sector, and different facts and circumstances. Therefore, the Commission dismissed this claim.

- (253) Furthermore, Nippon Steel claimed that it was not MISI’s legally recognised partner in business due to the shareholding in a third entity, because the Court of Justice in *Baltic Master* [^ntr92-L_202602547EN.000101-E0092] required for this qualification that the national provisions concerning recognised partners were satisfied, and MISI and Nippon Steel did not enter such agreement (so-called *kumiai*) under Japanese law.

- (254) However, the Commission noted that Nippon Steel and MISI together jointly directly owned almost 100 % of the shares of a third entity. Regardless of the legal definition of related companies in Japan under Japanese law, under the referred Article 127 of the UCC Implementing Act, the provision normally applied for establishing relationship in trade defence investigations in the Union, those parties are considered legally recognised partners in business under Article 127(1)(b) of the UCC Implementing Act as joint owners of a third business under Japanese law and therefore related parties. Therefore, the Commission dismissed this claim.

- (255) In addition, Nippon Steel claimed that it was not in a position to exercise constraint or direction over MISI, because the size of the joint entities was minor as compared to Nippon Steel and MISI.

- (256) The Commission noted that Article 127 of the UCC Implementing Act does not mention any requirement in relation to the relative size of the controlled third entity compared to the controlling entities. Thus, even if the third entity is smaller in terms of revenues and personnel, the relationship can still be established pursuant to Article 127(1)(g) of the UCC Implementing Act. Therefore, the Commission dismissed this claim.

- (257) In any event, Nippon Steel claimed that, should the Commission decide to maintain its position that MISI is a related party of Nippon Steel, it should acknowledge that the sales between Nippon Steel and MISI take place on arm’s length terms and should treat sales to these entities in a manner akin to Nippon Steel’s sales to unrelated parties. In support, Nippon Steel brought sensitive elements concerning the framework agreements between Nippon Steel and its traders, the sales process and MISI’s purchases of the product concerned.

- (258) At the outset, the Commission acknowledged that the framework agreements with related entities that were provided to the Commission were similar in terms of content and conditions. However, none of the framework agreements specified how the final prices were set, how commissions were paid or other details relevant for the determination of arm’s length transactions.

- (259) Moreover, by the company’s own assertions in its submission and based on the verifications, the price negotiations for sales to final customers involving related traders in practice took place either through the trader or directly with the customer, meaning the trader’s influence in these negotiations was limited to acting as an intermediary. The final price was therefore mainly determined either by the final customer’s acceptance of Nippon Steel’s offer, or by Nippon Steel’s acceptance of the final customer’s counteroffer. In view of the limited (if any) influence of the related trader on the price setting, the price between related entities was considered unreliable.

- (260) In addition, the Commission did not dispute that MISI was free to sell and buy from other unrelated producers. However, the Commission noted that a comparison of the unit prices by supplier of the product under investigation to MISI during the investigation period, provided by MISI itself in its questionnaire reply, revealed that Nippon Steel’s unit price was indeed the lowest compared to other Japanese producers.

- (261) Finally, in any case, the Commission compared, on a product type basis, the price at FOB level of export sales destined to the Union between Nippon Steel and MISI, and between Nippon Steel and its other related export trader Nippon Steel Trading Corporation (‘NST’). Such comparison revealed that the price between Nippon Steel and MISI, and the price between Nippon Steel and NST, were aligned (1,5 % of difference). Thus, MISI benefitted from the same prices made to the related trader NST, whose relationship with Nippon Steel was not contested.

- (262) Therefore, the Commission rejected the claim that Nippon Steel’s sales to MISI were at arm’s length.

- (263) In conclusion, Nippon Steel stated that, in order to support the claims summarised above in this section, MISI would separately respond to the relevant parts of the questionnaire. The Commission received the questionnaire reply of MISI and of its related importer in the Union Marubeni-Itochu Steel Europe GmbH (‘MISEA’).

- (264) In its comments on the final disclosure, Nippon Steel again disagreed with the Commission’s conclusion that it was related to MISI. However, it did not bring any new arguments or evidence. The Commission consequently rejected the claim.

- (265) The GOJ submitted that, although it was true that Nippon Steel and MISI jointly owned shares in a third entity unrelated to the transactions at issue in this investigation, Nippon Steel had no direct or indirect control over MISI. Thus, according to the GOJ, holding the minority shares in a third entity not involved in the relevant export transactions could not be a sufficient ground to conclude that Nippon Steel and MISI are in association within the meaning of Article 2.3 of the WTO ADA.

- (266) The Commission acknowledged that Nippon Steel did not have direct control over MISI, as their relationship was established due to the joint ownership of a third entity. However, such relationship gave rise to association within the meaning of Article 2.3 of the WTO ADA, insofar as MISI exported to the Union only the product under investigation manufactured by Nippon Steel, and not the product under investigation purchased from, and manufactured by, other Japanese producers. The investigation findings showed that such commercial behaviour did not derive from an agreement between Nippon Steel and MISI, but rather from a voluntary commitment by MISI. However, the Commission considered that such commitment, despite voluntary, undoubtedly bound the company’s freedom on price setting and strategy. Indeed, MISI was dependent on a single supplier for its Union sales of the product under investigation. These constraints result in a lack of independent action on the part of MISI. Besides this, the verification showed that MISI shared with Nippon Steel information about possible customers in need of Nippon Steel’s products and about markets in which Nippon Steel could be willing to expand. Thus, MISI provided to Nippon Steel a service wider than the usual purchase and resale activity of a trader. Thus, the Commission rejected the claim that the relationship between Nippon Steel and MISI did not result in association.

- (267) Moreover, the GOJ claimed that the Commission did not show that the relationship between Nippon Steel and MISI rendered the export transactions unreliable for the purposes of calculating the dumping margins, given that the third entity establishing the relationship was not involved in the export transactions and the role of MISI in price negotiations was limited.

- (268) The Commission considered that the fact that the third entity establishing the relationship between MISI and Nippon Steel was not involved in the export transactions to the Union was not relevant. Indeed, a relationship can be very well established through a company not involved in the export of the product under investigation to the Union. Moreover, the third entity establishing the relationship was not involved in the export transactions to the Union but based on its business report, was anyway processing and selling steel in Japan. The Commission also considered that MISI’s limited role in price negotiations may be an indication of the degree of control that Nippon Steel retained on the final price. Therefore, the Commission rejected the claim.
