- (415) On the basis of all the above elements, the Commission concluded that the particular market situation affected differently the domestic prices in Taiwan as compared to the export prices to the Union. Therefore, the Commission concluded that the PMS did not allow for a proper comparison between the domestic prices in Taiwan and the export prices to the Union according to Article 2(3) of the basic Regulation.

- (416) Following the final disclosure, the CSC Group claimed that the particular market situation at issue did not impede a proper comparison between domestic and export sales. In particular, CSC Group claimed that the Commission’s analysis of proper comparison was incomplete and did not take into account the fact that steel overcapacity was a global issue, whose effects are experienced also in the Union. In this respect, CSC Group alleged that in this Regulation the Commission found that the oversupply of Chinese steel in international markets impacted steel producers globally, including both Taiwan and the Union [^ntr113-L_202602547EN.000101-E0113]. CSC Group referred also to Regulation (EU) 2026/1384. Similarly, the authorities of Taiwan also referred to Regulation (EU) 2026/1384 and to its preparatory documents. The authorities of Taiwan also claimed that the OECD-GFSEC report demonstrated that the push-out effect of Chinese overcapacity had an equal impact on the export performance of third countries, as well as on the Union industry.

- (417) The Commission noted that the particular market situation at issue was the push-out effect of Chinese overcapacity in Taiwan’s market, and not global overcapacity. CSC Group relied on a part of this Regulation [^ntr114-L_202602547EN.000101-E0114] devoted to global overcapacity as a possible other factor causing injury to the Union industry, at recitals (961) to (963), and not to the particular market situation and its analysis. The Commission further noted that the phenomenon at issue in Regulation (EU) 2026/1384 was global overcapacity, rather than the push-out effect of Chinese overcapacity. However, precisely in the excerpt cited by CSC Group there is a reference to ‘imports from countries with excess capacity, or […] the push-out effect’ of the imports from those countries. This investigation specifically analysed the push-out effect of Chinese imports affected by overcapacity and found it to constitute a particular market situation in Taiwan. In the other countries concerned, instead, the Commission did not find it to constitute a particular market situation. As shown at recitals (413) and (414), such particular market situation had an effect in Taiwan’s domestic market different from the effect on the Union market. Therefore, it impeded a proper comparison. This is a different exercise from the comparison of the effects on the export performance of Taiwan’s industry and on the Union industry. In any case, the Commission showed at recital (413) that the conditions in the Union market and Taiwan’s domestic market were different. Thus, the Commission rejected this claim.

- (418) The CSC Group also claimed that the Commission, in its analysis, did not consider the indirect effects of overcapacity on the Union market, such as whether the countries concerned lost market share in third countries and re-directed their exports to the Union, or the increase of the market share of other third countries (specifically Korea and Ukraine) in the Union obtained by redirecting to the Union domestic sales or export destined to other third countries.

- (419) The Commission noted that CSC group did not provide any evidence of whether such imports into the Union were the result of overcapacity. Moreover, the reason behind such imports would not change the Commission’s analysis and conclusions in recitals (410) to (415), that were based on the situation in the Union market as established with the verified data gathered in the investigation, that took into account all imports into the Union.

- (420) In any event, CSC Group compared the average unit import price into the Union from non-concerned countries with the average unit import price into Taiwan from China. CSC Group claimed that the two declined similarly, showing the import prices into the Union were impacted by the global overcapacity.

- (421) The Commission noted that CSC Group compared import prices into the Union with import prices into Taiwan, so towards different destinations. Moreover, import prices of non-concerned countries into the Union include Chinese imports into the Union, which are affected by overcapacity. Furthermore, CSC Group compared the percentage decrease of prices, rather than their actual level. Finally, the Commission analysed the push-out effect of Chinese overcapacity only in the countries concerned, no allegations were brought with respect to other third countries. Therefore, this claim was rejected.

- (422) Additionally, CSC Group compared the average unit import price into the Union from non-concerned countries with domestic prices in Taiwan, and claimed that the import price into the Union from non-concerned countries declined more steeply than the domestic price in Taiwan. According to the CSC Group, this would show that overcapacity would negatively impact both domestic and export prices, so that the Commission should adjust profitability on both the domestic and the export side.

- (423) The Commission noted that CSC Group compared import prices into the Union from other countries with domestic prices in Taiwan, so prices both originating from different sources and towards different destinations. Furthermore, CSC Group compared the percentage decrease of prices, rather than their actual level. Moreover, CSC Group assumed that imports into the Union from other countries were affected by overcapacity only because declined more steeply than domestic prices in Taiwan. However, import from non-concerned countries into the Union include Chinese imports and, as shown in Table 11 and at recitals (412) and (413), Chinese imports and imports from non-concerned countries had very different relevance and market share in the Union and in Taiwan’s market. Thus, the Commission rejected this claim.

- (424) The authorities of Taiwan claimed that, to establish that a proper comparison was prevented, the Commission should have assessed the relative effect of the particular market situation on Taiwan’s domestic and export prices, pursuant to *Australia – Anti-Dumping Measures on Paper* [^ntr115-L_202602547EN.000101-E0115]. The authorities of Taiwan’s view was that a particular market situation consisting in the push-out effect of Chinese overcapacity affected by its own nature export prices in a comparable way to domestic prices and necessarily had the same effects on both. In support, the authorities of Taiwan indicated that the Commission’s findings showed a considerable decrease in both Taiwan’s domestic price and export price. In this respect, the CSC Group claimed that, as the profitability of domestic sales to unrelated customers, also the profitability of export sales to unrelated customers declined in a similar way since 2022. According to the CSC Group, this would show that overcapacity would negatively impact both domestic and export prices, so that the Commission should adjust profitability on both the domestic and the export side.

- (425) The Commission noted that the decline of domestic and export prices derived from different reasons. Indeed, as shown in Table 12, domestic prices in Taiwan followed (without being able to match them) the steeper decline of lower Chinese import prices into Taiwan. The lower price and steeper decline allowed Chinese imports to rapidly increase their market share in Taiwan from 2022 to the investigation period, as shown in Table 11, to the detriment of the Taiwan industry’s domestic sales. If Taiwan’s domestic industry had not followed Chinese import prices in the downward trend, it is likely that the domestic industry would have lost further market shares. So, Taiwan’s domestic industry was forced to lower its prices by the competitive pressure (i.e., the push-out effect) of Chinese imports affected by overcapacity. This constitutes the particular market situation affecting Taiwan’s domestic market that renders domestic profits unreliable. On the contrary, when exporting the displaced volumes to the Union, Taiwan’s exporters were not subject to the same competitive constraints of their own domestic market, given that the conditions of competition were different in the Union market as shown in recitals (410) to (414), where Chinese imports had no impact. Therefore, the Commission rejected the claim that the push-out effect of Chinese overcapacity affected Taiwan’s export prices in a comparable or similar way as Taiwan’s domestic prices and consequently did not adjust profitability on the export side.

- (426) Moreover, the authorities of Taiwan claimed that it was clear that the particular market situation affected also the conditions of competition in the Union from the fact that, despite Chinese imports into the Union having a low market share, the Union sought to offset the effects of steel overcapacity with an act of general policy such as Regulation (EU) 2026/1384. Thus, according to the authorities of Taiwan, the particular market situation should not be evaluated solely on the basis of the market share held by Chinese imports into the Union.

- (427) The Commission noted that Regulation (EU) 2026/1384 was meant to tackle global overcapacity, whereas this investigation was based on the allegation of a particular market situation consisting in the push-out of Chinese overcapacity in, *inter alia*, Taiwan. Thus, in the context of the proper comparison analysis, the Commission was not required to analyse the effects of global overcapacity in the Union market but, at most, the effects of the particular market situation in the Union market. Moreover, Regulation (EU) 2026/1384 does not change the facts that: (i) as noted in recitals (388) and (409), there was a particular market situation in Taiwan during the investigation period; and (ii) as noted in recital (415), that particular market situation affected the proper comparison between the normal value and the export price. This had to be addressed. Therefore, the Commission rejected this claim.

- (428) The authorities of Taiwan claimed that a number of public statements of the complainant [^ntr116-L_202602547EN.000101-E0116] outlined the effects of global steel overcapacity over export prices.

- (429) The Commission noted that the investigation analysed only the push-out effect of Chinese overcapacity, and not global overcapacity. Therefore, the Commission rejected this claim.

- (430) The CSC Group claimed that, even if the Commission concluded that global steel overcapacity had a greater impact on Taiwan’s domestic market than on the Union export market, it could not determine that there was no impact on the Union market. On the contrary, it should have assessed the relative impact on domestic sales versus export sales and adjusted also the profits on export sales by 4,8 %.

- (431) The Commission noted that the particular market situation found in Taiwan’s domestic market was not global steel overcapacity, but the push-out effect of Chinese overcapacity. As explained in recitals (410) to (414), the conditions of competition in the Union market are different. As a consequence, no adjustment to the export price because of the particular market situation was warranted. Therefore, the Commission rejected this claim and confirmed its conclusions with regard to proper comparison in recital (415).

- (432) Finally, the authorities of Taiwan claimed that the Commission, in its proper comparison analysis, failed to consider the differences in grades between Chinese imports into Taiwan and the average unit price of various product mixes, among other factors which could influence the difference in the conditions of competition between the Union and Taiwan.

- (433) The Commission noted that it already addressed the authorities of Taiwan’s concerns as regards the alleged differences in grades between Chinese imports into Taiwan at recital (405). As regards the average unit price of various product mixes, the Commission recalled that, in the context of the dumping, undercutting and underselling calculations, the comparison of domestic and export sales, as well as the comparison of the imports from the countries concerned into the Union with the domestic sales of the Union industry, took place per PCN and showed sufficient matching. Therefore, in the context of the proper comparison analysis, the Commission did not need to further analyse the prices in the Union or of imports into the Union at product type level or to consider the precise product type to which the prices corresponded. As regards the Chinese import price into Taiwan, the Commission did not need to analyse the prices per product type or to consider the product types imported, insofar the product under investigation imported into Taiwan from the PRC competed against Taiwan’s domestic production, as indicated in recital (405). Therefore, this claim was rejected.

- (434) CSC reiterated the claims set out in recitals (416), (420), (422), (424) and (430), and addressed in recitals (417), (421), (423), (425) and (431), in its comments on the additional final disclosure, despite the fact that these matters were not subject to the additional final disclosure and the company was instructed to only comment on the issues covered by the additional final disclosure.

- (435) In conclusion, the Commission confirmed its conclusion at recital (415).

##### 3.4.3.   *Normal value*

- (436) As set out in recitals (388) and (415), the Commission established the existence of a particular market situation in the domestic market of Taiwan, which did not allow for a proper comparison. All domestic sales of CSC Group were affected because Chinese imports resulting from the overcapacity in the steel sector in the PRC artificially decreased the price of all domestic sales of CRF. Therefore, such sales could not be considered as made under normal conditions and the profit generated could not constitute a normal profit.

- (437) As a consequence, in accordance with Article 2(3) of the basic Regulation, the Commission constructed the normal value on the basis of the cost of production in the country of origin plus a reasonable amount for SG & A costs and for profits.

- (438) As explained in recitals (384) to (387), the effect of the PMS in Taiwan resulted in artificially low profitability but did not affect the costs of production and the SG & A costs in Taiwan. Therefore, for the construction of the normal value, the Commission relied on the actual costs of production and the actual SG & A costs recorded by CSC and CHS.

- (439) Concerning profit, the Commission considered that the profit realised by the exporting producer on sales of like products on the domestic market was unsuitable for use due to the effect of the PMS on the actual profit margin, as explained in recital (388).

- (440) The Commission determined the impact of the PMS on the actual profit margin based on a report prepared by the OECD and published by GFSEC [^ntr117-L_202602547EN.000101-E0117] (‘OECD-GFSEC report’). According to this report, the average of the difference between profit actually achieved by steel companies across the world, excluding the PRC, and the profit which could have been achieved by the same companies had the PRC’s overcapacity not surged the way it did, was 4,8 %.

*Table 15*

**Actual versus estimated profitability (%)**

|  |  |  |  |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
|  | 2015 | 2017 | 2020 |
| Actual profit | 9 | 17,75 | 16,5 |
| Estimated profit | 14 | 22,7 | 21 |
| Average of the difference | 4,8 | 4,8 | 4,8 |
| *Source:* OECD-GFSEC report, pp. 18-19, Box 3 and Figure 3, | *Source:* OECD-GFSEC report, pp. 18-19, Box 3 and Figure 3, | *Source:* OECD-GFSEC report, pp. 18-19, Box 3 and Figure 3, | *Source:* OECD-GFSEC report, pp. 18-19, Box 3 and Figure 3, |

- (441) The Commission considered that any meaningful profit adjustment per product type should take into account the specific variations in costs of production, domestic sales prices and resulting profit levels across those product types since this information is available to the Commission.

- (442) Therefore, to establish a reasonable amount for profit within the meaning of Article 2(3) of the basic Regulation, and in line with Article 2(6)(c) thereof, the Commission, as a starting point and following its standard dumping calculation methodology, first identified all domestic sales transactions made in the ordinary course of trade for each product type. Since the profitability of those transactions was negatively affected by the PMS found, relying on their actual profitability would not result in a reasonable amount for profit within the meaning of Article 2(3). To address this, the Commission adjusted the profitability of the transactions made in the ordinary course of trade by the average loss of profit attributable to the PMS.

- (443) Following the final disclosure, the authorities of Taiwan claimed that the construction of the normal value allowed by Article 2.2.2 of the WTO ADA and Articles 2(3) and 2(6) of the basic Regulation was already sufficient to ensure that the push-out effect was accounted for. According to them, there was no legal basis to depart from the actual profit based on domestic sales in the ordinary course of trade in favour of a cross-country benchmark. This was especially the case because, in their view, profitable domestic transactions were predominantly transactions in high-quality, annealed CRF coils, which did not compete with, and were not affected by, the non-annealed CRF coils that constituted the vast majority of Chinese CRF imports into Taiwan.

- (444) The Commission explained at recitals (384) to (387) the effects of the PMS on the domestic profitability in Taiwan. Due to the PMS, the profit could not be considered reasonable. For this reason, the Commission relied on an alternative benchmark, still pursuant to Articles 2(3) and 2(6) of the basic Regulation. In any case, the Commission disagreed with the authorities of Taiwan’s view on the actual profit realised by Taiwan’s exporting producers. While high-quality, annealed CRF may be more profitable compared to non-annealed CRF, domestic profits were not due only to high-quality, annealed CRF. Indeed, the Commission noted that the domestic sales of non-annealed product types of the largest producer in the sampled group, CSC, during the investigation period were all profitable for 11 of 12 non-annealed product types, and largely profitable for the remaining product type. All these domestic profitable sales were considered in the ordinary course of trade and thus included in the dumping calculation. Therefore, the Commission used also profits of non-annealed product types, that the authorities of Taiwan assert to be the product types most exposed to the competition of Chinese imports. Due to the push-out of Chinese imports, the level of such profit was still lower than what would have been in the absence of the PMS. For this reason, the Commission needed to resort to an adjustment to offset the effects of the PMS and reach a reasonable profit. In any case, with specific regard to annealed and non-annealed CRF, as explained in recital (405), annealed and non-annealed CRF were substitutable as raw materials used to produce downstream products, and thus were in competition. Only, non-annealed CRF were cheaper, and thus preferred by downstream users, compared to annealed CRF. Finally, Chinese imports during the investigation period included also annealed CRF. Therefore, the Commission rejected this claim.

- (445) The authorities of Taiwan claimed that the profit adjustment was incompatible with Article 2.2.2 of the WTO ADA. Indeed, they argued that, when the actual amount for profits cannot be based on actual data pertaining to production and sales in the ordinary course of trade by the exporting producer concerned, the Commission should rely for any other reasonable methods on amounts not exceeding the profits normally realised by exporting producers in the domestic market of the country concerned. The OECD-GFSEC report relied on by the Commission would be unsuitable, according to the authorities of Taiwan, for several reasons. First, the report did not specifically indicate a reasonable profit applicable to the CRF market in Taiwan in the investigation period. Second, there were methodological notes inviting a cautious interpretation, the study was not peer-reviewed, was funded also through the contribution of the Union, and appeared not to rely on financial statements and annual reports for the profitability analysis. Third, the profit margin taken into account was the overall profit margin of steel producers, not specific as the actual profit margin of Taiwan’s producer. Finally, the authorities of Taiwan claimed that they had already objected to this methodology, proposed by the complainant, in their comments on initiation, summarised at recital (21). In respect of the Commission’s rebuttal in recital (22), the authorities of Taiwan claimed that the Commission did not make reference to its obligations under Article 2.2.2 of the WTO ADA and did not prove that the report included actual data on profits from Taiwan’s producers.

- (446) At the outset, the Commission noted that, as mentioned in recital (442), it established the profit on ‘any other reasonable method’, as foreseen by Article 2(6)(c) of the basic Regulation, corresponding to Article 2.2.2(iii) of the WTO ADA. Indeed, the Commission could not rely on letter (a) or point (i), since the Commission did not have at its disposal actual profit amounts of other exporters or producers in Taiwan, and no exporter or producer in Taiwan besides the sampled CSC Group submitted a questionnaire reply. Nor could the Commission rely on letter (b) or point (ii), since it did not have CSC Group’s profitability information specific for the same general category of products as CRF, and in any case it would have been also affected by the PMS. Thus, the Commission relied on letter (c) or point (iii). In so doing, the Commission did not have at its disposal a profit normally realised by other exporters or producers on sales of products of the same general category in Taiwan’s domestic market. Indeed, the profitability of all CRF producers in Taiwan was affected by the PMS. Additionally, the profitability of producers of downstream products would also be affected by the existence of a PMS for CRF as a raw material, while, given the uses of CRF, industries like construction or automotive would not be appropriate as such products would no longer be in the same general category. At the same time, producers of upstream products, such as HRF, would be also producers of CRF, and more upstream products, such as iron ore, cannot be considered in the same general category as CRF. Still, Article 2(6)(c) of the basic Regulation required the Commission to identify a profit normally realised in Taiwan.

- (447) Thus, the Commission resorted to the profit normally realised by CSC Group itself. Moreover, the Commission looked at the narrowest interpretation of the same general category of products, limited to the exact same product, i.e. CRF. So, the Commission took into account the profit normally realised by CSC Group’s on sales of CRF on Taiwan’s domestic market. However, CSC Group was loss-making on its domestic sales of CRF to unrelated customers. Since CSC Group was loss-making, the Commission could not identify a profit normally realised based on the actual profit of CSC Group, as there was none. Moreover, the evidence on file demonstrated that the overall profitability of the company was significantly affected by transactions which the basic Regulation does not consider to be made in the ordinary course of trade. Indeed, such transactions are excluded from profit analysis under Article 2(6) of that Regulation. Therefore, in order to find a profit normally realised, the Commission considered only CSC Group’s profit in the ordinary course of trade. CSC Group’s profit in the ordinary course of trade was much more accurate and granular than any actual profit realised by other producers on sales of products in the same general category as CRF in Taiwan. Considering the finding that such profit was subdued due to the particular market situation, the Commission adjusted it to the level that could be expected but for the effects of the PMS found. To that end, the Commission considered that the profit normally realised by CSC Group’s on sales of CRF on Taiwan’s domestic market, adjusted by the average of the difference between actual and hypothetical profitability of non-Chinese steel identified by the OECD-GFSEC report and reported in Table 15, constituted a profit normally realised or, given the PMS affecting the profit itself, realisable also by other exporters or producers on sales of products of the same general category in the domestic market of the country of origin. Since the study encompassed steel companies, it referred to products in the same general category as CRF and even included them. Thus, it was sufficiently homogeneous to be used to adjust CSC Group’s profit normally realised on domestic sales of CRF.

- (448) To further support this, the Commission noted that the OECD-GFSEC report simulated the difference between an actual profit and the hypothetical profit in the absence of overcapacity. Placed in the context of the case, this translated into a profit in the absence of the push-out effect of Chinese overcapacity, which in the hypothetical scenario did not surge, and thus did not displace Taiwan’s domestic sales. The OECD-GFSEC report simulated such hypothetical profit as an average of non-Chinese steel companies across the world. The database used by the OECD-GFSEC report included companies in Taiwan [^ntr118-L_202602547EN.000101-E0118]. While the OECD-GFSEC report does not reach a difference between actual and hypothetical profit specifically for Taiwan, it obtains an average difference for all non-Chinese companies, thus applicable to Taiwan’s companies as well, specifically to the sampled exporting producer CSC Group. It has to be noted that, for the purposes of the OECD-GFSEC report, actual profit data of the companies were taken into account to develop a hypothetical profit free from the effects of overcapacity. The differential between actual and hypothetical profit constituted an appropriate benchmark to free the profit of CSC Group in the ordinary course of trade from the effects of the PMS and identify a reasonable profit. Indeed, absent the PMS, CSC Group’s profitable sales of CRF would have been more profitable.

- (449) Addressing the remaining claims of the authorities of Taiwan, first, as far as the OECD-GFSEC report referred to the non-Chinese steel sector, and thus to the same general category of products as CRF, the resulting hypothetical profit was applicable to this sector in Taiwan. Second, the methodological notes were standard wording in policy and academic works proposing new economic or econometric analyses. The Commission did not consider the fact that the work was not peer-reviewed relevant, since it was prepared for the GFSEC, that constitutes the most renowned reference in the matter of steel overcapacity. The Commission failed to understand the basis on which the authorities of Taiwan argued that it contributed to the funding of the study. However, assuming that the authorities of Taiwan referred to the Union’s contributions to the OECD and/or the GFSEC, the Commission did not consider this to be relevant. The study was not published for the purposes of this investigation and the authorities of Taiwan did not put forward any alternative. Also, the analysis in figure 3 in box 3 of the OECD-GFSEC report did indeed rely on annual reports and financial statements, as corresponded to the ratio between the EBITDA and revenues. Third, the Commission considered the fact that the profit margin referred to the steel sector sufficient to meet the legal standard of ‘products of the same general category’ as CRF. Finally, the Commission considered that the objection brought by the authorities of Taiwan in their comments on initiation, summarised at recital (21), did not relate to the substance of the methodology, but rather to the requirements of the legal standards. Once the Commission addressed the comment in recital (22), it was fully entitled to rely on the methodology of the OECD-GFSEC report proposed by the complainant, which was not contested in its substance by any interested party. The Commission provided the clarifications required as regards its obligations under Article 2.2.2 of the WTO ADA and the corresponding provisions of the basic Regulation in recitals (442) and (446). The Commission considered that they constituted a sufficient justification for the proposed methodology. Therefore, the Commission rejected this claim.

- (450) Following the final disclosure, the CSC Group claimed a clerical error in calculating the average manufacturing unit cost established per PCN for one of its companies.

- (451) The Commission found this claim to be justified. Consequently, the normal value calculation for this company was revised, and the dumping margin for the CSC Group and for non-sampled cooperating companies was adjusted accordingly. The recalculation did not affect the residual dumping margin.

##### 3.4.4.   *Export price*

- (452) The sampled exporting producers exported to the Union directly to independent customers.

- (453) Therefore, the export price was the price actually paid or payable for the product concerned when sold for export to the Union, in accordance with Article 2(8) of the basic Regulation.

##### 3.4.5.   *Comparison*

- (454) Article 2(10) of the basic Regulation requires the Commission to make a fair comparison between the normal value and the export price at the same level of trade and to make allowances for differences in factors which affect prices and price comparability. In the case at hand the Commission chose to compare the normal value and the export price of the sampled exporting producers at the ex-works level of trade. As further explained below, where appropriate, the normal value and the export price were adjusted in order to: (i) net them back to the ex-works level; and (ii) make allowances for differences in factors which were claimed, and demonstrated, to affect prices and price comparability.

- (455) In the sensitive version of their questionnaire replies, both CSC and CHS requested the addition of one-digit at the end of the PCNs to account for alleged end-use market segments. According to the sampled exporting producers in Taiwan, these market segments resulted in price differences that the PCN structure put forward by the Commission did not take into account. Additionally, such PCN structure compared domestic sales in a high-value segment with export sales to the EU concentrated in a price-sensitive segment.

- (456) The Commission noted that the digit added by the sampled exporting producers in Taiwan did not correspond to a technical or physical characteristic which could allow to uniquely distinguish and identify the alleged market segments. The descriptions of the market segments showed that there was not a single variable or set of variables which could allow to distinguish the various segments, but each of them was defined based on different factors. For example, one segment was defined based on quality requirements and price inelasticity, another one was based on the content of a certain element, and a further one was based on the content of other elements. As a consequence, the only real distinguishing factor was the end-use, according to the sampled exporting producers’ own classification. By tracing back the characteristics of the products sold for the different end-uses, the sampled exporting producers artificially identified several market segments. However, the PCN structure proposed by the Commission already includes a number of factors that allow to classify the same products and compare them as like products. Such PCN structure was fully adequate. This was specifically the case because, shortly after the initiation of the case, on 1 October 2025, the Commission slightly modified the PCN structure to ensure that automotive grades based on proprietary automotive standards equivalent to European Nomenclature (‘EN’) standard 10130 2006 and solid solutions steels (EN standard 10268) were correctly classified in the PCN structure [^ntr119-L_202602547EN.000101-E0119]. Therefore, the Commission rejected this claim.

###### 3.4.5.1.   Adjustments made to the normal value

- (457) As explained in recitals (436) to (442), the normal value was established at the ex-works level of trade by using costs of production together with amounts for SG & A expenses and for profit, which were considered to be reasonable for that level of trade. Therefore, no adjustments were necessary to net the normal value back to the ex-works level.

- (458) In the sensitive version of their questionnaire reply, CSC Group claimed, in the event that the Commission rejected their proposal to modify the PCN structure as explained in recital (455), that they would have requested adjustments to the normal value or the export price for (i) differences in physical characteristics, (ii) differences in level of trade, and (iii) other factors. During the verification, CSC Group requested such adjustment based on Article 2(10)(k) of the basic Regulation concerning other factors and provided a calculation of the adjustment. CSC Group explained that the reasons for the adjustment were that (i) customers paid different prices on the domestic market because of the difference in these other factors, and (ii) such difference was due to a combination of differences in physical characteristics, because of specific steel grades, and of level of trade, because of the type of customers. With respect to the type of customers, CSC Group claimed that there were differences in functions and conditions of sales. Specifically, CSC certification as a qualified vendor to the customer was a prerequisite for business relations, and prices reflected such additional condition. The adjustment was thus meant to account for the difference in prices due to the end-use. In the domestic market, customers of alleged segment A accepted higher price, whereas, on the Union export market, sales focused mainly on alleged segment B, though there were also sales in alleged segment A. During the verification, CSC clarified that, based on the type of customers, segment A could be further split in segment A1 on the domestic market, which commanded higher prices, and segment A2 on the Union export market, which commanded lower prices but still higher than segment B.

- (459) At the outset, the Commission noted that CSC Group did not support its claim for price difference between sales sectors with any evidence in terms of costs.

- (460) As regards the claimed difference in physical characteristics, the Commission requested CSC Group to provide evidence on any physical or chemical characteristics which could clearly distinguish the alleged segments, as already mentioned in recital (456). CSC Group did not provide evidence demonstrating that some customers paid more than others for similar products for any difference in physical or chemical characteristics. Regarding other factors, the Commission requested CSC Group to provide supporting evidence such as additional selling expenses or additional costs. CSC Group explained that there was no specific additional packing costs and that the certification process costs was not depreciated but booked as an overhead expense. Therefore, the Commission considered that CSC Group did not provide such evidence.

- (461) As regards the differences claimed between the type of customers, the Commission noted that CSC Group did not make any correlation between the high prices and the costs of the certification process or other factors.

- (462) In addition, the Commission initially noted that high prices could be explained by the fact that CSC Group was the only domestic producer active in the alleged segment A. However, following a more detailed analysis of the segment and the degree of competition within it, the Commission concluded, in recital (481), that such a scenario would only be possible if CSC Group were restricting competition in that segment. In the absence of any evidence or claims to that effect, the Commission did not pursue that line of reasoning. Instead, its rebuttal focused on the complete lack of any demonstrated causal link between the alleged factors and the alleged price premium. The conclusions in recital (481) were disclosed to CSC Group in the additional final disclosure and were not contested by CSC Group.

- (463) Therefore, the Commission rejected this claim.

- (464) In its comments on final disclosure, CSC Group reiterated its request for a fair comparison between domestic and export prices by accounting for distinct end-use of CRF in the automotive and non-automotive sectors, given the substantial price difference depending on final customer. CSC Group repeated that the requested adjustments were necessary to correct for a comparison between a normal value based on higher-priced automotive sales and export prices based on lower-priced non-automotive sales. It restated that the automotive segment was fundamentally different from the non-automotive segment and was subject to sustained price differences, which were also examined during the Commission’s verification. First, sales to automotive original equipment manufacturers (‘OEMs’) and their supply chains were subject to stringent technical and quality requirements, long-term contracts, and demanding certification processes. The verification confirmed these requirements, including automotive-specific quality certifications and manufacturer approvals, with CSC Group being the sole qualified supplier in Taiwan for certain products. Second, reliability and risk mitigation were key considerations for the automotive customers, rather than the lowest price. Third, prices in the automotive segment were less elastic, because switching suppliers is particularly costly and time-consuming. As a result, qualified suppliers such as CSC face less competition when selling to automotive customers than when selling to non-automotive customers. Finally, CSC Group recalled that the safeguard measures on steel differentiated steel for automotive use [^ntr120-L_202602547EN.000101-E0120].

- (465) Regarding the legal basis, CSC Group clarified in its comments for disclosure that the adjustment would be warranted under Article 2(10)(k) of the basic Regulation for differences in other factors because (i) customers paid different prices on the domestic market because of the difference in those factors, and (ii) such difference was due to a combination of factors listed in Article 2(10) of the basic Regulation, namely Article 2(10)(a) of the basic Regulation for differences in physical characteristics, because of specific steel grades, and 2(10)(d)(i) of the basic Regulation for differences in the level of trade, because of the type of customers.

- (466) Against this background, the CSC Group claimed that the Commission at recitals (459) to (461) did not properly address its comments. In particular, CSC Group alleged that the Commission substantially addressed only the claimed differences in physical characteristics but did not address in substance the differences in the level of trade and in other factors. CSC Group clarified that the distinction between automotive and non-automotive products was not solely based on differences in the physical characteristics but on a combination of factors, as reflected in the different legal bases. According to CSC Group, by rejecting the claim only based on physical characteristics, the Commission did not consider the question of whether the prices of automotive and automotive CRF are comparable without an adjustment, taking into account the factors affecting those prices, including differences in physical characteristics. In this respect, CSC Group referred to a previous investigation on acrylonitrile-butadiene-styrene resins [^ntr121-L_202602547EN.000101-E0121] (‘ABS’). Finally, CSC Group claimed that it provided evidence demonstrating that some customers paid more than others for similar products due to differences in physical or chemical characteristics, in the form of requirements for specific technical specifications and certifications linked to these specifications, including specific engineering and material standards. Additionally, CSC was able to charge higher prices in domestic sales for the automotive industry.

- (467) The Commission disagreed with CSC Group on the fact that it did not properly address its claim. In recitals (459) to (461), the Commission addressed the separate factors and legal bases put forward by CSC Group.

- (468) At recital (460), the Commission addressed the claimed differences in physical characteristics. The Commission did not mean to address the full claim by means of this recital alone but analysed on that occasion one of the claimed factors affecting prices. The Commission reiterated, as already noted in recital (460), that CSC Group did not provide any measurable physical and technical characteristics of the product under investigation which could support its claim. The certification, specifications and standards that CSC brought forward as arguments do not physically or chemically differentiate CRF for the automotive industry from CRF for other industries. Consequently, CSC group failed to prove that physical characteristics were a factor that could warrant an adjustment.

- (469) Furthermore, CSC Group took exception with the relevance of differences in costs in the context of an adjustment for physical characteristics. CSC Group claimed that under Article 2(10)(a) of the basic Regulation, adjustments for physical characteristics must be quantified solely on a ‘reasonable estimate of the market value of that difference’ and noted that the Commission treated such adjustments as price allowances in its internal calculation sheets.

- (470) The Commission noted that CSC Group ignored the explicit condition precedent set out in the chapeau of Article 2(10), which states that an adjustment shall only be granted ‘if it is demonstrated that [the factors] affect price comparability’. The phrase ‘reasonable estimate of the market value’ invoked by the CSC Group dictates the method of quantification of an allowance only after the threshold requirement of price affectation has been established by the claimant. In the present case, the CSC Group failed to demonstrate that the specialized physical requirements of the automotive sector systematically led to distinct price settings on the domestic market for identical product types that are not already fully and adequately reflected in the PCN matching matrix. The Commission emphasized that its internal modelling choice to apply allowances to prices did not relieve the exporting producer of its primary legal obligation to provide verifiable evidence that the physical differences distort a fair comparison. In any case, the Commission examined the cost of production to analyse whether the alleged higher requirements concerning technical specifications and quality were reflected in it, as would be logical, and could not conclude they affected the cost of production as explained in recital (460). Therefore, the Commission rejected this claim.

- (471) Moreover, CSC Group claimed that the Commission incorrectly rejected CSC Group’s adjustment claims regarding the level of trade and other factors based on the absence of cost variations between similar products sold to automotive sector and non-automotive sector. According to CSC Group, the Commission, by referring to costs, applied an incorrect legal standard. CSC Group claimed that Article 2(10)(d)(i), and (k) of the basic Regulation did not require evidence of differences in production costs to warrant an adjustment. According to CSC Group, the relevant elements are the difference in market value and in prices paid by customers.

- (472) At the outset, the Commission rejected the interpretation of the basic Regulation provided by CSC Group. The Commission did not consider that a cost analysis is the primary or only standard to assess a claim under Article 2(10)(d)(i), and (k) of the basic Regulation. However, the Commission noted that it is fully entitled to request and evaluate cost structures as an objective evidentiary tool to verify whether an alleged domestic price difference is structural, consistent, and genuinely affects price comparability. The Commission recalls that under Article 2(10) of the basic Regulation, the primary objective of the investigation is to ensure a fair comparison between the export price and the normal value. Where an interested party requests an adjustment under Article 2(10), it bears the burden of proof to demonstrate that its claim satisfies both the substantive and evidentiary conditions laid down in that provision. In the case at hand, the Commission considered that the difference in market value and in prices paid by customers must be proved by reference to a difference in the level of trade and a difference in other factors respectively.

- (473) With regard to the level of trade, the Commission further considered that a level of trade adjustment requires proof of consistent and distinct commercial functions in functions and prices of the seller for the different levels of trade (for instance wholesale versus retail) in the domestic market and that this evidence was not submitted. As clear from the differentiation of sales channel made in the transaction-by-transaction listing of domestic and Union sales, CSC sold to both traders and end users both in the domestic and in the Union market. With regard to the alleged automotive sector, CSC sold to both automotive manufacturers and traders in the domestic market, whereas it sold only to traders in the Union market. Traders of automotive products in the domestic and Union market operated at the same level of trade. Additionally, any alleged difference in level of trade between the domestic market (traders of automotive products and automotive makers) and the export market (only traders of automotive products) was not demonstrated by any differences in functions and prices of CSC Group for the different levels of trade (i.e., traders or automotive makers) in the domestic market. Be it domestic end users (in the automotive sector or not) or domestic traders (in the automotive sector or not), all CSC Group’s domestic market buyers purchased directly from CSC Group, without differences in functions and prices. Automotive manufacturers and non-automotive purchasers operated at the same functional level: they are industrial end-users purchasing directly from CSC Group, whether in the construction, tool or automotive industry. The features of the automotive industry on which CSC based its claim represented standard business-to-business practices, rather than a distinct step in the channel of distribution. Since the CSC Group failed to show a different pricing structure based on distinct distribution channels, the criteria of Article 2(10)(d) of the basic Regulation were not met. Therefore, the Commission rejected the claim for a level of trade adjustment.

- (474) The Commission then analysed whether any adjustment would be warranted under Article 2(10)(k).

- (475) At the outset, the Commission noted that CSC claimed that the other factors to be considered under Article 2(10)(k) were ‘a combination of the following factors listed in article 2(10) basic Regulation, namely Article 2(10)(a) for differences in physical characteristics and 2(10)(d)(i) for differences in level of trade’.

- (476) Article 2(10)(k) concerns exclusively adjustments for factors not provided for under points (a) to (j). Consequently, CSC failed to identify any factor that could be addressed under this provision. The Commission recalled that Article 2(10)(k) of the basic Regulation cannot be interpreted as a mechanism to circumvent the strict evidentiary benchmarks established under the preceding sub-paragraphs of Article 2(10) of the basic Regulation.

- (477) In any event, as Article 2(10)(k) also requires a causal link between the factor claimed and the alleged price effect, the Commission analysed all the evidence on file and the argumentation brought forward by CSC.

- (478) In essence, the claim of CSC rests on the fact that customers consistently paid different prices on the domestic market. However, nothing in its argumentation clearly identified any factor (different from those provided for under points (a) to (j) of Article 2(10) of the basic Regulation) that would explain the higher prices. Based on the evidence on file, the Commission considered that the stringent technical and quality requirements, long-term contracts, and demanding certification processes mentioned did not justify the prices on their own. The prices charged were also due to a deliberate choice of its customers, as CSC itself explained (see recital (464)). These constituted ordinary commercial conditions, not a factor affecting price comparability.

- (479) The reference to the safeguard measures was ineffective, since in that context the Commission noted that corrosion resistant steel for the automotive industry were not covered in the industry’s request for anti-dumping measures, whereas in this investigation on CRF they are clearly part of the product scope. Instead, in the investigation on ABS, the category of mass-balanced ABS at issue was not captured by the PCN, whereas in the present investigation the destination to the automotive industry constitutes an end use and is not relevant for the PCN classification, as explained by the Commission in recital (456). So, while a difference not captured by the PCN can be relevant for an adjustment under Article 2(10)(k) of the basic Regulation, the investigation on ABS does not constitute a useful example for a possible adjustment in the present case.

- (480) Finally, in the alternative to the adjustments for differences in physical characteristics, level of trade and other factors, CSC Group claimed that, if the Commission considered that CSC’s higher domestic automotive prices resulted from the fact that CSC is the only domestic producer active in the segment, those prices are formed under competitive conditions that do not exist on the EU market. Indeed, CSC did not hold a comparable position in the Union market. So, the Commission should exclude domestic automotive sales from the comparison, because the EU export prices did not have a price premium corresponding to the Taiwanese automotive market.

- (481) The Commission noted that each segment of each geographical market is subject to its own specific competitive conditions. Those conditions are shaped, *inter alia*, by the number of suppliers and customers active in the market and by their respective market positions. The mere fact that, under those conditions, one customer is willing to pay more and another less for the same product type does not justify a downward adjustment for the latter and an upward adjustment for the former or any exclusions from the normal value. Such price differences may simply reflect normal market forces and should therefore contribute to the determination of normal value in a market economy subject to normal competitive conditions. The fact that CSC happens to be the only domestic producer active in the relevant segment does not, in itself, mean that it is able to charge a premium for that reason. CSC remains subject to competitive constraints, since the segment is open to imports and to entry by other domestic producers. The situation might be different if CSC were able to restrict competition, whether through unilateral conduct or through agreements with other market participants. However, CSC has not claimed that this is the case.

- (482) For all the reasons explained above, the Commission rejected this claim.

- (483) Following the additional final disclosure, with regard to physical differences and the Commission’s rebuttal in recital (468), CSC Group claimed that technical specifications and standards applicable to automotive products were not commercial labels attached to otherwise identical products. Rather, CSC Group manufactured the CRF concerned in accordance with the technical specifications required by the individual automotive manufacturer. The purpose of the technical standards is to define the characteristics that the steel must possess in order to satisfy the manufacturer’s requirements.

- (484) The Commission noted that CSC Group described the mere process of customisation of products at the request of a client. Such customisation, precisely because it is individualised, does not define a specific physical characteristic or a homogeneous set of physical characteristics that allow to distinguish CRF destined to the automotive sector from other CRF. Therefore, the Commission rejected this claim.

- (485) In addition, CSC Group claimed that the Commission, insofar it found in recital (470) that CSC Group failed to demonstrate that the physical requirements of the automotive sectors resulted in distinct price-setting, acknowledged the existence of a difference in physical characteristics.

- (486) The Commission noted that recital (470) merely recalled the claim that CSC Group failed to substantiate. Thus, it rejected this claim.

- (487) Second, with regard to physical differences, CSC Group claimed that the Commission erred in finding that the physical requirements of the automotive sectors did not result in distinct price-setting. CSC Group argued that it had provided sufficient evidence demonstrating an effect on prices and price comparability. This evidence included: (i) strict technical specifications and standards; (ii) the demanding qualification process required by automotive manufacturers to obtain the status of qualified vendor; and (iii) the maintenance of the automotive-specific ISO/TS16949 Quality Management System certification. Such qualifications and certifications made switching suppliers more burdensome and time-consuming for automotive customers, thereby reducing competition and price elasticity. This explained the fact that CSC Group was the only qualified vendor of certain CRF products sold to automotive end-users in Taiwan. Finally, the Commission recorded in CSC’s mission report that the automotive segment was more profitable compared with other coil segments and that the existence of a price premium for automotive products was fully corroborated by CSC Group’s internal sales tracking and official domestic price catalogue.

- (488) At the outset, the Commission noted that CSC Group expressly listed these elements as relevant under its claim for differences in physical characteristics. So, the Commission assessed them under this angle, even though they appeared ineffective for this claim. The Commission further noted that the CSC Group failed to substantiate its claim concerning alleged differences in physical characteristics, as it did not submit any new elements in support of its assertion. Moreover, the Commission found that this type of sales on the domestic market was not the only one subject to specific norms. For example, the sales to the Union to automotive users were also subject to European Nomenclature standards. Furthermore, the qualified vendor status and the entrenched commercial relationships did not define physical characteristics but were ordinary commercial conditions deriving from a deliberate choice of CSC Group’s customers. Therefore, the Commission reiterated that the evidence referred to by the CSC Group failed to demonstrate the existence of one or more specific physical characteristics that would distinguish CRF products destined for the automotive sector from other types of CRF products. CSC identified automotive sales in its internal system, as it also identified other types of steel such as tool or drum steel, simply based on the end use and customer. This is confirmed by the fact that CSC’s system did not differentiate CRF sold to an automaker, for which CSC was a qualified vendor, and CRF sold to a trader of automotive parts, for which CSC was not a qualified vendor. Consequently, in the absence of any substantiated evidence isolating the impact of physical characteristics from other normal commercial factors, the Commission rejected the CSC Group’s claim.

- (489) CSC Group claimed that the Commission required an impossible burden of proof, higher than the standard applied by the Court of Justice in *Sinopec* [^ntr122-L_202602547EN.000101-E0122]. CSC Group claimed that it demonstrated that the relevant differences exist and that they coincide with a substantial and consistent price premium.

- (490) The Commission considered that CSC Group failed to establish that the factual circumstances were relevant to price comparability and that they necessarily affected it. In this regard, the CSC Group submitted a mixed claim under Article 2(10)(k) of the basic Regulation based on a combination of factors falling under Article 2(10)(a) and Article 2(10)(d)(i) thereof. However, in its latest claim referred to in recital (487), CSC Group failed to specify which individual elements justified which specific adjustment. In other words, CSC Group identified a price difference on the domestic market depending on a customer, which, as explained in recital (481) is a normal occurrence on a free market which should be reflected in, rather than eliminated from, the normal value. CSC Group then failed to explain that price difference in terms of concrete price affecting factors as required by Article 2(10) of the basic Regulation. Without such connection, there is no reason for the adjustment. A mere price difference does not necessarily show a price premium; without any demonstrable link to a concrete factor, is an ordinary market occurrence shaping the normal value for a particular product type. Consequently, in the absence of a clear distinction and proper substantiation of the legal grounds claimed, the Commission rejected the claim.

- (491) CSC Group also reiterated its claim on the relevance of cost of production, already addressed at recital (470).

- (492) Third, as to the alleged difference in the level of trade, CSC Group claimed that its sales to the automotive sector were characterized by qualified vendor status, demanding product certification procedures, manufacturer-specific production and quality requirements, and automotive-specific quality management certification. CSC Group argued that these requirements were not present in the same manner in its sales to other industrial customers. Furthermore, CSC Group claimed that since Article 2(10)(d)(i) of the basic Regulation expressly recognized that differences in the level of trade may include ‘any differences which may arise in OEM (original equipment manufacturer) sales’, the concept of level of trade encompassed more than simply the customer's position in the distribution chain.

- (493) The Commission considered that qualified vendor status, demanding product certification procedures, manufacturer-specific production, quality requirements, and automotive-specific quality management certification did not constitute differences in the level of trade. By way of example, whether or not a vendor was qualified did not change its functions as a selling producer. The reference to ‘differences which may arise in OEM […] sales’ of Article 2(10)(d)(i) of the basic Regulation deals with adjustments where there is a difference in prices between own-branded products (Own Brand Manufacturer sales or OBM sales) and OEM sales, the latter being sales to buyers who would usually resell the product under their own brand and carry out the functions, and bear the corresponding costs, notably after-sales and advertising costs. In the case of CSC, there are no such differences in sales as all its sales were own-branded. Thus, the Commission considered that CSC Group did not prove that, in this instance, OEM sales constituted a different level of trade. Therefore, the Commission rejected this claim.

- (494) Fourth, as to differences in other factors, CSC Group claimed that it had identified several factors specific to automotive sales that were not, or at least not necessarily, captured by the other subparagraphs of Article 2(10) basic Regulation. These factors included: (i) demanding certification procedures applicable to automotive suppliers, (ii) long-term contracts and technical agreements requiring strict compliance with automotive manufacturers’ quality management systems and process standards. While acknowledging that certain requirements, such as manufacturer-specific technical specifications, might be reflected in the physical characteristics of the product and thus fall within Article 2(10)(a), CSC Group argued that other factors, such as the qualification and certification process itself, did not necessarily constitute a physical difference. Consequently, to account for the combined effect of these factors, which did not all fall within the specific categories listed in the preceding subparagraphs of Article 2(10), it relied, in the alternative, on Article 2(10)(k). According to CSC Group, the claim under Article 2(10)(k) did not rest merely on the existence of different domestic prices, but on specific underlying demonstrating that automotive customers consistently paid substantially higher prices when those factors were present.

- (495) At the outset, the Commission noted that as set out in recital (487), CSC Group had also listed the qualification and certification process, as well as the compliance with automotive manufacturers’ requirements, among the factors supporting a claim for an adjustment for differences in physical characteristics under Article 2(10)(a) of the basic Regulation. In its subsequent argument, however, CSC Group acknowledged that the qualification and certification process did not necessarily constitute a physical difference. Regarding the remaining factors, the Commission reiterated its view that demanding certification procedures, long-term contracts and technical agreements requiring strict compliance with automotive manufacturers’ requirements did not determine prices on their own. The pricing levels resulted also from the commercial choices of the customers concerned. Moreover, as explained in recital (490), a price difference does not in itself demonstrate a price premium. It could equally demonstrate a price deduction for non-automotive sales and thus without any concert connection to a particular factor is a normal market occurrence which should be reflected in the normal value for a particular product type during comparison with the export price rather than neutralised. Consequently, as these elements constituted ordinary commercial conditions and, without any concrete link to a price premium, they do not qualify as a factor affecting price comparability within the meaning of the Article 2(10) of the basic Regulation. Therefore, the Commission rejected this claim.

- (496) Fifth, CSC Group reiterated the relevance of the safeguard measures on steel mentioned in recital (464) and of the investigation on ABS mentioned in recital (466), on which the Commission maintained the view expressed in recital (479).

- (497) Finally, CSC Group claimed a contradiction between the Commission’s assertion in paragraph 356 of the General Disclosure Document (reflected in recital (462)) according to which ‘high prices could be explained by the fact that CSC Group was the only domestic producer active in the alleged segment A’, and the statement in recital (481) according to which the fact that CSC happened to be the only domestic producer active in the relevant segment did not, in itself, mean that it was able to charge a premium for that reason. CSC Group alleged that the question remained of whether comparing domestic automotive prices formed under the particular competitive position of CSC Group with CSC Group’s export prices formed under different competitive conditions resulted in a fair comparison.

- (498) The Commission noted that the apparent contradiction between paragraph 356 in the General Disclosure Document (reflected in recital (462)) and recital (481) reflected the evolution of its assessment of the relevant market conditions and CSC Group’s position within the segment concerned throughout the investigation. Paragraph 356 was based on the initial hypothesis that CSC Group could leverage its market position to charge higher prices. However, a more detailed analysis established that the segment was open to competition from both domestic suppliers and exporters. In those circumstances, the Commission concluded that CSC Group could sustain the alleged price premium only if competition in that segment were restricted, either through unilateral conduct or in coordination with other market participants. In the absence of any evidence or even claim of such restrictions, the initial hypothesis underlying paragraph 356 could not be sustained, as explained in recital (462). Indeed, in its comments on the additional final disclosure, CSC Group did not contest the Commission’s conclusion in recital (481), pointing instead to the alleged contradiction. Therefore, the Commission rejected this claim.

###### 3.4.5.2.   Adjustments made to the export price

- (499) In order to net the export price back to the ex-works level of trade, adjustments were made on the account of: customs duty, other import charges, freight, insurance, handling loading and ancillary expenses.

- (500) Allowances were made for the following factors affecting prices and price comparability: bank charges, commissions and, only for CSC, credit cost.

##### 3.4.6.   *Dumping margins*

- (501) For the sampled exporting producers, the Commission compared the weighted average normal value of each type of the like product with the weighted average export price of the corresponding type of the product concerned, in accordance with Article 2(11) and (12) of the basic Regulation.

- (502) During the verification visit, CSC Group provided a simplified version of the profit and loss accounts of one of its unrelated traders in third countries, involved in export sales to the Union, so that such expenses could be used to establish the CIF Union frontier price, duty unpaid, used as denominator to express the dumping margin as a duty.

- (503) However, the costs and profits reported in such profit and loss accounts did not cover all traders, while they did include the operations of that trader related to other business and other products than the product concerned. Moreover, the type of costs included in the SG & A for that unrelated trader was not specific enough to avoid double-counting of e.g. transport or other costs. Finally, none of these documents offered any light on whether the final value of the goods at CIF Union border included both the costs incurred and the alleged profits realised by those intermediaries.

- (504) Therefore, the Commission rejected the claim,

- (505) On this basis, the definitive weighted average dumping margins, expressed as a percentage of the CIF Union frontier price, duty unpaid, are as follows:

| Company | Definitive dumping margin (%) |
| --- | --- |
| — China Steel Corporation — Chung Hung Steel Corporation | 33,3 |

- (506) For the cooperating exporting producer outside the sample, the Commission usually calculates the weighted average dumping margin, in accordance with Article 9(6) of the basic Regulation. However, in this instance the Commission sampled only one group of exporting producers in Taiwan. Therefore, that margin corresponded to the margin of the sampled group of exporting producers.

- (507) On this basis, the definitive dumping margin of the cooperating exporting producers outside the sample is 33,3 %.

- (508) For all other exporting producers in Taiwan, the Commission established the dumping margin on the basis of the facts available, in accordance with Article 18 of the basic Regulation. To this end, the Commission determined the level of cooperation of the exporting producers. The level of cooperation is the volume of exports of the cooperating exporting producers to the Union expressed as proportion of the total imports from Taiwan to the Union in the IP, that were established on the basis of Eurostat.

- (509) The level of cooperation in this case is low because the imports of the cooperating exporting producers constituted around [60-70 %] of the total exports to the Union during the IP. On this basis, the Commission decided to establish the residual dumping margin at the level of the highest margins established for the product types representing more than 5 % of the exports to the Union by CSC Group.

- (510) The definitive dumping margins, expressed as a percentage of the CIF Union frontier price, duty unpaid, are as follows:

| Company | Definitive dumping margin (%) |
| --- | --- |
| — China Steel Corporation — Chung Hung Steel Corporation | 33,3 |
| Other cooperating companies | 33,3 |
| All other imports originating in Taiwan | 59,6 |

#### 3.5.   **Türkiye**

##### 3.5.1.   *Particular market situation: push-out effect*

###### 3.5.1.1.   Analysis

- (511) In order to establish the existence of a PMS in the market of Türkiye due to the push-out effect of Chinese imports of CRF, the Commission carried out the following analysis: first, the Commission analysed the degree of penetration of Chinese imports in Türkiye’s domestic market in terms of volume; secondly, the Commission looked at the impact of these imports on the prices in Türkiye’s domestic market.

- (512) The Commission examined country-wide indicators and company-level indicators based on data provided by the GOT and the sample exporting producers respectively. Both sets of data were verified on spot as indicated in recital (104).

###### 3.5.1.2.   Consumption

- (513) The Commission determined the captive consumption based on captive use reported by the GOT for each Turkish CRF producer. No captive sales were reported. Consumption on free market was established as a sum of domestic sales on the free market and imports. The sum of captive consumption and consumption on free market represents total consumption of CRF in Türkiye.

- (514) The consumption of CRF in Türkiye evolved as follows:

- (515) Cold-rolled flat steel products can be further processed into galvanised steel or organic coated (pre-painted) steel. In the period considered, the consumption of CRF to manufacture downstream products decreased between 2022 and 2023. In 2024, it returned to the level of 2022 and continued growing in the investigation period. In the investigation period, captive consumption of CRF in Türkiye was by 8 % higher than in 2022.

- (516) Consumption on the free market increased by 5 % in 2023. However, afterwards in 2024, it dropped and remained at similar level in the investigation period. In total, consumption on free market decreased by 4 % between 2022 and the investigation period.

- (517) Total consumption temporarily decreased in 2023 (-5 %), then oscillated close to the 2022 levels in 2024 and in the investigation period.

- (520) Total imports increased during the period considered (+8 %), including a significant increase in 2023 (+27 %). Their market share increased by 12 % or [4-4,9] p.p. Imported CRF originated mainly in Korea, the Union, Russia and in the PRC.

- (521) Imports from the PRC fluctuated in the period considered. In 2023, they increased by 10 % as compared to 2022. In 2024, they dropped to less than 70 % of the 2022 volume. In the investigation period, they partially recovered and reached 85 % of the 2022 volume. Market share of Chinese CRF in Türkiye fluctuated accordingly. It slightly increased by 4 % or [0,4-0,5] p.p. in the first two years of the period considered, then fell by [2,7-3,2] p.p. in 2024. In the investigation period, it reached [7,2-8,9] %.

- (522) Imports from Korea doubled in the period considered. The surge was particularly pronounced between 2022 and 2023 when the import volumes increased by 133 %. In 2024, the imports remained well above the level of 2022 (+69 %) and further grew in the investigation period to reach 205 % of 2022.

- (523) Considering the shrinking domestic market, the market share of Korean imports grew even more than their volume. In the period considered, it increased from [5,3-6,5] % in 2022 to [11,3-13,9] % in the investigation period, which represents a growth of 113 %.

- (524) Imports from the Union increased by 90 % between 2022 and 2024. In the investigation period, they dropped but remained 69 % higher than in 2022. The market share of imports from the Union increased by 76 % from [4,9-6,0] % in 2022 to [8,6-10,6] % in the investigation period.

- (525) Imports from Russia decreased by almost 30 % in the period considered. Their market share dropped from [10,6-13,1] % in 2022 to [7,9-9,7] % in the investigation period.

- (526) Although the United Kingdom was the fifth most important source of imported CRF in Türkiye, it lagged far behind the top four countries of origin with decreasing import volumes (-35 %) and negligible market shares (between 0,3 % and 1,1 % in the period considered, while the lowest market share was observed in the investigation period).

- (530) Market share of the domestic sales decreased accordingly. Between 2022 and the investigation period Turkish CRF producers lost 6 % of their domestic market. In 2023, import volumes increased significantly which translated into a considerable decrease of the share of domestic sales on the Turkish CRF market (-10 %).

- (531) Nominal domestic sales price increased significantly (+60 %). When corrected for the effect of inflation, it decreased continuously throughout the period considered. In the investigation period, it was by 13 % lower than in 2022.

- (533) Average import prices remained above the level of domestic prices throughout the period considered with the exception of 2023. The average was however heavily influenced by imports from the Union, which were by up to 30 % higher than domestic prices (in 2023). In the investigation period, prices of CRF imported from the Union exceeded the domestic prices by 28 %.

- (534) The average price of imports from the PRC was 13 % higher than the domestic prices in 2022, then dropped to the level of 89 % of the domestic prices in 2023. In the following period (2024, investigation period), they remained roughly at the same level of domestic prices.

###### 3.5.1.5.   Conclusion

- (535) Although the domestic sales of Turkish CRF producers decreased at a higher pace than the consumption on free market, they maintained a dominant market share on the Turkish CRF market. The total increase of imports was driven mainly by imports from Korea and the Union. Imports from the PRC decreased and lost market share in the period considered. In addition, the PRC ranked only as number four in terms of origin of imported CRF behind Korea, the Union and Russia.

- (536) In terms of prices, Chinese import prices were at similar levels as domestic prices, while import prices from other supplying countries, namely the Union, were significantly higher.

- (537) Considering the volumes, prices and market shares of domestic sales by Turkish CRF producers and import volumes and prices from China, Korea and the Union, the Commission found that the market penetration by CRF of Chinese origin was rather modest and could not have significantly affected the prices on the Turkish CRF market. Consequently, the Commission concluded that Chinese imports did not create a PMS in the Turkish market.

##### 3.5.2.   *Application of the provisions of Article 18(1) of the basic Regulation*

###### 3.5.2.1.   Tatmetal Çelik Sanayi ve Ticaret A. Ş. (‘Tatmetal’)

- (538) The Commission informed in the Notice of Initiation and in the letter accompanying the questionnaire sent to Tatmetal 18 September 2025, that where it was found that any interested party supplied false or misleading information, the information may be disregarded, and use may be made of facts available.

- (539) The Commission informed Tatmetal on 16 April 2026 of its intention to apply Article 18 of the basic Regulation due to a number of issues which emerged during the verification visit.

- (540) First, with regard to full-hard steel, Tatmetal made a deliberate choice to change the Product Control Number construction and informed the Commission only during the onsite verification. This irregularity affected both domestic and export sales transactions.

- (541) Second, the Commission identified sales, in the investigation period, of the product under investigation to the free trade zones in Türkiye to customers who processed cold-rolled flat steel products into downstream products of different characteristics and use, e.g. kitchenware. These sales were classified as other sales and not reported as domestic sales, nor EU sales.

- (542) Third, Tatmetal reported two separate sets of foreign exchange rates: the foreign exchange rates for domestic sales were extracted from the company’s accounting system, while the exchange rates applicable to EU sales were reported from the customs invoice. The Commission identified several instances of non-matching exchange rates on the same date reported from the company’s accounting system. The Commission further noted that the customs exchange rates used in the sales to the Union to unrelated customers for the same dates were lower than the exchange rates reported from the company’s accounting system.

- (543) Fourth, with regard to EU sales reported by Tatmetal, the Commission identified a number of transactions which were in fact exported to North Macedonia, not the Union. These transactions were registered in the port of Thessaloniki, but the final destination was located in North Macedonia, as corroborated with the company source data and the collected sampled transactions.

- (544) Fifth, the transport expenses reported in EU sales table were not complete. Several CIF and CFR transactions did not have corresponding ocean freight or handling expense invoices reported.

- (545) In its comments, with regard to point one on the PCN construction, Tatmetal argued that full hard products did not have a surface quality and were not described by the relevant European norm (EN 10130) in the same way as annealed cold-rolled products and that allocating a surface quality as defined in the PCN would have led to providing misleading information.

- (546) The Commission considered the unexposed surface aspect as the most suitable to full hard products, as full hard products are not meant to be exposed and are not finished in the sense of EN 10130. The Commission reclassified the affected PCN in the domestic sales listing and EU sales listing.
