- (547) With regard to point two on sales made to the free trade zones in Türkiye, Tatmetal submitted that free trade zone sales were not domestic sales under Tatmetal’s accounting rules, and they could not be identified as EU-specific export sales under Turkish Law. Tatmetal alleged that free trade zone sales were defined by the Turkish law as export sales, not domestic sale, as under Turkish export and VAT legislation [^ntr125-L_202602547EN.000101-E0125] shipments from Türkiye to a free zone fall within the export regime. Tatmetal further quoted the Law No. 3218 on Free Zones, adopted on 6 June 1985 [^ntr126-L_202602547EN.000101-E0126], under which free zones, although forming part of the Turkish Customs Territory, are treated as outside that territory for the purposes of import duties and trade-policy measures, and goods placed in a free zone ordinarily benefit from the facilities attached to exportation. Furthermore, Tatmetal submitted supporting documents concerning the freight cost for the transactions sold to the free zones in case the Commission decided to treat the sales as either domestic or EU sales.

- (548) The Commission noted the explanation of Tatmetal that, although the free zones form part of the Turkish Customs Territory, they are treated as outside that territory for the purposes of import duties and trade-policy measures. In the present case, the Commission observed that the product under investigation was sold to customers in the free zones, which are part of the Turkish Customs Territory. Tatmetal claimed that they had no information on what happened to the goods after the sales were concluded. The Commission noted from the websites of the respective customers that their business activities were in downstream industries, such as kitchenware and steel shelves systems. Substantial transformation of the product under investigation was therefore necessary in the free zones, under the Turkish Customs Territory, before the subsequent export. The Commission therefore treated these sales as sales to the domestic market.

- (549) Furthermore, the Commission noted that the additional supporting documents concerning the transport cost for the transactions sold to the free zones in Türkiye consisted of a worksheet for domestic freight cost with a unit freight cost per zones in Türkiye. The Commission noted that Tatmetal did not provide any actual accounting data supporting the allocation of freight expenses to the sales to the free trade zones. The argument was rejected.

- (550) With regard to point three on exchange rates, Tatmetal submitted that for export sales, it reported the foreign exchange rate (‘FX’) based the customs closing date (‘intac’), whereas for domestic sales Tatmetal applied the Turkish Central Bank (‘TCB’) daily exchange rate. Tatmetal explained that the buying rate of TCB applied for EU sales listing, and the selling rate of TCB applied for domestic sales listing.

- (551) The Commission noted that Tatmetal made a deliberate choice of converting the net invoice value in TRY with a FX based on the customs closing date (intac). It remained unclear why the FX rate on the intac date was more appropriate to use instead of the FX rate of the invoice date, except for the company’s statement that this approach was aligned with the accounting records. The Commission observed that, in most of the cases, the intac exchange rate reported in the EU sales listing was higher than the buying or selling FX applicable at the same invoice date reported in the EU sales listing. This resulted in an increased value of the invoice in the accounting currency in comparison to the FX of the Turkish Central Bank, applied for the foreign currency sales on the domestic market. The company only explained to the Commission that it did not use the FX rate of the invoice date in sales to the Union to unrelated customers during the on-spot verification. Such late disclosure of information prevented the Commission’s from analysing the submitted data properly, so impeding the investigation significantly.

- (552) The Commission further noted that the FX reported in the EU sales listing applicable at the purchase order date were in some cases different from the FX reported in the domestic sales listing applicable at the purchase order date. In this situation, the Commission considered that a consistent use of the monthly average FX rates as appropriate to correct the inconsistencies and incorrectly reported FX rates. The Commission therefore applied the facts available with regards to the reported FX rates. The arguments of Tatmetal were rejected on those grounds.

- (553) With regard to point four on sales to North Macedonia, Tatmetal explained that the sales listing could have been inaccurate or misleading for destination-allocation purposes because they could have reflected customer address information, invoice-related data, automatically assigned ERP addresses, or incorrect preliminary shipment information. For this reason, Tatmetal reported the country information on the basis of the official customs list. Tatmetal submitted documents to demonstrate that the final destination of the product under investigation of the transactions mapped by the Commission were shipped to Greece, not North Macedonia.

- (554) The Commission noted that Tatmetal decided to rely on the information from the export customs declaration. In the additional documents submitted, Tatmetal referred to the delivery term (CIF/CFR Thessaloniki) and the port of discharge from the export customs declaration (Thessaloniki, Greece). For the transactions sampled by the Commission, in all four instances the final destination on the sales order confirmation was North Macedonia, and/or the buyers address was located in North Macedonia, and/or the buyers address at the invoice was located in North Macedonia, and the final destination in the ERP sales order entries in all four instances was located in North Macedonia. Additionally, Tatmetal did not support its claim by, e.g. submitting the customs import declaration, delivery notes, or complete bill of lading / packing list with the final destination. As the Commission was not able to confirm whether these transactions ended in the Union, it excluded them from the EU sales listing. The argument was rejected. In further checks, the Commission identified an additional transaction, which was sold to Switzerland. This transaction was removed from the EU sales listing.

- (555) With regard to point five on transport expenses in EU sales listing, Tatmetal argued that certain cost associated with export were not directly identifiable in the customs declaration forms. Instead, those costs were reported in the selling, general and administrative cost of export sales, in the category ‘others’ or in ‘headquarter fees’. In reply to the Article 18 letter, Tatmetal carried out manual review of affected sales to report missing ocean freight and reported the additional ocean freight and handling expense.

- (556) The Commission reviewed the submitted calculations and working files for the missing export-related handling cost and ocean freight and accepted the calculation.

- (557) On 15 July 2026, the Commission informed Tatmetal that it identified further issues on which basis it intended to apply the provisions of Article 18 of the basic Regulation (‘second Article 18 letter’).

- (558) The Commission pointed out that the company did not provide any evidence supporting the allocation of freight expenses to domestic sales transactions. During the on-spot verification, the company claimed that there was no link between the freight cost incurred on the domestic market and the individual transactions. The company explained how it used internal data to report the freight cost for domestic sales transactions. In this respect, the company failed to provide access to the data used to establish the values of freight cost reported per transaction and only provided the preparation worksheet for domestic sales transactions that contained the reported allowances, including freight cost, on the very last day of the on-spot verification. Furthermore, the relevant employees were not available for further clarifications during the on-spot verification. Finally, no documentary evidence, e.g. invoices received from transportation companies, was submitted although explicitly requested.

- (559) In its comments, Tatmetal first argued that there was no indication in the verification report that Tatmetal had failed to provide the necessary information or that the provided information could not have been verified.

- (560) Second, the company maintained that contrary to the Commission’s claim, the logistics employees were available via phone and would have made themselves available in person if the Commission requested so.

- (561) Third, the company claimed that there was no greater level of specific evidence other than the internal data reflected in the preparation worksheet for the reported domestic sales transactions that could corroborate the freight cost reported for domestic sales transactions.

- (562) Fourth, Tatmetal argued that the Commission’s expectation that the company should have provided additional corroborative evidence unsolicited and on its own initiative is unreasonable and in contravention of the Commission’s obligations under Article 6.1 of the WTO Agreement on the Implementation of Article VI of the General Agreement on Tariffs and Trade 1994 (‘WTO AD Agreement’).

- (563) Fifth, the company claimed that the Commission was provided with the masterfile for domestic sales transactions on the first day of the on-spot verification rather than on the last day as claimed by the Commission. Another preparation worksheet was submitted on the last day of the on-spot verification as that was the moment when the Commission requested it.

- (564) Finally, the company submitted that the unit freight cost data included in the preparation worksheet provided on the last day of the verification together with the shipping documents should be considered a reasonable method of providing sufficient evidence supporting the reported allowances for domestic freight.

- (565) Notwithstanding the above, Tatmetal submitted the internal record of freight cost and invoices issued for transportation services rendered in its comments to the Article 18 letter.

- (566) The Commission recalled that the verification report contains an account of the verification process. It does not provide any judgment on the level of cooperation by the verified company. The verification report correctly captured the explanations provided by the company with regard to the freight cost related to domestic sales transactions without making any conclusions concerning the adequacy of the provided evidence. Therefore, the fact that the verification report did not identify the deficiencies in the supporting evidence provided cannot be understood as the Commission’s acceptance of the data. Consequently, the Commission rejected the claim described in recital (559).

- (567) With regard to the timeliness of the provision of data used for the preparation of the questionnaire reply concerning the domestic sales transactions and related cost and the availability of the relevant personnel, it must be noted that the company’s general inability to provide explanations and to substantiate the information provided in other parts of the questionnaire reply caused delays in the verification process. Consequently, the Commission was only allowed to examine the domestic and export sales transactions on the very last day of the on-spot verification. Therefore, the Commission was forced to prioritise and was not able to inspect the provided information in such a level of detail as that which would normally be necessary. Therefore, any potential availability of the logistics staff to join the verification in person was irrelevant since the Commission was only informed about the method used to report domestic freight on the very last day of the on-spot verification. The company’s claim that a masterfile for domestic sales was provided on the first day of the verification is incorrect. The company indeed submitted several so-called masterfiles. With regard to sales, it provided a sales report that did not contain any information on freight expenses, i.e. it was not relevant to the issue at hand. Consequently, the arguments presented in recitals (560) and (563) were dismissed.

- (568) With regard to the company’s claim that shipping documents (documents proving that the goods were handed over to the carrier for transportation) and unsupported unit freight cost are appropriate method to corroborate the reported freight cost for domestic transactions, in particular as there was allegedly no more specific evidence available in the company, the Commission noted that the company itself submitted transport invoices in response to the second Article 18 letter. This clearly shows that the company was in possession of evidence that could corroborate the domestic freight cost. Therefore, the company’s claims detailed in recitals (561) and (564) were rejected.

- (569) With regard to the documentation submitted in response to the second Article 18 letter, the Commission rejected it as its veracity could not be verified at this stage of the procedure.

- (570) Finally, the Commission disagreed that it was in breach of Article 6.1 of the WTO AD Agreement when it expected the company to provide evidence corroborating the reported freight cost on its own initiative. The Commission requested documents for a sample of domestic sales transactions that would support the reported data, such as product type, customer, destination, relationship with the customer, sales quantity and price, cost incurred on the sales transaction. The Commission specifically requested documents supporting the reported freight cost. The company however failed to provide such documents although it was clearly in possession of transport invoices as explained in recitals (565) and (568). Consequently, the Commission rejected the claim described in recital (562).

###### 3.5.2.2.   Borçelik Çelik Sanayi Ticaret A.Ş., Bursa (‘Borcelik’)

- (571) Under point 10 of the Notice of initiation, the Commission reminded the interested parties that it they refused access to or did not provide necessary information within the time limits, or significantly impeded the investigation, the findings might be based on facts available in accordance with Article 18 of the basic Regulation. The Commission further reiterated the consequences of the lack of cooperation in the letter sent to the sampled exporting producers on 18 September 2025 on their inclusion in the sample.

- (572) On 17 June 2026, the Commission informed Borcelik that a number of issues arose during the investigation that might lead to the application of Article 18 of the basic Regulation.

- (573) First, the Commission requested the company to submit a trial balance four times, in the questionnaire, in the first and second deficiency letters, and at the beginning of the on-spot verification when it transpired that the version prepared by the company was missing certain data. The trial balance provided by the company following the fourth request was in a format that required further processing. The failure of the company to submit the requested trial balance in a proper format delayed the investigation.

- (574) Second, the company did not provide all information requested in table G – Profit & Loss. In particular, the company only decided to provide a breakdown of foreign exchange gains and losses into actual and translations during the on-spot verification. Considering the large number of accounts, on which foreign exchange gains and losses were recorded, the Commission could not satisfactorily verify the submitted information.

- (575) Third, despite being specifically requested to provide, well ahead of the on-spot verification, any corrections to minor errors the company noticed while preparing for the verification, the company only submitted such corrections on the first day of the verification. In addition, some of the corrections performed in the data on domestic sales could not be considered minor. As a result of those corrections, the freight expenses linked to individual domestic sales transactions increased by 250 %.

- (576) Fourth, the freight expenses linked to the domestic sales transactions were supported by evidence submitted by the company (reports on shipments and shipping cost extracted from the company’s records) for only a negligible share of the domestic sales representing less than 3 % of the relevant transactions.

- (577) Fifth, only during the on-spot verification, the company informed the Commission that it did not report freight expenses incurred for transport of the product under investigation between the factory and an external warehouse. The supporting evidence mentioned in recital (576) did not contain the necessary information either.

- (578) The company requested a hearing with the Commission services and submitted comments on the Commission’s intention to apply Article 18 of the basic Regulation with regard to the issues described in recitals (573) to (577).

- (579) During the hearing and in its comments, Borcelik contested the Commission’s intention to apply facts available under Article 18 of the basic Regulation and argued that Borcelik did not impede the course of the investigation.

- (580) With regard to the first point on trial balance, Borcelik claimed that the trial balance covering costs and revenues accounts for the whole investigation period was already provided in the initial questionnaire reply, and thus the Commission already had the information relevant for the determination of SG & A expenses on the record. Borcelik added that the trial balance for assets and liabilities accounts was provided in response to the first deficiency letter. After the second deficiency letter, a full trial balance covering assets, liabilities, costs and revenues was submitted, and the full trial balance was also provided during the onsite verifications.

- (581) The Commission noted that in its comments on the Article 18 letter, Borcelik merely recounted how the trial balance was submitted. The company’s account confirmed that the trial balance was submitted in parts, and that the Commission received a full version only during the onsite verification. In this respect, it must be noted that the reply to the second deficiency letter was due and submitted at the beginning of the verification visit. Therefore, the Commission considered that the factual account submitted by the company in its the comments on the Article 18 letter confirmed the Commission’s observations. In addition, nothing in the company’s comments contradicts the Commission’s conclusions that the full trial balance was received only very late in the proceeding, which undermined the analysis of the data submitted by the company. Consequently, the Commission confirmed its findings that the late submission of the full trial balance impeded the investigation.

- (582) With regard to the second point on the breakdown of foreign exchange gains and losses, Borcelik claimed that apart from the relevant table containing two separate lines for realised and translation gains and losses, the questionnaire itself did not explicitly require a breakdown of those expenses. In addition, the company maintained that neither Article 2(6) of the basic Regulation, Article 2.2.2 of the WTO Anti-dumping Agreement, nor the Union case law make a distinction between realised and translation foreign exchange gains and losses. In Borcelik’s view, a breakdown of foreign exchange gains and losses into realised and translations therefore did not constitute necessary information. Borcelik further argued that should the Commission consider the breakdown necessary information, it should have requested it in the deficiency letter. Finally, the company challenged the Commission’s conclusions that the late provision of the breakdown and the underlying evidence (account ledgers) impeded the investigation, as the Commission should have been aware of the large number of accounts on which foreign exchange gains and losses were booked from the initial questionnaire reply. Borcelik maintained that resort to facts available was unwarranted for this issue, as the account ledgers for foreign exchange gains and losses were verifiable during the verification visit and collected as verification exhibits.

- (583) The Commission noted that Borcelik did not dispute the fact that the information requested in the relevant table of the questionnaire, which is an integral part of the questionnaire, was not provided. The fact that the Commission does not focus on a specific deficiency in the deficiency letter issued to the company, does not invalidate the initial request to provide the respective information stemming from the content of the anti-dumping questionnaire. Furthermore, the fact that the company on its own initiative submitted a revision where it separated the two categories of foreign exchange gains and losses right before the verification of the relevant parts of the questionnaire reply confirmed that it was aware that the breakdown constituted necessary information. The Commission further considered that it had the discretion to verify the realised and translation foreign exchange gains and losses to ensure that these amounts were correctly attributed to SG & A expenses based on actual data pertaining to production and sales of the production under investigation. Finally, indeed the Commission was aware of the number of accounts on which foreign exchange gains and losses were booked. The breakdown provided by the company during the onsite verification was however disconnected from the titles of the relevant accounts. This means that any assumptions drawn from the information provided in the questionnaire reply would have been meaningless should the Commission rely on the account titles in its analysis. The Commission therefore confirmed its conclusion that the fact that Borcelik split the foreign exchange gains and losses into realised and translations only during the onsite verification caused delays in the verification schedule.

- (584) With regard to the third and fourth points on transport related expenses, Borcelik alleged that the Commission’s instructions on deadlines were conflicting, as the pre-verification letter suggested that changes submitted at the start of verification would not automatically trigger the use of Article 18 of the basic Regulation. Borcelik further claimed that the impact of these corrections was not that substantial and that the Commission misunderstood the information contained in data collected during the onsite verification, which according to the company contained only examples of the calculation of freight expenses for domestic sales. Finally, the company submitted that other error corrections that were reported at the same time were accepted by the Commission.

- (585) The Commission noted that Borcelik did not dispute the fact a revised version of domestic freight expenses was provided only at the start of the verification. The deadlines communicated to the company were clear. First, the Commission set a deadline for corrections of minor errors discovered during preparation for the onsite verification. The deadline for those corrections was set for a date preceding the beginning of the verification. The Commission also stated that any corrections provided after the beginning of the verification may give rise to the application of Article 18 of the basic Regulation. Second, the Commission indeed requested further information on domestic sales that were due at the beginning of the verification. The Commission however considered that this additional request did not constitute a new opportunity to provide further corrections. The Commission maintained its position concerning the severity of the corrections provided. They concerned around 60 % of domestic sales and the reported domestic sales expenses increased by 250 %. Other corrections were indeed accepted as they were minor and did not change the picture of the submitted information significantly. Finally, the Commission considered that there was no evidence on the file supporting the claim that the provided data on domestic freight expenses was a mere example. First, the company did not present it as an example during the onsite verification. Second, the collected extract from the company’s records contained information for all months of the investigation period and covered several transport companies. Therefore, it is not clear how such dataset can represent a mere sample of the full information on domestic freight expenses. Therefore, the company’s claim that the issue at hand did not warrant the use of facts available was rejected.

- (586) The company did not contest the Commission’s findings concerning the fifth point on the reporting of transport expenses for sales channels where external warehouses were involved.

##### 3.5.3.   *Normal value*

- (587) The Commission first examined whether the total volume of domestic sales for each sampled cooperating exporting producer was representative, in accordance with Article 2(2) of the basic Regulation. The domestic sales are representative if the total domestic sales volume of the like product to independent customers on the domestic market per exporting producer represented at least 5 % of its total export sales volume of the product concerned to the Union during the investigation period. On this basis, the total sales by each sampled exporting producer of the like product on the domestic market were representative.

- (588) The Commission subsequently identified the product types sold domestically that were identical or comparable with the product types sold for export to the Union for the exporting producers with representative domestic sales.

- (589) The Commission then examined whether the domestic sales by each sampled exporting producer on its domestic market for each product type that is identical or comparable with a product type sold for export to the Union were representative, in accordance with Article 2(2) of the basic Regulation. The domestic sales of a product type are representative if the total volume of domestic sales of that product type to independent customers during the investigation period represents at least 5 % of the total volume of export sales of the identical or comparable product type to the Union. The Commission established that for a small number of product types that were exported to the Union during the investigation period, there were either no domestic sales at all, or the domestic sales of that product type were below 5 % in volume and thus not representative.

- (590) The Commission next defined the proportion of profitable sales to independent customers on the domestic market for each product type during the investigation period in order to decide whether to use actual domestic sales for the calculation of the normal value, in accordance with Article 2(4) of the basic Regulation.

- (591) The normal value is based on the actual domestic price per product type, irrespective of whether those sales are profitable or not, if:

- (a) the sales volume of the product type, sold at a net sales price equal to or above the calculated cost of production, represented more than 80 % of the total sales volume of this product type; and

- (b) the weighted average sales price of that product type is equal to or higher than the unit cost of production.

- (592) In this case, the normal value is the weighted average of the prices of all domestic sales of that product type during the IP.

- (593) The normal value is the actual domestic price per product type of only the profitable domestic sales of the product types during the IP, if:

- (a) the volume of profitable sales of the product type represents 80 % or less of the total sales volume of this type; or

- (b) the weighted average price of this product type is below the unit cost of production.

- (594) The analysis of domestic sales showed that less than 30 % of all domestic sales of Tatmetal and less than 50 % of all domestic sales of Borcelik were profitable and that the weighted average sales price was higher than the cost of production. Accordingly, the normal value was calculated as a weighted average of the prices of all domestic sales during the investigation period or a weighted average of the profitable sales only.

- (595) For the product types for which sales were overall profitable, the normal value was calculated either as a weighted average of the prices of all domestic sales during the investigation period or as a weighted average of the profitable sales only depending on the volume of profitable sales.

- (596) Where there were insufficient sales of a product type of the like product in the ordinary course of trade or where a product type was not sold in representative quantities on the domestic market, the Commission constructed the normal value in accordance with Article 2(3) and (6) of the basic Regulation. For the product types for which there were no sales on the domestic market and where there was no specific information about market prices for those product types not sold by the sampled exporting producers on the domestic market, the Commission constructed the normal value in accordance with Article 2(3) and (6) of the basic Regulation.

- (597) Normal value was constructed by adding the following to the average cost of production of the like product of the cooperating sampled exporting producers during the investigation period:

- (a) the weighted average SG & A expenses incurred by the cooperating sampled exporting producers on domestic sales of the like product, in the ordinary course of trade, during the IP; and

- (b) the weighted average profit realised by the cooperating sampled exporting producers on domestic sales of the like product, in the ordinary course of trade, during the IP.

- (598) For the product types not sold in representative quantities on the domestic market, the average SG & A expenses and profit of transactions made in the ordinary course of trade on the domestic market for those types were added. As there was no other source of domestic sales price for the product types not sold at all on the domestic market, the normal value was constructed by adding weighted average SG & A expenses and profit of all transactions made in the ordinary course of trade on the domestic market to the cost of production of those product types.

- (599) Considering the Commission’s conclusions on the partial lack of cooperation by Borcelik, the Commission used facts available to estimate the following elements of the SG & A expenses:

- — Freight expenses incurred by the company for transport of the product under investigation between the factory and an external warehouse;

- — Total value of freight expenses that could not be allocated to individual domestic sales transactions due to the lack of supporting evidence.

- (600) With regard to the freight expenses, the Commission rejected the allocation of freight expenses per transaction. Instead, the Commission included the total reported value in the SG & A expenses used to determine the profitability of domestic sales.

- (601) In its comments on final disclosure, Borcelik commented on the Commission’s treatment of freight expenses. Since those comments also concern comparison between the normal value and the export price, they were addressed in section 3.5.5.

- (602) In its comments on final disclosure, Borcelik claimed that the Commission should revise its allocation of interest expenses for export-oriented loans to both domestic and export sales. According to Borcelik, the Commission’s rejection of its differentiation between export-oriented loans and other loans, based on the use of the loans and the lack of a mechanism to link them to exports, should be reversed. Borcelik argued that, since the mere existence of an export commitment was enough for the Commission to consider export-oriented loans as export subsidies in subsidy investigations [^ntr127-L_202602547EN.000101-E0127], the Commission should apply the same principle in this case to determine whether interest expenses incurred for such loans should be allocated to export sales only or to all sales. The company reiterated its claim in its comments on the additional final disclosure, despite the fact that the matter was not subject to the additional final disclosure and the company was instructed to only comment on the issues covered by the additional final disclosure.

- (603) The Commission examined the claim. The Commission emphasised that its decision to allocate interest expenses linked to export-oriented loans was based on the facts of the case. In the present investigation, the Commission assessed whether the expenses incurred from the export-oriented loans should be allocated solely to export sales. The on-spot verification showed that to be granted an export-oriented loan, the company must undertake an export commitment and that there is a system in place by which the bank follows up on the fulfilment of the commitment. There was however no evidence on the file proving that the funds acquired via an export-oriented loan had to be or were used purely to cover the cost associated with export sales, e.g. by directly linking the use of the loan to the procurement of raw materials which would have to be used only in the production of finished goods for exports. Nor did the company submit such evidence in its comments on final disclosure. Consequently, the claim was rejected.

- (604) In its comments on final disclosure, Borcelik claimed that the Commission double-counted R & D expenses. In this respect, the company reiterated the accounting treatment of R & D expenses. It also claimed that the income received from subsidies in connection with these R & D expenses should be taken into consideration.

- (605) The Commission examined the claim and in particular verified whether the explanations concerning the accounting treatment of R & D expenses were in line with the company’s trial balance. The Commission found that indeed, the amortisation of R & D expenses was included in the cost of sales and in the SG & A expenses. Thus, including the R & D expenses incurred during the investigation in the determination of the SG & A percentage would mean that the R & D expenses were considered twice. The Commission corrected its calculation and used the revised SG & A percentage in the calculation of the dumping margin.

- (606) In its comments on final disclosure, Borcelik pointed out that the Commission removed income from sales of scrap and income from late payments from SG & A expenses and included both items under total sales. When calculating the SG & A percentage, the Commission however used only the turnover generated by sales of the product under investigation and omitted to include the turnover generated by the two above-mentioned income items. Borcelik requested the Commission corrected this calculation error. The company reiterated its claim in its comments on the additional final disclosure and again on the second additional final disclosure, despite the fact that the matter was not subject to either additional final disclosure and the company was instructed to only comment on the issues covered by those additional final disclosures.

- (607) The Commission’s methodology was intentional and not a result of an error. The Commission requested the exporting producers directly in the questionnaire to report various types of income under the category of turnover instead of amongst the subcategories of SG & A expenses. In addition, the instructions showed that income other than from sales of the product under investigation (or other finished products for that matter) should not have been allocated to the total sales of each category of products. Therefore, income from sales of scrap and from late payments was not supposed to be used as the denominator when expressing the determined SG & A as a percentage. The company however ignored the above-mentioned instructions and reported the respective income categories under SG & A expenses. The Commission therefore removed the two income items from SG & A expenses and did not consider them when establishing the value of turnover used to determine the SG & A percentage. Consequently, the claim was rejected.

- (608) With regard to the freight expenses between the factory and the warehouse, the Commission used the information on the location of those warehouses and estimated the average unit freight cost based on the freight expenses reported by the company on a transaction-by-transaction basis. Such expenses were the best available information on the file for the estimation of the missing freight expenses, despite the Commission having rejected the allocation. Although the underlying data was flawed and overall, not entirely reliable, without estimating the cost of transport between the factory and the external warehouses, the SG & A expenses would have been undervalued.

- (609) As detailed in recitals (574) and (581), the Commission identified issues with the information on foreign exchange gains and losses provided by the company. Although the data was provided with a significant delay, the Commission concluded that it was detailed enough to establish a reliable value of realised foreign exchange gains and losses to be included in the SG & A expenses.

- (610) Borcelik sourced hot-rolled coils, the main raw material in the manufacturing of CRF, partially from a related supplier, AMFCE. Therefore, the Commission examined whether those purchases were made at arm’s length prices. The Commission compared the sales prices of AMFCE to unrelated customers for relevant steel grades with the sales prices to Borcelik at ex-works level. The Commission found that AMFCE sold hot-rolled coils of relevant steel grades to unrelated customers for prices higher than prices charged to Borcelik for comparable steel grades. Consequently, the Commission adjusted the cost of hot-rolled coils reported by Borcelik to an arm’s length level. This resulted in an increase of the cost of production of 1,8 %.

- (611) The adjusted cost of production was used for the profitability analysis of domestic sales described in recital (590), as well as for the construction of the normal value described in recitals (597) and (598).

- (612) In its comments on final disclosure, Borcelik claimed that it could not check the correctness of the calculation as the Commission did not provide any information how it arrived at this figure, other than the figure itself and an explanation why AMFCE allegedly refused the disclosure of this information. According to Borcelik, this would impede it to exercise its rights of defence and constitute a breach of Article 19(2) of the basic Regulation.

- (613) Borcelik also claimed that, despite having no means to check, other information on record on average purchase prices from the Arcelor Mitall Group would seem to contradict the Commission’s conclusion regarding its arm’s length analysis.

- (614) Finally, Borcelik claimed that average comparisons over a one-year (or longer) period might not be appropriate to determine whether transactions are under arm’s length conditions given significant fluctuations in raw material markets, and that the proper price comparison should be done comparing prices in the same market, that is, Türkiye. In this regard, Borcelik requested the Commission to clarify whether (1) the price comparison used for the arm’s length analysis was an average for the whole investigation period or not; (2) the location of AMFCE’s customers; and (3) sales volume of these customers.

- (615) The company reiterated the claims set out in recitals (612) to (614) in its comments on the additional final disclosure, despite the fact that these matters were not subject to the additional final disclosure and the company was instructed to only comment on the issues covered by the additional final disclosure.

- (616) The Commission explained in the company specific final disclosure the methodology used to assess whether Borcelik purchased HRC from AMFCE at market price. The Commission collected the full transactions listing concerning sales of HRC by AMFCE to all its customers during the remote cross-check. The Commission then identified steel grades that were sold to both, Borcelik and unrelated customers, in non-negligible quantities. The Commission compared the weighted average price of those steel grades when sold to unrelated customers and to Borcelik. Indeed, the Commission did not provide Borcelik with the underlying data and the resulting calculations. As already explained, this was due to AMFCE’s reservations concerning confidentiality. The limited cooperation of the group, Borcelik being related to AMFCE via a common shareholder, on this topic limited the Commission’s ability to disclose the full underlying data and the resulting calculations. The group cannot on one hand prevent access of one entity to the data of another entity and then use its own behaviour to claim that the Commission impeded its rights of defence.

- (617) With regard to the claim set out in recital (613), the Commission noted that the company used a completely irrelevant set of data for its analysis. It first classified the purchased HRC into three categories. Then Borcelik compared the prices paid to unrelated suppliers and to AMFCE. First, the Commission noted that the classification the company created did not allow to arrive at reasonable conclusions. The company indeed grouped 50 % of HRC grades purchased from AMFCE under the category ‘other’, which does not allow for a detailed analysis on a like-to-like basis. Second, to examine whether the prices paid to a related supplier were at arm’s length, the sales prices of the supplier must be examined, i.e. a comparison of purchase prices paid by Borcelik to unrelated suppliers and to AMFCE was for this purpose irrelevant.

- (618) With regard to Borcelik’s claims set out in recital (614), the Commission noted that the company did not provide any evidence of the alleged fluctuations in the prices of raw materials. In addition, data collected from Borcelik and AMFCE show that relevant purchases/sales were not concentrated in certain months or quarters but were evenly spread across the whole investigation period. Indeed, the Commission performed the analysis based on weighted averages for the whole investigation period, considering unrelated customers of AMFCE notwithstanding their location. The pool of unrelated customers contained customers to which AMFCE sold lower volumes than to Borcelik and also such to which it sold comparable or higher volumes. Therefore, the Commission considered that the analysis was not tainted by the quantities sold by AMFCE to individual customers.

- (619) Consequently, based on the considerations set out in recitals (616) to (618), Borcelik’s claims detailed in recitals (612) to (614) were rejected.

- (620) Considering the Commission’s conclusions on the partial lack of cooperation by Tatmetal, the Commission used facts available to estimate the total value of freight expenses of the SG & A cost that could not be allocated to individual domestic sales transactions due to the lack of supporting evidence.

- (621) Following the facts set out in recital (558), the Commission rejected the allocation of freight expenses per transaction in the domestic sales listing. Instead, the Commission included the total reported value in the SG & A expenses used to determine the profitability of domestic sales.

- (622) In its comments on final disclosure, Tatmetal requested the Commission to add, to the amount of total sales of the product under investigation on the domestic market to independent customers, the sales destined to the free trade zones in Türkiye in order to determine the profitability of the domestic sales including the sales to free trade zones.

- (623) The Commission analysed the claim and agreed to include the respective amount of sales to free trade zones to establish the profitability on the domestic market. The claim was accepted.

- (624) Furthermore, in its comments on final disclosure, Tatmetal submitted several comments on the determination of the profitability of domestic sales. Due to the sensitivity of the nature of the comments, challenging the adjustments on the level of individual accounts, the Commission responded to the comments in an individual disclosure to the company. The comments regarding exchange profits and exchange losses were partially accepted. The comments regarding extraordinary income and losses and revised scrap offset adjustment were rejected. The comment regarding the inflation adjustment was rejected and the underlying value was revised by the Commission.

- (625) Following additional final disclosure, Tatmetal opposed the Commission’s use of the first half of 2024 data pertaining to the inflation adjustment from the trial balance as a proxy for the first half of 2025. Tatmetal submitted that Türkiye’s General Circular On Tax Procedure Law Communiqué No. 582 [^ntr128-L_202602547EN.000101-E0128] mandated that no inflation adjustments shall be made in the first, second and third provisional tax periods of the 2025 accounting period. Tatmetal further submitted the Law No. 7571 [^ntr129-L_202602547EN.000101-E0129] confirming the provisions of the prior-mentioned Circular. Tatmetal requested the Commission to exclude the inflation adjustment of H1 2024.

- (626) The Commission found the claim justified and revised the dumping margin of Tatmetal accordingly.

- (627) Furthermore, Tatmetal reiterated its claim on the other revenues and the month-end exchange rate valuations, although the substance was not subject to the additional final disclosure, only the calculation formulas. The company also made a new comment, on the financial revenue in Table G-PL, even though the company was instructed to only comment on the issues covered by the additional final disclosure.

##### 3.5.4.   *Export price*

- (628) The sampled exporting producers exported to the Union either directly to independent customers or through related companies acting as an importer.

- (629) For the exporting producers that exported the product concerned directly to independent customers in the Union, the export price was the price actually paid or payable for the product concerned when sold for export to the Union, in accordance with Article 2(8) of the basic Regulation.

- (630) For the exporting producers that exported the product concerned to the Union through related companies acting as an importer, the export price was established on the basis of the price at which the imported product was first resold to independent customers in the Union, in accordance with Article 2(9) of the basic Regulation. In this case, adjustments to the price were made for all costs incurred between importation and resale, including SG & A expenses, and for profits accruing. The Commission relied on the actual SG & A costs reported by the related importers. With regard to the profit, given that no unrelated importers cooperated in this investigation, the Commission relied on the profit established for an unrelated importer in an anti-dumping investigation concerning imports of CRF originating in the PRC and the Russian Federation, namely 2 % [^ntr130-L_202602547EN.000101-E0130], which was also used in the recent *HRF* investigation [^ntr131-L_202602547EN.000101-E0131].

- (631) In its comments on final disclosure, Tatmetal submitted that the credit notes in the EU sales listing were already converted to TRY before the Commission carried out a conversion calculation and thus double-converted the credit notes values.

- (632) The Commission analysed the supporting evidence submitted by Tatmetal and noted that, contrary to the claim above, Tatmetal converted only one credit note to TRY in the EU sales listing. The currency of the remaining credit notes was indeed EUR, and the Commission applied the conversion formula to calculate the net invoice values. Furthermore, the Commission noted inconsistencies in the credit notes values reported in EUR in the additional evidence submitted and, as consequence, corrected the value of the credit notes in the EU sales listing. The argument was partially accepted.

- (633) Following additional final disclosure, Tatmetal disagreed with the Commission’s treatment of credit notes in the EU sales listing and referred to a billing adjustment document submitted within the questionnaire reply. Tatmetal requested that all credit notes reported are treated in TRY.

- (634) The Commission compared the credit notes numbers from the billing adjustment. The Commission matched the related sales invoices from the said document with all sales listing, which was collected during the verification visit, and concluded that the credit note values were reported in EUR, not TRY. The argument was rejected.

- (635) In its comments on final disclosure, Tatmetal submitted that the Commission made an error in the calculation of the CIF value for the transactions under FCA, FOB and CFR incoterms. Tatmetal requested the Commission to update the calculation formula.

- (636) The Commission analysed the claim and added the relevant amounts to establish the CIF value in the EU sales listing. The unit amounts of ocean freight and handling & loading expense were established following the reply to the Article 18 letter. The claim was accepted.

- (637) Following additional final disclosure, Tatmetal pointed out a clerical error in CIF values of a limited number of FCA transactions.

- (638) The Commission corrected the CIF values and the values of allowances of the impacted transactions. The change was negligible. The argument was accepted.

##### 3.5.5.   *Comparison*

- (639) Article 2(10) of the basic Regulation requires the Commission to make a fair comparison between the normal value and the export price at the same level of trade and to make allowances for differences in factors which affect prices and price comparability. In the case at hand the Commission chose to compare the normal value and the export price of the sampled exporting producers at the *ex-works* level of trade. As further explained below, where appropriate, the normal value and the export price were adjusted in order to: (i) net them back to the *ex-works* level; and (ii) make allowances for differences in factors which were claimed, and demonstrated, to affect prices and price comparability.

###### 3.5.5.1.   Adjustments made to the normal value

- (640) As explained in recital (639), the normal value was established at the ex-works level of trade by using costs of production together with amounts for SG & A expenses and for profit, which were considered to be reasonable for that level of trade. Adjustments were made under Article 2(10) for exchange rates, transport, insurance, handling, loading and ancillary costs, packing costs, discounts and rebates, credit cost and warranties and guaranties provided to independent domestic customers.

- (641) Both Turkish exporting producers claimed an adjustment for duty drawback under Article 2(10)(b) of the basic Regulation for the import duty on hot-rolled flat steel coils (HRC). Borcelik and Tatmetal claimed that the import duties need to be paid for imported materials and that the cost of production for domestic sales is higher than for export sales, which do not incur this additional cost of import duties.

- (642) In Türkiye, an import duty applies on HRC, which are inputs in the manufacture of cold-rolled flat steel products. However, under the inward processing regime (‘IPR’), domestic producers are exempted from the payment of such duty if the imported HRC are used to produce finished products that are finally exported. The two sampled exporters did not provide evidence of payment of the import duty during the investigation period that would warranty an adjustment for the purpose of fair comparison. On the contrary, the provided information showed that they fulfilled the export commitment linked to each IPR permit. For this reason, the claim was considered unfounded and therefore rejected.

- (643) In its comments on final disclosure, Borcelik pointed out that the Commission removed the commission paid to a related trader on the domestic market from the allowances reported with regard to the domestic sales to related and unrelated customers. The Commission however omitted to remove the very same commission reported as income from the resales of the related trader to domestic unrelated customers.

- (644) The Commission examined the claim and found that indeed, as a result of a clerical error, the commission received by the related trader was not removed from the allowances reported with regard to the resales of the domestic trader to unrelated customers on the domestic market. The Commission corrected the error.

- (645) In its comments on final disclosure, Borcelik claimed that the Commission had applied an inconsistent approach when it removed the reported credit costs from the domestic sales while, for Union sales, it disregarded the reported credit costs but still included a recalculated amount based on the payment terms reported. According to Borcelik, the same approach should be used for all sales as payment modalities were the same on both markets.

- (646) The Commission disagreed. The company claimed that it employed the same payment modalities for sales on the domestic and the Union markets. The investigation however showed that the payment methods used for the sales to the Union to a large extent did not make it possible to collect payments in the same way as on the domestic market. The Commission however found that for export sales via a specific domestic trader, the payment methods were similar to those used on the domestic market. Therefore, for the corresponding transactions, the Commission removed the credit cost allowance also from the sales to unrelated customers in the Union. The confidential details of the above analysis were disclosed to the company in the company-specific disclosure.

- (647) In its comments on additional final disclosure, Borcelik reiterated that the Commission used an inconsistent approach when it rejected the credit cost for domestic sales and accepted it for the sales to the Union, with the exception of sales via an unrelated domestic trader. While the company agreed that the contracted payment terms influence the price setting, it challenged the Commission’s treatment of the reported credit cost based on the payment modalities, the manner in which the invoices are ultimately paid.

- (648) The Commission clarified that indeed certain payment modalities, in particular if applied consistently and known when the payment term is contractually agreed, may disqualify the effect of the provided commercial credit on the level of the sales price. For example, the Commission considered that, where recurring customers maintain an open account with a supplier and settle their outstanding debts through occasional lump-sum payments rather than paying each invoice in full by its individual due date, the contractually agreed payment term does not inform the supplier’s price setting. Consequently, the claim was rejected.

- (649) In its comments on final disclosure, Borcelik challenged the Commission’s treatment of transport-related expenses covering direct transportation expenses and transport expenses between Borçelik’s factory and (unrelated) warehouses. Concretely, Borcelik claimed that direct transportation expenses should be deducted as an allowance from the domestic sales price and should be removed from the SG & A costs used for the ordinary course of trade test.

- (650) Borcelik also claimed that the Commission’s inclusion, in the domestic sales, of the freight cost from the plant to external warehouses was inconsistent with its past practice, where, at least for domestic sales, transport costs to a warehouse (regardless of whether it is owned by a related or an unrelated party) could not be deducted as an allowance under Article 2(10)(e) basic Regulation [^ntr132-L_202602547EN.000101-E0132]. According to Borcelik, this would also mean that the Commission could not have applied facts available regarding these expenses, as they did not have to be reported in the first place.

- (651) Borcelik also claimed that the freight to rented warehouses should not be added to the SG & A expenses as a transport related expense.

- (652) The company reiterated the claims set out in recitals (649) to (651) in its comments on the additional final disclosure, despite the fact that these matters were not subject to the additional final disclosure and the company was instructed to only comment on the issues covered by the additional final disclosure.

- (653) With regard to the accounting treatment of the expenses incurred for freight between the factory and rented warehouses, the Commission first noted that the account code under which the expenses were allegedly recorded was reported by the company neither in the submitted chart of accounts nor in the trial balance. As discussed in recitals (573), (580) and (581), the company failed to provide a complete trial balance even after several requests. Following the comments by the company on final disclosure, it appears that the version finally received during the on-spot verification had still omitted some accounts. In addition, the Commission examined the report from the company’s cost accounting and found that even if the freight expenses were not treated as SG & A expenses by the company, they were not recorded in the cost of production either. Therefore, the Commission confirmed that to capture all cost that contributed to the full cost related to the production and sales of the product under investigation, the inclusion of the freight cost under SG & A expenses was an appropriate approach to reflect the cost for the ordinary course of trade test. Whilst internal transport costs (between various warehouses of the producer) may not be deduced when netting back either the export price or the normal value to the ex-works level of trade (since they belong to that level of trade), as long as they relate to the domestic sales, they are still costs ‘pertaining to production and sales, in the ordinary course of trade, of the like product by the exporter or producer under investigation’ within the meaning of Article 2(6) of the basic Regulation. Consequently, the claims set out in recitals (649) to (651) were rejected.

- (654) Finally, Borcelik and Tatmetal claimed that the Commission violated the obligation under Article 2(10) basic Regulation to carry out a fair comparison at the same level of trade because adding transportation expenses in the SG & A costs and thus in the normal value led to a normal value not at ex-works level, while the export price did not include such expenses and was consequently at ex-works level. Tatmetal suggested that the Commission reintroduced the domestic freight cost in the domestic sales listing and maintained the SG & A cost at ex-works level.

- (655) In its comments on final disclosure, Tatmetal also claimed that if the Commission maintained that the domestic freight values calculated by Tatmetal were inappropriate to use, it should not allocate the sum of those values to the domestic freight cost in the SG & A construction. Tatmetal requested the Commission to disregard entirely Tatmetal’s domestic freight calculations and allocate the total domestic transport figure, which was determined from Tatmetal’s trial balance, on a turnover basis in Table G-Profit and Loss.

- (656) The Commission examined Borcelik’s claim set out in recital (654). Indeed, for a small number of transactions the freight cost was sufficiently supported by evidence. The delivery terms applicable to individual transactions were also properly verified. Therefore, the Commission identified the lowest actual unit freight cost based on the verified information and applied this freight cost to all transactions where the goods were delivered to the customer. The Commission considered that since this adjustment was carried out under Article 18 of the basic Regulation, using the lowest unit freight cost ensured that the company’s dumping behaviour was not underestimated as a result of its non-cooperation on this issue. The details of this revision were disclosed to the company in a company-specific disclosure.

- (657) Following additional final disclosure, Borcelik referred to the provisions of Article 18(3) of the basic Regulation and argued that since the Commission had already used the information submitted in an annex to the questionnaire reply and in a verification exhibit, it should have used it in a such a way to establish a more accurate unit freight cost. In this respect, Borcelik submitted that the unit freight cost used by the Commission was too low. The company pointed out that the simple average unit freight cost available in the annex to the questionnaire reply or in the verification exhibit was much higher. The company further highlighted the range of unit freight cost submitted in the annex to the questionnaire reply.

- (658) In addition, the company pointed out that by allocating only a portion of the incurred transport expenses and including the residual value in SG & A expenses used in the ordinary course of trade test, the resulting normal value is not at ex-works level.

- (659) The Commission noted that the company failed to explain why a simple average established on the information from a verification exhibit that had already been found significantly deficient, namely it provided transportation expenses for a negligible share of all domestic sales transactions, should be more appropriate than the methodology applied by the Commission. First, as explained in recital (656), the Commission chose the lowest unit freight cost to ensure that the company’s dumping behaviour was not underestimated in a situation of its non-cooperation on this matter. Second, considering how incomplete the shipment report submitted in the verification exhibit was (covering only less than 3 % of the relevant domestic sales transactions, see also recital (576)), a simple average unit cost could not be considered more appropriate or representative of the actual freight cost than the methodology used by the Commission. The annex to the questionnaire reply contained a price list of transportation services and was indeed used to estimate the freight cost between the factory and a warehouse (for details, see recitals (577), (599), (608), and (649) to (653)) as the best available proxy. This annex was however not directly verified as during the on-spot verification, the company explained that it used the shipment report collected as the above-mentioned verification exhibit. The unit freight prices reported in the price list started at a price higher than the lowest unit freight cost identified by the Commission in the shipment report. This indicates that the annex itself might have been incomplete or did not reflect the actually paid prices for transportation services. The Commission rejected this claim.

- (660) With regard to the claim that the normal value was not at ex-works level, the Commission found it justified and revised the dumping margin accordingly.

- (661) With regard to Tatmetal, the Commission reiterated that no evidence was submitted to confirm the domestic freight expenses on a transaction-by-transaction basis. The Commission further observed that the delivery address/pick-up was not recorded in the accounting system to trace the destination of the domestic sales reported as being delivered to the client. Nevertheless, the Commission considered that since this adjustment was carried out under Article 18 of the basic Regulation, using the lowest reasonable unit freight cost, in this case Kocaeli area, was and adequate proxy for freight expenses) to establish the normal value at the ex-works level of trade. The argument was partially accepted.

- (662) Following additional final disclosure, Tatmetal submitted that by allocating only a portion of the incurred transport expenses and including the residual value in SG & A expenses used in the ordinary course of trade test, the resulting normal value is not at ex-works level.

- (663) The Commission found the claim justified and revised the dumping margin accordingly.

- (664) Following additional final disclosure, Tatmetal submitted that an error occurred in the allocation of freight costs pertaining to domestic sales delivered to customers. Tatmetal requested that the Commission rectify the error.

- (665) Upon analysis of Tatmetal's claim, the Commission revised the data pertaining to domestic sales and updated Table G-PL accordingly.

###### 3.5.5.2.   Adjustments made to the export price

- (666) In order to net the export price back to the ex-works level of trade, adjustments were made on account of freight, insurance, handling loading and ancillary expenses.

- (667) Allowances were made for the following factors affecting prices and price comparability: packing cost, credit cost and bank charges.

- (668) In its comments on final disclosure, Borcelik submitted that the Commission incorrectly revised the interest rate used to determine the credit cost for resales of the product under investigation by Bamesa Otel to unrelated customers in the Union.

- (669) The Commission examined the claim. Indeed, when revising the interest rate as agreed with the company, the Commission made a clerical error. The Commission corrected the error and revised the level of credit cost in the dumping margin calculation.

- (670) With regards to Tatmetal, due to the significant inconsistencies in the FX reported in the domestic sales listing and the EU sales listing, as described in recitals (551) to (553), the Commission applied facts available. The Commission replaced the reported daily foreign exchange rates with the monthly exchange rates provided for in the anti-dumping questionnaire for exporting producers.

- (671) In its comments on final disclosure, Tatmetal submitted that when applying corrections to the EU sales listing, the Commission mistakenly attributed ocean freight to certain FOB and FCA transactions.

- (672) The Commission analysed the attribution of the ocean freight on transaction-by-transaction basis and corrected the mistake of allocating an ocean freight to certain FOB and FCA transactions. The argument was accepted.

###### 3.5.5.3.   Currency conversions

- (673) Domestic sales were carried out in various currencies, including TRY, EUR, USD. Exports to the Union were performed in EUR or USD. Therefore, a currency conversion was necessary to compare the normal value with the export price. The Commission converted the sales prices to TRY, the accounting currency of the sampled exporting producers.

- (674) To determine which date should be considered the date of sale pursuant to Article 2(10)(j) of the basic Regulation, the Commission examined whether the material terms of sale were established based on the invoice or the purchase order/sales contract. The Commission found that a vast majority of sales were based on individual purchase orders rather than long-term sales contracts. The purchase order defined the material terms of sale, such as the product specifications, quantity, time of delivery, unit price (normally denominated in a foreign currency, mainly USD), payment term. The purchase order allowed for a tolerance in the quantity delivered, the price was however definitely set in the purchase order. The evidence collected from the sampled exporting producers confirmed that prices charged in the invoice were not adjusted to reflect the fluctuations of CRF prices or the fluctuations of the exchange rates between the currency of the purchase order and the accounting currency of the sampled exporting producers. Therefore, the Commission found that the purchase order more appropriately established the material terms of sale and thus its date should be used for currency conversions under Article 2(10)(j) of the basic Regulation.

- (675) In their comments on final disclosure, both Borcelik and Tatmetal claimed that the material terms of sale were not set at the time of the purchase order. According to them, the elements in such orders were not definitive at the time the orders were set and were only definitively established at the time of invoicing. Both companies supported the claim referring to the evidence on record, where the verified sales by the Commission confirmed the existence of differences between the purchase order and the invoices, differences that went in some instances beyond the tolerances agreed in the purchase order.

- (676) The Commission noted from the judgement of the General Court in *Özkan Demir* [^ntr133-L_202602547EN.000101-E0133] that it is for the Commission to establish why the date of purchase order more appropriately establishes the material terms of sale. The Commission noted that it is apparent from Article 2(10)(j) of the basic Regulation, read in conjunction with the introductory part of Article 2(10) thereof referring to the fair comparison at the same level of trade between the export price and the normal value, that the term ‘material’ refers to the trading terms of the sale as opposed to legal terms. Such trading terms are, in particular, the price, quantity, methods of payment and delivery, or other commercial characteristics of a transaction specific to a particular economic activity or type of production.

- (677) The Commission observed that the Turkish lira depreciated overall by comparison with the euro by approximately 33 % during the investigation period and that the time lag between the date of the purchase order and the invoice date of Borcelik and Tatmetal was varied greatly and reached significant lengths: between 0 days and 130 days for domestic sales and between 0 days and 182 days for export sales while both companies expressed their domestic and export sales in foreign currencies, thus anticipating strong variations in the valuation of the Turkish lira compared to foreign currencies.

- (678) With regard to the evidence provided by Borcelik, i.e. the supporting documents for additional transactions with different prices at the time of the purchase order and at the time of the invoice, the Commission found that it in fact did not support the company’s claims. The Commission collected supporting documents for 47 invoices covering CRF sales to unrelated customers on the domestic market and to both, related and unrelated customers, in the Union. These invoices normally covered a number of individual transactions (in the present investigation, normally each coil represented a separate transaction). In those documents, the company found only one instance where the delivered quantity was outside of the agreed tolerance. The Commission considered that this cannot break the observed pattern of material terms of sale being set in the purchase order, in particular as there might have been other reasons for the change in quantity, such as partial deliveries or backorder. The company also claimed that with regard to four invoices concerning sales to the Union, the price changed between the purchase order and the invoice and, therefore, the material terms of sale changed as well. The Commission observed that in all four cases, the goods were sold to the Union via an unrelated domestic trader. The company submitted as evidence price offers but not the actual purchase orders. This was confirmed by the date on the price offers and the date of the purchase orders recorded in the company’s accounting records. In all four cases, the offered price was at ex-works level, but the sales transaction was carried out at FOB delivery term. It is therefore likely that the price difference, which was always the same for all four invoices, represented the additional freight, handling and loading expenses incurred by Borcelik.

- (679) Consequently, based on the considerations detailed in recitals (676) to (678), Borcelik’s claim set out in recital (675) was rejected.

- (680) With regard to the evidence submitted by Tatmetal, this consisted of two transactions, sampled by the Commission during the verification, that would according to the company invalidate the reasoning of the Commission. This because there was an observed change in quantities, sales prices and, in one case, a change of payment terms between the purchase order/sales contract and the invoice.

- (681) In the verification, the Commission sampled 10 domestic sales transactions and 14 EU export sales transactions. The Commission observed that the quantity difference quoted by Tatmetal for the two sampled transactions was within the quantity and weight tolerances agreed in and thus set by the purchase order. The Commission further observed that in one of the two quoted transactions the price difference was minimal (less than 0,1 %), which cannot be considered as a breach of the terms of sale included in the purchase order. The existence of a departure from the conditions set out in the purchase order was established only in one purchase order out of 24 examined by the Commission. The Commission considered that the magnitude of the departure from the terms of sale established in the purchase orders was minimal and this one instance of non-compliance was an exception, which could not invalidate the arguments put forward by the Commission that the the material terms of sale were set at the time of the purchase order. The argument was rejected.

- (682) In its comments on final disclosure, Tatmetal alleged that if the Commission applied the exchange rate at the time of the purchase order to convert the invoice value, then the same exchange rate should have been applied to convert direct selling expenses, such as freight, demurrage, commission and handling and loading expense in the EU sales listing. Tatmetal claimed that the price of direct selling expenses in other currencies was set in advance and the Commission should apply the average delay between the time of the purchase order/sales contract and the invoice calculated by Tatmetal. Tatmetal submitted supporting evidence to support its claim.

- (683) The Commission clarified that, in its calculations, it applied the foreign exchange rate used at the date of the invoice for those expenses. The Commission reminded that the direct selling expenses, such as domestic or export freight expenses, were recorded in the company’s accounting at the time of the invoice and were reported in the domestic sales table and the EU sales table based on the exchange rate of the invoice. The Commission therefore accepted the values converted to TRY reported by Tatmetal. The Commission noted that the use of the purchase order date related to an adjustment for currency conversion as per Article 2(10)(j) of the basic Regulation whereas adjustments pertaining to transport, insurance, handling, loading and ancillary costs are governed by Article 2(10)(e) of the basic Regulation whereby the cost incurred shall be used, as the Commission did.

- (684) Notwithstanding the above, the Commission analysed the evidence based on which Tatmetal established the average delay between the exchange rate at the purchase order date and the exchange rate at the invoice date and noted that among the three submitted evidence files for the adjustment of the exchange rate for the allowances in the EU sales listing, one evidence file was an email exchange between Tatmetal and the transport provider that happened on one particular day. Tatmetal linked this email exchange to an export sale transaction, but it was not possible to cross-reference the products discussed in the email exchange to any export sales transactions. Furthermore, the email correspondence contains price offers, but does not disclose the characteristics of the purchase order or sales contract terms. For instance, there was no clear mention of whether the product was CRF, no terms of sale, no negotiated quantities, etc. The second evidence contained an email exchange on price offers with another transport provider and a related invoice for the transport service. However, the Commission noted that the invoice was issued for galvanised steel coils and did not appear to be related to CRF. The third evidence was an email exchange regarding ocean freight and while some terms of sales were listed in the email, there were no details that could link the email to the transactions in the EU sales listing. The claim was rejected.

- (685) Both Borcelik and Tatmetal further claimed that, in case the Commission still considered that the material terms of sale were set at the time of the purchase order, the same principle should apply to their purchases of raw materials. Consequently, the Commission should carry out the currency conversions for those purchases based on the date of the purchase order, and not the invoice date. In this respect, Borcelik pointed out that the General Court confirmed in Ereğli Demir ve Çelik Fabrikaları TAŞ and Others v European Commission the obligations/conditions of Article 2(10)(j) of the basic Regulation regarding currency conversions equally apply to export sales, domestic sales and cost of production [^ntr134-L_202602547EN.000101-E0134].

- (686) In this respect, the Commission noted that Article 2(10)(j) of the basic Regulation is applicable in situations ‘[w]here the comparison requires a conversion of currencies’. The costs of Borcelik and Tatmetal were recorded in their accounts, reported to the Commission and verified in TRY. Since the dumping calculation was for the two exporting producers was conducted in TRY, the comparison between the normal value and the export price did not require a conversion of the currency of these costs and thus Article 2(10)(j) did not apply. The claim was therefore rejected.

- (687) Borcelik, in addition, referred to the supporting documents regarding purchases of raw materials submitted during the on-spot verification. On the basis of those documents, the company calculated an average lead time between a purchase order and the invoice and requested that that lead time was used to estimate the purchase order date for the other purchases of raw materials.

- (688) As explained in recital (686), the comparison in the present case did not require currency conversions with regard to the cost of raw materials. Nevertheless, for the sake of completeness, the Commission analysed the supporting documents and observed that the collected documents covered only less than 0,1 % of the purchased raw materials. In addition, the purchase order dates and invoice dates used in the calculation did not correspond to the submitted documents. The company used the ‘date of last change’ recorded in its accounting system as the date of the purchase order although the purchase orders submitted with the supporting documents showed different dates. At the same time, the company did not use the date of the commercial invoice to determine the lead time. Instead, it used what appears to be the date of the customs clearance.
