To sum up, even if there was a need for currency conversion with regard to the cost of production *quod non*, the company did not provide consistent and reliable information that would allow for such conversions to be carried out. Consequently, the company’s request to revise the value of raw materials using the foreign exchange rate on the date of the purchase order based on the information provided by the company was rejected.

- (689) Similarly, in response to the final disclosure, Tatmetal provided information to be used for the requested conversion of raw material prices using the exchange rate on the date of the purchase order. In this respect, the company submitted the purchase listing of HRC with manually inserted dates of purchase orders.

- (690) As explained previously (see recital (686)), the Commission found that the comparison did not require currency conversions concerning the cost of raw materials in the present case. In any event, Tatmetal did not bring forward any evidence to demonstrate the correctness of the purchase order dates, nor of conditions laid out in the purchase orders/sales contracts of the purchases of raw materials. The Commission further noted that Tatmetal did not provide any reference to a legal basis, on basis of which such adjustment should be warranted. The argument was rejected.

- (691) In its comments on final disclosure, Borcelik claimed that the Commission did not meet its burden of proof for the currency conversion adjustment it made pursuant to Article 2(10)(j) of the basic Regulation because it did not explain how the currency conversions affected prices and price comparability.

- (692) The Commission considered that the purpose of Article 2(10)(j) of the basic Regulation combines elements of practical necessity of currency conversions where the transactions are denominated in various currencies and fairness of comparison, for example when it directs which exchange rate shall be used in cases where there are currency forwards directly linked to export sales. With regard to the practical necessity of currency conversions Article 2(10)(j) of the basic Regulation provides guidance concerning which date should be used to determine the exchange rate: it shall be the date of sale, which is normally the invoice date unless the material terms of sale are established on another date. In the present case, the Commission found that the material terms of sale were established in the purchase order as explained in recital (674). Therefore, it decided that it was more appropriate to use the exchange rate of the date of the purchase order to convert the invoice value denominated in a foreign currency into TRY.

- (693) In its comments on final disclosure, Borcelik claimed that the Commission adopted a discriminatory approach by applying currency conversions based on the order date only to Türkiye, without explaining why the situation of Turkish exporting producers differs from that of exporting producers in other countries. Product specifications, quantities, delivery conditions, unit prices and payment terms may already be agreed to a significant extent on the order in all countries concerned. It further claimed that to be the Commission’s own position regarding sales in the steel industry [^ntr135-L_202602547EN.000101-E0135].

- (694) Türkiye has experienced a prolonged period of continuous high inflation (in some years even hyperinflation) that translated in the depreciation of Turkish lira as mentioned in recital (677). Such situation was not observed in any of the other countries subject to this investigation. Consequently, the Commission rejected the claim of discrimination between Türkiye on one hand and the other countries concerned on the other.

- (695) Borcelik reiterated its claims concerning currency conversions in its comments on the additional final disclosure, despite the fact that the matter was not subject to the additional final disclosure and the company was instructed to only comment on the issues covered by the additional final disclosure.

##### 3.5.6.   *Dumping margins*

- (696) For the sampled cooperating exporting producers, the Commission compared the weighted average normal value of each type of the like product with the weighted average export price of the corresponding type of the product concerned, in accordance with Article 2(11) and (12) of the basic Regulation.

- (697) On this basis, the definitive weighted average dumping margins expressed as a percentage of the CIF Union frontier price, duty unpaid, are as follows:

| Company | Definitive dumping margin (%) |
| --- | --- |
| Borçelik Çelik Sanayi Ticaret A.Ş. | 7,8 |
| Tatmetal Çelik Sanayi ve Ticaret A. Ş. | 3,0 |

- (698) For the cooperating exporting producers outside the sample, the Commission calculated the weighted average dumping margin, in accordance with Article 9(6) of the basic Regulation. Therefore, that margin was established on the basis of the margins of the sampled exporting producers, disregarding the margins of the exporting producers with zero and *de minimis* dumping margins, as well as margins established in the circumstances referred to in Article 18 of the basic Regulation.

- (699) Given the factual circumstances of the case, notably that non-cooperation was only established at a late stage of the procedure and the Commission only partially relied on facts available to establish both margins, the Commission considered that Article 9(6) still applied despite having established the margins of both sampled exporting producers in the circumstances referred to in Article 18 of the basic Regulation. Consequently, the Commission found it appropriate to rely on those margins to set the dumping margin for the non-sampled cooperating exporting producers. On this basis, the definitive dumping margin of the cooperating exporting producers outside the sample is 4,9 %.

- (700) For all other exporting producers in Türkiye, the Commission established the dumping margin on the basis of the facts available, in accordance with Article 18 of the basic Regulation. To this end, the Commission determined the level of cooperation of the exporting producers. The level of cooperation is the volume of exports of the cooperating exporting producers to the Union expressed as proportion of the total imports from the country concerned to the Union in the IP, that were established on the basis of Eurostat.

- (701) The level of cooperation in this case is high because the exports of the cooperating exporting producers constituted 100 % of the total imports during the investigation period. On this basis, the Commission decided to establish the dumping margin for non-cooperating exporting producers at the level of the sampled company with the highest dumping margin.

- (702) The definitive dumping margins, expressed as a percentage of the CIF Union frontier price, duty unpaid, are as follows:

| Company | Definitive dumping margin (%) |
| --- | --- |
| Borçelik Çelik Sanayi Ticaret A.Ş. | 7,8 |
| Tatmetal Çelik Sanayi ve Ticaret A. Ş. | 3,0 |
| Other cooperating companies | 4,9 |
| All other imports originating in country concerned | 7,8 |

#### 3.6.   **Vietnam**

##### 3.6.1.   *Particular market situation: push-out effect*

###### 3.6.1.1.   Analysis

- (703) In order to establish the existence of a PMS in the market of Vietnam due to the push-out effect of Chinese imports of CRF, the Commission carried out the following analysis: first, the Commission analysed the degree of penetration of Chinese imports in Vietnam’s domestic market in terms of volume; secondly, the Commission looked at the impact of these imports on the prices in Vietnam’s domestic market.

- (704) In its analysis, the Commission relied on indicators at country-wide level provided by the authorities of Vietnam, and at company level provided by the sampled group of exporting producers, POSVIET. Both sets of indicators were verified on spot, as indicated in recital (106).

###### 3.6.1.1.1.   Domestic market: volume effect

- (705) At the outset, the Commission analysed the consumption of CRF in Vietnam as reported by the authorities of Vietnam on the basis of domestic sales and imports.

- (706) Consumption in Vietnam developed as follows:

- (707) Between 2022 and 2024, the most recent year with available data, the consumption in Vietnam increased by 23 percentage points. The increase in consumption of product under investigation was driven by several laws, resolutions, and policy measures adopted by the GOV aimed at promoting public investment, particularly in infrastructure and construction, during the period 2024–2025 [^ntr137-L_202602547EN.000101-E0137].

- (709) Import data show that the imports of the product under investigation into Vietnam increased by 54 % between 2022 and the IP. This increase of imports reflected the increase in domestic consumption, stimulated by the Vietnamese government plans to increase infrastructure investments in 2024 and 2025.

- (710) Out of these imports, the import volumes of CRF from the PRC increased by 6 % between 2022 and the IP. In the total volumes of imports, the share of imports from the PRC decreased from 51 % in 2022 to 46 % in the IP. The PRC nevertheless remained the first source of imports of CRF into Vietnam throughout the period considered.

- (714) In terms of market share, the market share of the domestic industry remained above 85 % in all years between 2022 and the IP. The market share of imports also remained stable at [10 %-12 %] between 2022 and the IP. The market share of Chinese import decreased slightly, from [5 %-7 %] in 2022 to [4 %-6 %] in 2024.

- (715) Both the increase of total import volumes described in recital (708) and the increase of domestic sales described in recital (711) mirrored in the increase of domestic consumption described in recital (706), and as a consequence the market shares of both remained at roughly the same levels. Given the volumes, it follows that it was mainly the Vietnamese industry that benefitted from the increasing consumption.

- (716) In view of the above, the Commission concluded that the Vietnamese domestic industry had a dominant position in the Vietnamese market from 2022 to the IP, with no other country with a market share above 6 %.

###### 3.6.1.1.2.   Domestic market: price effect

- (717) The Commission subsequently analysed the effect of Chinese imports on prices in Vietnam’s domestic market. In order to do that and given that the market share of countries other than China was small, the Commission analysed the unit prices of imports from China and of domestic sales.

- (718) The unit price of imports and domestic sales developed as follows:

- (719) The price analysis showed that throughout the period considered, the average Chinese import price was constantly significantly above the average Vietnamese domestic price, with both following a similar downward trend.

###### 3.6.1.2.   Conclusion on push-put effect

- (720) Based on the above, the limited penetration of Chinese imports into Vietnam in the period examined had no downward effect on the domestic prices. Indeed, the Vietnamese industry had most of the Vietnamese market share from 2022 to the IP, while the market share of Chinese imports was insignificant in comparison. Chinese import prices were consistently above Vietnamese domestic prices. Consequently, the Commission concluded that Chinese imports did not create a PMS in the Vietnamese market.

##### 3.6.2.   *Particular market situation: other claims*

- (721) The Commission also investigated whether the measures identified in the complaint and that allegedly distorted the cost of production of CRF in Vietnam led a particular market situation in the sense of Article 2(3) of the basic Regulation. These measures were:

- (a) Export restrictions applicable to iron ore, steel scrap and coal;

- (b) Presence of vertically integrated producers on the market;

- (c) Significant production of CRF from hot-rolled products originating in the PRC; and

- (d) Other regulatory initiatives.

- (722) With regard to points (a) on export restrictions applicable to iron ore, steel scrap and coal, (b) on vertical integration of producers, and point (c) on significant production of CRF from HRC originating in the PRC, the investigation established that POSVIET produced the product under investigation exclusively from hot rolled coils and that the majority of those coils [^ntr142-L_202602547EN.000101-E0142] were sourced from Korea. POSVIET therefore was neither integrated, nor used iron ore, steel scrap and coal from Vietnam nor hot-rolled products originating in the PRC.

- (723) With regard to point (d) Other regulatory initiatives, the Commission noted that the Steel Industry Development Strategy to 2030 with a vision to 2050 was terminated by the Vietnamese government and that the Directive 494/CT-TTg dated 20 April 2010, referred to in the Complaint on the use of domestically produced supplies and commodities was in fact binding to public entities, such as ministries, ministerial-level agencies and government-attached agencies. The Directive did not apply to private entities and in any case, those regulatory initiatives could not have affected POSVIET as explained in recital (722).

- (724) Consequently, none of these measures affected POSVIET and, since it made [80 %-100 %] of the exports of the product under investigation to the Union, the Commission concluded that the provisions of Article 2(3) on PMS could not be applied to POSVIET or to Vietnam either.

##### 3.6.3.   *Normal value*

- (725) The Commission first examined whether the total volume of domestic sales of the sampled cooperating exporting producer was representative, in accordance with Article 2(2) of the basic Regulation. The domestic sales are representative if the total domestic sales volume of the like product to independent customers on the domestic market per exporting producer represented at least 5 % of its total export sales volume of the product concerned to the Union during the investigation period. On this basis, the total sales of the sampled exporting producer of the like product on the domestic market were representative.

- (726) The Commission subsequently identified the product types sold domestically that were identical or comparable with the product types sold for export to the Union.

- (727) The Commission then examined whether the domestic sales of the sampled exporting producer on its domestic market for each product type that is identical or comparable with a product type sold for export to the Union were representative, in accordance with Article 2(2) of the basic Regulation. The domestic sales of a product type are representative if the total volume of domestic sales of that product type to independent customers during the investigation period represents at least 5 % of the total volume of export sales of the identical or comparable product type to the Union. The Commission established that for several product types representing less than 0,5 % of total volume of export sales to the Union there were no domestic sales of identical or comparable product type.

- (728) As there were no sales of certain product types of the like product in the ordinary course of trade on the domestic market, the Commission constructed the normal value in accordance with Article 2(3) and (6) of the basic Regulation.

- (729) The Commission next defined the proportion of profitable sales to independent customers on the domestic market for each product type during the investigation period in order to decide whether to use actual domestic sales for the calculation of the normal value, in accordance with Article 2(4) of the basic Regulation.

- (730) The normal value is based on the actual domestic price per product type, irrespective of whether those sales are profitable or not, if:

- (a) the sales volume of the product type, sold at a net sales price equal to or above the calculated cost of production, represented more than 80 % of the total sales volume of this product type; and

- (b) the weighted average sales price of that product type is equal to or higher than the unit cost of production.

- (731) If both tests are met, the normal value is the weighted average of the prices of all domestic sales of that product type during the IP.

- (732) The normal value is the actual domestic price per product type of only the profitable domestic sales of the product types during the IP, if:

- (a) the volume of profitable sales of the product type represents 80 % or less of the total sales volume of this type; or

- (b) the weighted average price of this product type is below the unit cost of production.

- (733) The analysis of domestic sales showed that approximately 27 % of all domestic sales were profitable and that the weighted average sales price was higher than the cost of production. Of those, product types for which profitable sales exceeded 80 % represented approximately 7 % of all domestic sales. Accordingly, the normal value was calculated as a weighted average of the prices of all domestic sales during the investigation period for those product types. In addition, product types for which profitable sales were equal to or lower than 80 % of total domestic sales of those product types represented 20 % of all domestic sales. Consequently, the normal value was calculated as a weighted average of the prices of profitable sales only during the investigation period for those product types.

- (734) Normal value was constructed by adding the following to the average cost of production of the like product of the cooperating sampled exporting producer during the investigation period:

- (a) the weighted average SG & A expenses incurred by the cooperating sampled exporting producer on domestic sales of the like product, in the ordinary course of trade, during the IP; and

- (b) the weighted average profit realised by the cooperating sampled exporting producers on domestic sales of the like product, in the ordinary course of trade, during the IP.

- (735) Where there were no domestic sales of a product type of the like product, the Commission looked for alternative sources for prices in the ordinary course of trade. As there were no other sampled cooperating or available domestic producers of the like product in the country concerned, no such prices were available. the Commission constructed the normal value for the relevant product types in accordance with Article 2(3) and (6) of the basic Regulation.

- (736) POSVIET sourced a significant share of hot-rolled coils (‘HRC’), the main raw material in the manufacturing of CRF, from several related suppliers. Therefore, the Commission examined whether those purchases were made at arm’s length prices. The Commission compared the sales prices of POSCO Co. Ltd. (‘POSCO HQ’) to unrelated customers for relevant steel grades with the sales prices to POSVIET at ex-works level. The Commission considered that the sales of POSCO HQ were an appropriate proxy to examine the purchase prices of HRC from any of the related suppliers. The Commission found that POSCO HQ sold HRC of relevant steel grades to unrelated customers for prices higher than prices charged to POSVIET for the same steel grades. Consequently, the Commission adjusted the cost of HRC reported by POSVIET to an arm’s length level. This resulted in an increase of the cost of production of 3,7 %.

- (737) The adjusted cost of production was used for the profitability analysis of domestic sales described in recital (733), as well as for the construction of the normal value described in recitals (734) and (735).

- (738) Following final disclosure, POSVIET claimed that the arm’s length adjustment was not carried out correctly. In particular, it pointed out that another related supplier submitted the requested information and supporting evidence. This information contained data on purchase prices and sales prices (charged to POSVIET and to several unrelated customers). POSVIET argued that for purchases of HRC from this related supplier, the adjustment should not have been carried out, as the sales to POSVIET generated a margin, arguing that these prices were at arm’s length. The company reiterated its claim in its comments on the additional final disclosure, despite the fact that the matter was not subject to the additional final disclosure and the company was instructed to only comment on the issues covered by the additional final disclosure.

- (739) The Commission examined the collected data. First, the Commission found that from the information provided by the related supplier, it is not clear whether the reported purchase price contained all acquisition cost or only the price charged by the supplying company. Second, the margin (difference between the reported purchase and sales price) was so low that it could hardly be considered a reasonable profit, in particular since the sales of this related supplier to its unrelated customers generated a considerably higher margin, which indicates that the sales to POSVIET were not at arm’s length price. This analysis is limited by the fact that the sales listing provided by the related supplier did not contain information on HRC type, e.g. based on steel grade or other attributes. Detailed analysis was provided to POSVIET separately as it contained confidential, company-specific data. Consequently, the claim detailed in recital (738) was rejected.

##### 3.6.4.   *Export price*

- (740) The sampled exporting producer exported to the Union either (a) directly to independent customers, (b) through a related company located in a third country acting as a trader, POSCO International Corporation (‘PIC Korea’), exporting directly to the Union, or (c) through PIC Korea exporting through related companies acting as an importer in the Union, POSCO INTERNATIONAL Deutschland GmbH (‘PI Germany’) and POSCO INTERNATIONAL Italia S.r.l (‘PI Italy’).

- (741) For the export sales of the product concerned carried out directly to independent customers in the Union, the export price was the price actually paid or payable for the product concerned when sold for export to the Union, in accordance with Article 2(8) of the basic Regulation.

- (742) For the export sales of the product concerned to the Union carried out through related companies acting as an importer, the export price was established on the basis of the price at which the imported product was first resold to independent customers in the Union, in accordance with Article 2(9) of the basic Regulation. In this case, adjustments to the price were made for all costs incurred between importation and resale, including SG & A expenses, and for profits accruing. The profit margin used for the adjustments was 2 %, as explained in recital (630).

- (743) Adjustments reflecting all cost incurred between importation and resale were made for handling, loading and transportation in the Union, and credit cost incurred.

##### 3.6.5.   *Comparison*

- (744) Article 2(10) of the basic Regulation requires the Commission to make a fair comparison between the normal value and the export price at the same level of trade and to make allowances for differences in factors which affect prices and price comparability. In the case at hand the Commission chose to compare the normal value and the export price of the sampled exporting producers at the *ex-works* level of trade. As further explained below, where appropriate, the normal value and the export price were adjusted in order to: (i) net them back to the *ex-works* level; and (ii) make allowances for differences in factors which were claimed, and demonstrated, to affect prices and price comparability.

###### 3.6.5.1.   Adjustments made to the normal value

- (745) As explained in section 3.6.3, the normal value was established based on the domestic sales price or constructed using costs of production together with amounts for SG & A expenses and for profit, which were considered to be reasonable for that level of trade.

- (746) The Commission made adjustment for transport, handling and loading cost incurred for sales on the domestic market under Article 2(10)(e) of the basic Regulation in order to net the domestic sales price back to ex-works level, credit granted to independent customers on the domestic market under Article 2(10)(g) of the basic Regulation, packing expenses incurred for domestic sales of the like product under Article 2(10)(f) of the basic Regulation, and for warranties and guaranties provided to independent domestic customers under Article 2(10)(h) of the basic Regulation.

- (747) Following final disclosure, POSVIET reiterated its claim that the Commission should have adjusted the normal value in case of sales of automotive CRF.

- (748) According to the company, in section 3.4.5 of this Regulation, the Commission itself confirmed that the classification of product types using the product control number (‘PCN’) and price comparability are not the same issue as it examined the request made by the Taiwanese exporting producers for an adjustment under 2(10) of the basic Regulation for physical differences, level of trade, and other factors individually. The company argued that the situation of the Taiwanese companies is materially different from POSVIET. POSVIET submitted that unlike the Taiwanese companies, it provided evidence concerning both higher cost and technical and commercial reasons for price difference.

- (749) In this respect, POSVIET maintained that product similarity was not the same as price comparability. According to the company, in the company-specific final disclosure, the Commission’s reasoning answered the question of whether automotive and non-automotive products are sufficiently similar to fall under the same PCN but not whether they are comparable in terms of price without an adjustment. The company referred to a price adjustment the Commission applied in the anti-dumping proceeding concerning imports of Acrylonitrile-Butadiene-Styrene Resins (‘ABS’) originating in the Republic of Korea and Taiwan [^ntr143-L_202602547EN.000101-E0143].

- (750) In addition, POSVIET submitted that its initial claim was not that higher cost had led to a higher price. The company argued that it had submitted evidence that the automotive grades had higher cost. The higher prices were however not only caused by the higher cost. The company claimed that there were additional unique economic features of automotive sales, namely:

- (a) Stringent specifications and certification;

- (b) The value automotive customers attach to the reliability and security of supply;

- (c) Reduced price sensitivity because qualifications and re-certification restrict supplier substitution.

- (751) In this respect, the company recalled the Commission’s findings in the framework of the steel safeguard investigation [^ntr144-L_202602547EN.000101-E0144] where the Commission separated automotive CRF from other types of CRF. In the company-specific final disclosure, the Commission considered that the example of the steel safeguard is not applicable in this case as in the safeguard investigation, the products were identified only on the basis of tariff classification while in the proceeding at hand the establishment of PCN allows for a comparison of like product types. The company however disagreed and maintained that the Commission had already recognised that automotive steel was commercially distinctive because it is produced to precise specifications, requires supplier certification, is supplied under long-term arrangements and should, in the circumstances examined there, be considered separately from standard products [^ntr145-L_202602547EN.000101-E0145].

- (752) POSVIET recalled the alleged evidence submitted to support the above allegations. It stressed that the observed high prices of automotive grades were not the only reason for the requested adjustment. The company claimed that the observed higher prices were a result of the above-mentioned features of automotive sales.

- (753) Furthermore, POSVIET alleged that the Commission’s analysis of the cost of production of automotive and non-automotive grades falling under the same PCN was flawed. The Commission determined the ranges of unit cost of production for each PCN under which automotive grades were sold and found that the unit cost of production of automotive sales fell well within those ranges. The company argued that weighted averages should have been determined instead. The weighted averages show that the average unit cost of production of automotive grades was overall and also on a per-PCN basis higher than the average unit cost of production of non-automotive grades. The company also noted that this creates an asymmetry considering that it did not sell any CRF to automotive customers in the Union. In this respect, the company again referred to the ABS investigation mentioned in recital (749).

- (754) Finally, POSVIET complained that the Commission did not address its request for an adjustment under Article 2(10)(d) of the basic Regulation (level of trade) and Article 2(10)(k) of the basic Regulation (other factors) individually. The company further maintained that it provided sufficient evidence to support its request for an adjustment under Article 2(10)(a) of the basic Regulation (physical differences). In this context, the company also reiterated its argument than the division of cold-rolled flat steel products into two subcategories (automotive and non-automotive) under the steel safeguard supported its claim of the existence of physical differences.

- (755) The Commission examined the individual elements of the company’s claim concerning an adjustment to the normal value based on either Article 2(10)(a), or Article 2(10)(d) or Article 2(10)(k) of the basic Regulation. Where necessary, the Commission elaborated on its reasons in a company-specific disclosure as some information and evidence provided were of confidential nature.

- (756) First, the Commission agreed that there might be reasons for an adjustment notwithstanding the established PCN classification, if impact on price comparability is demonstrated by the party claiming the adjustment. It however disagreed that any part of this Regulation confirmed that it was the case in this investigation. The analysis of the request of the Taiwanese exporting producers for an adjustment merely demonstrated that the Commission analysed and addressed all claims received by the respective parties.

- (757) The necessity for an adjustment must be examined on a case-by-case basis and based on the facts of the case. Therefore, findings made in the ABS investigation cited by the company are not applicable in the present case. In the ABS investigation, the detailed analysis of the mass-balanced product types showed that a simple differentiation between mass-balanced and conventional product types did not capture the resulting differences in costs and prices depending on the type of mass-balanced feedstock [^ntr146-L_202602547EN.000101-E0146]. In addition, the Commission found in that case that the product types for which an adjustment was applied were specifically developed for a single customer and the exporting producer was contractually obliged not to sell those product types to other customers.

- (758) Concerning the findings of the steel safeguard investigation, the Commission maintained its position. The reasoning used in the safeguard does not inform the decision that was to be made in the present investigation. The Commission did not decide to create a separate subcategory for automotive products because they were not comparable in terms of costs and prices. The Commission took this decision based on the interest of the users. In this respect, it was demonstrated that the automotive products were usually supplied just in time, which increased the risk that some specific product types would be crowded out from the free of duty quota by standard (non-automotive) products that can be massively supplied and stockpiled at the beginning of the period for which the tariff rate quota was defined.

- (759) With regard to POSVIET’s request for an adjustment, the Commission confirmed that, in the final disclosure, it focused on the analysis of the cost of production. The Commission considered that the specific nature of the automotive sales, in particular the alleged higher requirements concerning technical specifications and quality, would be reflected in the cost of production. In this respect, the Commission disagreed that it should have used the weighted average to compare whether the cost of production of automotive grades was aligned with the cost of production of non-automotive grades. In the present case, calculating a weighted average unit cost would be tainted by the quantities in which the individual product types falling under the same PCN were produced. A range based on the minimum and maximum provided a more appropriate picture of the cost of production applicable to non-automotive product types falling under one PCN. The Commission found that the automotive product types falling under the same PCN generated unit cost of production well within the range established for non-automotive product types.

- (760) To properly address the company’s claims under Articles 2(10)(a), 2(10)(d) or 2(10)(k) of the basic Regulation, the Commission recounted the claims made, and the reasoning and evidence provided supporting those claims. This analysis also addressed the claim that POSVIET’s situation is fundamentally different from the one of the Taiwanese exporting producers (see recital (748)).

- (761) In the initial questionnaire reply, POSVIET requested a revision of the PCN structure based on the precedent from the steel safeguard and the three elements listed in recital (750) and noted that should the Commission decide not to revise the PCN, it would make a request for an adjustment for physical differences, level of trade and/or other factors.

- (762) The company made the request only during the on-spot verification. POSVIET provided a submission where it elaborated on the specific features of the sales to automotive industry as opposed to the sales to non-automotive customers, a letter from its automotive supplier on its sourcing policy, reports issued by the automotive customer approving selected steel grades manufactured by POSVIET, price quotation for a period falling under the investigation period, and the calculation to establish the level of the adjustment.

- (763) The Commission examined this information and evidence to determine whether an adjustment for physical differences pursuant to Article 2(10)(a) of the basic Regulation would be appropriate. The Commission found that the company sold two categories of product types to its automotive customer: (i) product types manufactured in line with an industrial standard applicable to steel for *inter alia* automotive industry; and (ii) product types manufactured in line with an industrial standard applicable to cold-rolled products in general. The majority of the sales to the automotive client (75-85 %) fell under the second category. At the same time, these non-automotive product types sold to the automotive client were sold to other, non-automotive customers. In this respect, the Commission found that the company provided approval reports for only for the product types produced in line with the automotive industrial standard while it requested an adjustment for all its sales to the automotive customer. In addition, the evidence provided showed that indeed for the product types produced under the automotive industrial standard, the automotive customer analysed their compliance with the requested technical specifications. The specifications and the examination methods were both based on the automotive industrial standard. The Commission further found that the company sold the product types approved by the automotive customer under the automotive industrial standard to other customers although only in minor quantities. The Commission concluded that for the majority of the product types sold to the automotive customer, there were no differences in physical characteristics as the same steel grades were sold to non-automotive customers on the domestic market. A smaller share of product types sold to the automotive customer (15-25 %) was indeed manufactured in line with a specific industrial standard applicable to *inter alia* automotive industry (but also for cold-rolled flat steel products used for electric equipment or in construction). The Commission considered that physical differences between these product types and the product types sold to non-automotive customers were not demonstrated. Even if there were any, the effect on the normal value would be negligible as the total sales to the automotive customer represented a less than 2 % of total domestic sales of POSVIET. Consequently, the Commission rejected the request for an adjustment to the normal value for physical differences. Further details were provided to the company in a company-specific disclosure.

- (764) The Commission further examined the information and evidence mentioned in recital (762) to determine whether an adjustment for the level of trade pursuant to Article 2(10)(d) of the basic Regulation would be appropriate. The Commission recalled that for this adjustment to be justified, the company must show that, in relation to the distribution chain, the export price is at a different level of trade from the normal value and the difference has affected price comparability, which is demonstrated by consistent and distinct differences in functions and prices of the seller for the different levels of trade in the domestic market of the exporting country. The Commission found that the company merely identified that it charged higher prices to one customer, the automotive customer, on the domestic market. It however failed to explain and demonstrate the differences in functions that would explain the higher prices. In addition, POSVIET had only one automotive customer. Therefore, any pricing behaviour towards a single customer can be hardly considered as a proof of consistent and distinct differences between automotive and non-automotive sales. Considering all the above, the Commission rejected the claim.

- (765) With regard to the request for an adjustment under Article 2(10)(k) of the basic Regulation, the Commission provided a detailed analysis to the company in a company-specific disclosure. In general terms, the Commission found that the company did not demonstrate a correlation between the factors listed by the company as specific features of automotive sales and the prices charged to the automotive customer. In particular, the Commission found that automotive sales falling under some of the PCNs covering both, automotive and non-automotive sales, were lossmaking, i.e. these allegedly special features of automotive sales were not able to generate a price that would cover the cost of production. Consequently, the Commission rejected the claim.

- (766) Finally, based on the considerations set out in recitals (755) to (765), the Commission rejected the claims presented by POSVIET as elaborated in recitals (747) to (754).

- (767) Following additional final disclosure, POSVIET reiterated that using the weighted average for the comparison of automotive and non-automotive grades with regard to their cost of production was more appropriate than using ranges. The company maintained that the weighted average reflects the actual commercial importance of observations while a range gives a decisive importance to the two extremes irrespective of whether either is representative.

- (768) With regard to the Commission’s analysis of the company’s request for an adjustment for physical differences under Article 2(10)(a) of the basic Regulation, POSVIET introduced two arguments.

- (769) First, the company claimed that the Commission analysis based on industry standards according to which the product types sold to the automotive customer were produced was not relevant to make conclusions of whether an adjustment under Article 2(10)(a) of the basic Regulation was justified. The company argued that a product can fall under the same PCN or a broad industry standard and still be subject to additional customer-specific requirements.

- (770) Second, POSVIET argued that the specific requirements of its automotive customer had a demonstrated market value. In this respect, the company referred to the fact that the products are tested against the automotive customer’s specific technical requirements, the weighted average unit cost of production is higher than the cost of non-automotive grades under the same PCN, and the weighted average ex-works sales prices of the automotive grades are also higher than those of non-automotive sales for the same PCN.

- (771) With regard to the Commission’s assessment of the company’s request for an adjustment for differences in the level of trade (OEM sales) under Article 2(10)(d) of the basic Regulation, the company provided an overview of differences between the functions performed for automotive and non-automotive customers within the following categories: (a) qualification and testing, (b) forecasting and planning, (c) inventory and logistics, (d) pricing authority, (e) handling of claims and technical support. The company also claimed that the effect of those differences in functions had been demonstrated through the higher prices charged to the automotive customer and the consistency of those differences was demonstrated across transactions, products and periods. Finally, the company added that even if the Commission considered that the conditions of Article 2(10)(d)(i) of the basic Regulation were not fully met, it should grant a special adjustment under Article 2(10)(d)(ii) of the basic Regulation.

- (772) With regard to the Commission’s assessment of the company’s request for an adjustment for other factors under Article 2(10)(k) of the basic Regulation, the company argued that:

- (a) It made the claim under during the on-spot verification, not only after final disclosure;

- (b) Restricted sustainability was supported by evidence of certification process;

- (c) Although per-country prices are differentiated, regional pricing is centrally negotiated;

- (d) The weighted averages of cost of production and sales prices provide direct evidence of consistent price effect;

- (e) The loss-making PCNs do not disprove the price effect;

- (f) The absence of automotive sales to the Union requires the adjustment as the effect of other factors on prices has been allegedly established.

- (773) The Commission maintained that using the weighted average for a comparison of cost of production was not appropriate for the purpose of establishing whether the cost of production of automotive grades was consistently higher than the cost of production of non-automotive (commodity) grades. Indeed, where a number of different values are divided into two groups and an average is calculated for each group, one average will almost invariably be higher than the other. However, that difference, in itself, provides no indication of whether the individual values in one group are consistently higher than those in the other group. In addition, already in its comments on final disclosure the company argued that the Commission had not performed a proper examination of its requests for adjustments under Articles 2(10)(a), 2(10)(d) and 2(10)(k) of the basic Regulation. This analysis was carried out, and the Commission rejected the company’s requests on a basis different from the analysis of the average cost of production.

- (774) With regard to the claim under Article 2(10)(a) of the basic Regulation, the Commission noted that it did not base its conclusion on the mere fact that the product types sold to the automotive customer were products under a certain industry standard. The Commission does not dispute that products produced in line with various industry standards can fall under the same PCN. The core of the Commission’s argument was that there was no evidence of the existence of physical differences between individual grades produced according to commodity industry standard, which represented a vast majority of the company’s sales to the automotive customer while the same specific grades were also sold to non-automotive customers. The approval reports submitted by the company concerned only product types produced in line with the automotive industry standard, which represented a minority of the company’s sales to the automotive customer. In any event, the company did not explain the physical differences between the product types sold to the automotive customer and to non-automotive customers. The mere existence of price difference cannot be accepted as evidence of physical differences.

- (775) With regard to the claim under Article 2(10)(d) of the basic Regulation, the Commission analysed the functions declared by POSVIET with regard to automotive and non-automotive sales. For some alleged functions, namely qualification and testing, forecasting and planning, inventory and logistics, technical support, there was no supporting evidence on the file, or the evidence was only partial (the existence of approval/testing reports for a limited number of product types). With regard to handling of claims, there appears to be an overlap of provided functions and services. While the claims procedure appears to be clearly established for the automotive customer, similar procedure may be applied to non-automotive customers depending on the nature of the claim. With regard to pricing authority, it is not clear how the fact that prices are negotiated centrally represents a function related to the level of trade/OEM sales.

- (776) With regard to the claim under Article 2(10)(k) of the basic Regulation, the Commission noted that:

- (a) Although the company made the claim during the on-spot verification, it only clearly identified the factors that should be considered under these provisions in its comments on final disclosure;

- (b) The company did not provide any evidence of a comprehensive certification procedure but merely approval/testing reports for a limited number of product types sold to the automotive customer. The Commission found that regular testing of the compliance of the supplied products with the technical specifications of the automotive customer could not have resulted in restricted sustainability of suppliers;

- (c) With regard to the centralised negotiation of prices, the Commission noted that the company previously claimed that the prices charged to the automotive customer were not representative of the Vietnamese market because they were not set specifically for Vietnam but rather on a regional level. This was disproved by the Commission’s observations of the price quotation submitted by the company;

- (d) The relevance of weighted average cost of production (and prices for that matter) had been already addressed several times;

- (e) It was unclear how the fact that the automotive sales falling under two of eight relevant PCNs were loss-making is not disproving the company’s claim that an adjustment was justified. In particular, considering that the company proposed two methods to perform the adjustment and both of them were based on adjusting the prices to such level that sales of automotive grades under each relevant PCN would generate the same level of profitability as the non-automotive sales under that PCN.

- (777) In any event, POSVIET continuously based its claims for an adjustment under Articles 2(10)(a), 2(10)(d) or 2(10)(k) of the basic Regulation merely on the existence of higher prices charged to its automotive customer. The company then alleged the existence of differences in physical characteristics, functions related to the level of trade/OEM sales or other factors. Instead of providing supporting evidence for the existence of those differences, the company claimed that the mere existence of higher prices constituted evidence of those differences. In this regard, the Commission noted that the identified price difference on the domestic market depending on a customer is a normal occurrence on a free market which should be reflected rather than eliminated from the normal value. POSVIET nevertheless failed to explain that price difference in terms of concrete price-affecting factors as required by Article 2(10) of the basic Regulation. Without such connection there is no reason for the adjustment. A mere price difference does not necessarily show a price premium; it could equally show a price deduction for non-automotive clients, and, without any demonstrable link to a concrete factor, is an ordinary market occurrence shaping the normal value for a particular product type.

- (778) Following the considerations set out in recitals (773) to (777), the Commission rejected the claims described in recitals (767) to (772).

###### 3.6.5.2.   Adjustments made to the export price

- (779) In order to net the export price back to the ex-works level of trade, adjustments were made on the account of freight, insurance, handling loading and ancillary expenses under Article 2(10)(e) of the basic Regulation.

- (780) Allowances were made for the following factors affecting prices and price comparability: credit granted to independent customers on the domestic market under Article 2(10)(g) of the basic Regulation, packing expenses incurred for export sales of the product concerned to the Union under Article 2(10)(f) of the basic Regulation, bank charges under Article 2(10)(k) of the basic Regulation, and for commissions under Article 2(10)(i) of the basic Regulation. Commissions were paid by PIC Korea to a related company in Vietnam for handling of documents, communication and other services provided with regard to the exports of product concerned produced by POSVIET to the Union.

- (781) For all export sales carried out through PIC Korea, a trader, an adjustment was made under Article 2(10)(i) of the basic Regulation. The Commission considered that the SG & A expenses incurred by the trader and the profit of an unrelated importer of 2 %, as explained in recital (630) above, reliably reflected the commission an unrelated agent would charge for its services.

- (782) Following final disclosure, POSVIET reiterated its claim that the three trading companies – PIC Korea, PI Germany, and PI Italy (‘PI Group’) – were a single economic entity (‘SEE’). The company acknowledged that in anti-dumping proceedings, the SEE concept had been applied to a trader related to the exporting producer where the economic reality of the relationship showed that the trader acted as an internal export department of the producer. The company reiterated its claim in its comments on the additional final disclosure, despite the fact that the matter was not subject to the additional final disclosure and the company was instructed to only comment on the issues covered by the additional final disclosure.

- (783) The company however claimed that the fact that POSVIET is not part of the alleged SEE is not relevant in the present case and referred to the Union’s competition law which recognises that an undertaking is an economic unit that may consist of several distinct natural or legal persons. In this case, the test turns to the economic unity and control, and not to whether one of the entities is a producer. In this respect, POSVIET argued that the PI Group is an SEE because:

- (a) The accounts of the group were consolidated;

- (b) The sales functions of the three companies were centrally coordinated;

- (c) The two related importers in the Union did not set the prices independently;

- (d) The two related importers in the Union were set up to carry out functions that could not be performed by PIC Korea.

- (784) POSVIET submitted that it did not object to the deduction of SG & A expenses under both Article 2(9) and Article 2(10)(i) of the basic Regulation, it however objected to the double deduction of profit.

- (785) It also suggested that a single deduction of consolidated SG & A expenses under Article 2(9) of the basic Regulation would be appropriate.

- (786) Finally, the company recalled that pursuant to the judgment in *PT Musim Mas v Commission* [^ntr147-L_202602547EN.000101-E0147], where an SEE is recognised between a producer and a related trader, the trader cannot be considered performing functions of an agent working on a commission basis pursuant to Article 2(10)(i) of the basic Regulation.

- (787) The Commission examined the claim. Indeed, in anti-dumping proceedings, the concept of an SEE is used to describe situations where a trader is related to the exporting producer, and the economic reality of the relationship shows that the trader acts as an internal export department of the producer. By definition, this circumstance is not in contradiction with the understanding of an undertaking under Union competition law, although it must be noted that Union competition law is not applicable in trade defence investigations.

- (788) The Commission noted that POSVIET’s claim regarding the existence of a SEE among PIC Korea, PI German and PI Italy aimed at achieving a modification to how adjustments to the export price were applied. In essence, the company objected to an alleged double deduction of the notional profit under Articles 2(9) and 2(10)(i) of the basic Regulation or alternatively proposed to deduct consolidated SG & A expenses of the PI Group under Article 2(9) of the basic Regulation.

- (789) In this respect, the Commission recalled that the deduction of SG & A expenses and notional profit under Articles 2(9) and 2(10)(i) of the basic Regulation, while both relying on the same type of information, namely SG & A expenses actually incurred by the related companies and a profit of an unrelated importer, represent two separate exercises.

- (790) Under Article 2(9) of the basic Regulation, the Commission constructed the export price on the basis of the price at which CRF was resold to the first independent customer as the importers PI Germany and PI Italy were related to the exporting producer. In line with Article 2(9) of the basic Regulation, an adjustment for all costs incurred between importation and resale and for profits accruing was made. The costs incurred between importation and resale included in this case those reported by the importers on a transaction basis and SG & A expenses that could not be directly linked to individual transactions.

- (791) Once both export price and the normal value were established, the Commission then made an adjustment to the export price for the purpose of fair comparison pursuant to Article 2(10)(i) of the basic Regulation. The Commission found that PIC Korea performed functions similar to those of an agent working on a commission basis and was renumerated for these functions by the way of a mark-up. Since the mark-up was paid between related entities it was tainted by the relation. Therefore, the Commission used the SG & A expenses incurred by PIC Korea and the notional profit as a proxy for the commission that would have been paid to an independent agent performing functions of PIC Korea.

- (792) In this respect, POSVIET did not prove that the Commission’s approach was in violation of the basic Regulation. The Commission was legally required to deduct SG & A expenses and a profit to construct the export price under Article 2(9) of the basic Regulation. As for the adjustment under Article 2(10)(i) of the basic Regulation, the company did not dispute the Commission’s findings that PIC Korea performed functions similar to those of an agent working on a commission basis. Nor did the company claim and provide evidence that PIC Korea acted as an internal export department of POSVIET, in which case an adjustment under Article 2(10)(i) of the basic Regulation would indeed not be appropriate.

- (793) Following final disclosure, POSVIET submitted that level of the profit deducted under Article 2(10)(i) of the basic Regulation for PIC KR when selling directly to unrelated customers in the Union should be revised to reflect the limited functions of the company. In this respect, the company referred to the judgement in *PT Musim Mas v Commission* [^ntr148-L_202602547EN.000101-E0148], where the Court clarified that the use of Article 2(10)(i) of the basic Regulation is not based on the existence of a relationship between the trader and the exporting producer, but on the functions performed by the trader. The company argued that the level of the profit must be tailored to the functions performed by the trader, rather than those carried out by an importer.

- (794) Furthermore, POSVIET referred to the OECD Transfer Pricing Guidelines [^ntr149-L_202602547EN.000101-E0149] according to which ‘[t]he resales price margin will be influenced by the level of activities performed by the reseller. This level of activities can range widely from the case where the reseller performs only minimal services as a forwarding agent to the case where the reseller takes on the full risk of ownership together with the full responsibility for and the risks involved in advertising, marketing, distributing and guaranteeing the goods, financing stocks, and other connected services. If the reseller in the controlled transaction does not carry on a substantial commercial activity but only transfers the goods to a third party, the resale price margin could, in light of the functions performed, be a small one.’

- (795) In this respect, the company pointed out that PIC Korea does not provide warehousing or customs clearance, neither is it engaged in day-to-day customer relationship management in the sales channel where it sells directly to unrelated customers in the Union.

- (796) The company reiterated the claim set out in recitals (793) to (795) in its comments on the additional final disclosure, despite the fact that the matter was not subject to the additional final disclosure and the company was instructed to only comment on the issues covered by the additional final disclosure.

- (797) At the outset the Commission noted that, contrary to the claim made by POSVIET, the judgement *PT Musim Mas v Commission* [^ntr150-L_202602547EN.000101-E0150] does not link the profit used to quantify the adjustment under Article 2(10)(i) of the basic Regulation to the functions performed by the trader acting as an agent. Rather, it is the adjustment itself that is linked to those functions. In this respect the Commission recalled that the adjustment for commission under Article 2(10)(i) of the basic Regulation comprises two distinct components: (i) the actual SG & A costs of the related trader, and (ii) a notional profit of an unrelated trader active in the same sector. While the profit component serves as a proxy for the margin that a comparable independent trader would earn on the free market, the SG & A costs component reflects the actual expenses incurred on the account of the functions performed by the related traders. It is precisely this latter element, the actual SG & A costs, that ensures the adjustment accurately reflects the costs of the functions carried out.

- (798) The Commission confirmed that the adjustment under Article 2(10)(i) of the basic Regulation applied to exports to the Union via PIC Korea was not based on the existence of a relationship between POSVIET and PIC Korea but on the involvement of a trader in several sales channels in which the trader performed the functions similar to those of an agent working on a commission basis. The existence of the relationship however informed the determination of the level of the adjustment. While the actual SG & A expenses of PIC Korea were accepted as a part of the adjustment, the profit generated by the sales could not be considered as the purchase price was affected by the relationship between POSVIET and PIC Korea. Therefore, the profit of an unrelated importer in the Union was used as a suitable proxy.

- (799) Furthermore, the company asserted that the level of the profit should have been adjusted downwards since the company performed limited functions in comparison to the related importers in the Union and relied on the OECD Transfer Pricing Guidelines in this respect.

- (800) Regarding the OECD Transfer Pricing Guidelines, the Commission recalled that they are based on an ‘[i]nternational transfer pricing standard that OECD member countries have agreed should be used for tax purposes by [multinational enterprise] groups and tax administrations’ [^ntr151-L_202602547EN.000101-E0151]. The OECD Transfer Pricing Guidelines are not intended to predict what profit on the resale of particular goods is achievable or reasonable in a given market based on the functions performed by a trader. Profit allocation based on the functions performed by related entities in this context serves essentially as a safeguard against attributing profits to companies that perform few functions but are located in low-tax jurisdictions.

- (801) In any event, the Commission disagreed that the functions carried out by PIC Korea were limited as compared to the functions of the two related importers in the Union. For confidentiality reasons, a detailed analysis was disclosed to the company separately. In this context, the OECD Transfer Pricing Guidelines, on which the company relied in its argumentation, provides examples of situations where the functions of the trader are considered limited (minimal services as a forwarding agent) and where substantial, i.e. a higher margin can be expected (such as, for example, full risk of ownership, full responsibility for and the risks involved in advertising, marketing, distributing and guaranteeing the goods). PIC Korea clearly took over the ownership of the goods during the sea transportation and was responsible for the organisation of the distribution network. Therefore, its functions cannot be considered limited. Consequently, the claim set out in recital (797) was rejected.

##### 3.6.6.   *Dumping margins*

- (802) For the sampled cooperating exporting producer, the Commission compared the weighted average normal value of each type of the like product with the weighted average export price of the corresponding type of the product concerned, in accordance with Article 2(11) and (12) of the basic Regulation.

- (803) On this basis, the definitive weighted average dumping margins expressed as a percentage of the CIF Union frontier price, duty unpaid, is 16,0 %.

- (804) For the cooperating exporting producers outside the sample, the Commission calculates the weighted average dumping margin, in accordance with Article 9(6) of the basic Regulation. In the present case, the definitive dumping margin of the cooperating exporting producers outside the sample is the same as of the sampled cooperating exporting producer, i.e. 16,0 %.

- (805) For all other exporting producers in Vietnam, the Commission established the dumping margin on the basis of the facts available, in accordance with Article 18 of the basic Regulation. To this end, the Commission determined the level of cooperation of the exporting producers. The level of cooperation is the volume of exports of the cooperating exporting producers to the Union expressed as proportion of the total imports from the country concerned to the Union in the IP, that were established on the basis of Eurostat.

- (806) The level of cooperation in this case is high because the exports of the cooperating exporting producers constituted more than 80 % of the total imports during the investigation period. On this basis, the Commission decided to establish the dumping margin for non-cooperating exporting producers at the level of the cooperating sampled individually examined company.

- (807) The definitive dumping margins, expressed as a percentage of the CIF Union frontier price, duty unpaid, are as follows:

| Company | Definitive dumping margin (%) |
| --- | --- |
| POSCO VIETNAM CO., LTD | 16,0 |
| Other cooperating companies | 16,0 |
| All other imports originating in Vietnam | 16,0 |

### 4. **INJURY**

#### 4.1.   **Definition of the Union industry and Union production**

- (808) The like product was manufactured by 22 producers in the Union during the investigation period. They constitute the ‘Union industry’ within the meaning of Article 4(1) of the basic Regulation.

- (809) The total Union production during the investigation period was established at around 31,5 million tonnes, including production for the captive market. The Commission established the figure on the basis of all the available information concerning the Union industry, namely the verified questionnaire reply received from Eurofer, where possible cross-checked with the verified questionnaire replies of the sampled Union producers.

- (810) As noted in recital (94), a sample of three Union producers was selected. The definitive sample accounted for more than 24 % of the estimated total Union production and 17 % of the estimated sales of the like product on the free market in the Union and ensured a good geographical spread.

- (811) Overall, the Union industry is characterised by a significant degree of vertical integration. A substantial share of production is used captively for further downstream processing, while the remainder is sold on the free market in direct competition with imports.

#### 4.2.   **Determination of the relevant Union market**

- (812) To establish whether the Union industry suffered injury and to determine consumption and the various economic indicators related to the situation of the Union industry, the Commission examined to which extent the Union industry’s own use of its production, in particular for further processing, should be taken into account in that analysis.

- (813) As indicated above, the Union industry is largely vertically integrated, and the product under investigation constitutes a primary input for the manufacture of various higher value downstream products, such as galvanized and organic coated products. The Commission therefore found that a significant share of the sampled Union producers’ production was destined for captive use. In view of this, captive consumption and free market consumption were analysed separately.

- (814) The distinction between captive and free market is relevant for the injury analysis because products destined for captive use are not exposed to direct competition from imports, and transfer prices are set within the groups according to various price policies. By contrast, production destined for the free market is in direct competition with imports of the product under investigation, and prices are free market prices.

- (815) To provide a picture of the Union industry that is as complete as possible, the Commission obtained data for the entire CRF activity and determined whether the production was destined for captive use or for the free market.

- (816) The Commission examined certain economic indicators relating to the Union industry on the basis of data for the free market. These indicators are: sales volume and sales prices on the Union market; market share; growth; export volume and prices; profitability; return on investment; and cash flow. Where possible and justified, the findings of the examination were compared with the data for the captive market in order to provide a complete picture of the situation of the Union industry.

- (817) However, other economic indicators could meaningfully be examined only by referring to the whole activity, including the captive use of the Union industry. These are: production; capacity, capacity utilisation; investments; stocks; employment; productivity; wages; and ability to raise capital. They depend on the whole activity, whether the production is captive or sold on the free market.

#### 4.3.   **Union consumption**

##### 4.3.1.   *Captive consumption on the Union market (tonnes)*

- (818) The Commission established the Union captive consumption on the basis of the captive use and captive sales on the Union market of all known producers in the Union. On this basis, the Union captive consumption developed as follows:

*Table 25*

**Captive consumption on the Union market (tonnes)**

|  | 2022 | 2023 | 2024 | Investigation period |
| Captive consumption | 24 019 465 | 24 266 863 | 24 913 310 | 24 037 814 |
| *Index (2022 = 100)* | *100* | *101* | *104* | *100* |
| *Source:* Eurofer questionnaire reply. | *Source:* Eurofer questionnaire reply. | *Source:* Eurofer questionnaire reply. | *Source:* Eurofer questionnaire reply. | *Source:* Eurofer questionnaire reply. |

- (819) It was established that around 76 % of the Union production of CRF is dedicated to the captive market whilst the remaining 24 % is sold under free market conditions.

- (820) EU captive consumption increased by around 4 % between 2022 and 2024, reflecting a gradual expansion in captive demand. However, this trend was not sustained, as consumption subsequently decreased by approximately 3,5 % during the investigation period compared to 2024, broadly returning to its 2022 level. Overall, captive consumption showed only a limited variation during the period considered, indicating relatively stable downstream demand within vertically integrated operations.

##### 4.3.2.   *Free market consumption on the Union market (tonnes)*

- (821) The Commission established the Union free market consumption on the basis of (a) Eurofer data concerning the sales on the Union market of all known producers in the Union, as cross-checked with the information reported by the sampled Union producers; and (b) the imports into the Union from all third countries as reported by Eurostat. On this basis, the Union free market consumption developed as follows:

| *Source:* Eurofer questionnaire reply, sampled Union producers, Eurostat. | *Source:* Eurofer questionnaire reply, sampled Union producers, Eurostat. | *Source:* Eurofer questionnaire reply, sampled Union producers, Eurostat. | *Source:* Eurofer questionnaire reply, sampled Union producers, Eurostat. | *Source:* Eurofer questionnaire reply, sampled Union producers, Eurostat. |

- (822) Free market consumption decreased significantly over the period considered. It fell by 9 % in the first year. This was followed by a moderate increase of 2 % in the subsequent year. However, this recovery was temporary, as consumption declined again by 3 percentage points in the IP.

- (823) Overall, free market consumption recorded a total decrease of 10 % over the period considered. This decline was mainly driven by a contraction in downstream consumption, in particular in the construction sector and certain industrial applications, which are key end-use markets for steel products.

- (824) In addition, macroeconomic uncertainty and weaker economic growth in several Member States contributed to a more cautious purchasing behaviour among customers, who delayed or reduced orders and prioritised inventory reduction. This resulted in lower spot market activity and reduced transactional volumes in the free market segment.

- (825) Overall consumption, the sum of captive and free market consumption, evolved as follows during the period considered:

- (826) Overall Union consumption remained broadly stable over the period considered, with only limited fluctuations. It decreased slightly by 1 % in the first year. This was followed by a modest increase of 3 percentage points in the subsequent year. However, this increase was not sustained, as consumption declined by 4 percentage points in the investigation period.

- (827) Over the period considered, overall consumption remained broadly stable, with a slight decrease of around 2 % between 2022 and the investigation period. This relative stability was mainly due to captive consumption remaining broadly stable, which partially smoothed the declining trend observed in free market consumption, thereby cushioning the overall decrease in demand.

#### 4.4.   **Imports from the countries concerned**

##### 4.4.1.   *Cumulative assessment of the effects of imports from the countries concerned and import volumes and import prices from the countries concerned*

- (828) The Commission examined whether imports of product originating in the countries concerned should be assessed cumulatively, in accordance with Article 3(4) of the basic Regulation.

- (829) The margin of dumping established in relation to the imports from the countries concerned was above the *de minimis* threshold laid down in Article 9(3) of the basic Regulation. The volume of imports from each of the countries concerned was not negligible within the meaning of Article 5(7) of the basic Regulation. Market shares in the investigation period ranged between 3 % and 7 %.

- (830) The conditions of competition between the dumped imports from the countries concerned and the like product were similar. As noted in recital (125) the investigation showed that CRF sold by the exporting producers and the Union industry have the same basic physical chemical and technical characteristics as well as the same basic uses. Moreover, the imported products competed with each other and with the Union industry and with CRF produced in the Union because they are sold through the same sales channels and to similar categories of customers.

- (831) Therefore, all the criteria set out in Article 3(4) of the basic Regulation were met and imports from the countries concerned were examined cumulatively for the purposes of the injury determination.

##### 4.4.2.   *Volume and market share of the imports from the countries concerned*

- (834) Over the period considered, imports from the countries concerned increased overall, both in volume, by 28 %, and in market share, which reached 23 % in the IP. While developments varied significantly amongst the countries concerned, the general trend points to a strengthening presence of these imports on the Union market.

- (835) In 2023, import volumes showed mixed developments. Imports from India decreased slightly (-4 %), while imports from Türkiye dropped sharply (-49 %). By contrast, imports from Japan (+77 %), Taiwan (+50 %) and Vietnam (+139 %) increased significantly. As a result, the overall volume of imports from the countries concerned increased, accompanied by a rise in market share from 16 % to 19 % (+3 percentage points).

- (836) In 2024, the upward trend became more pronounced. Imports from India (+12 %), Japan (+10 %) and Taiwan (+30 %) continued to grow, while imports from Türkiye recovered strongly (+83 %) from the low level recorded in 2023. Imports from Vietnam also increased further (+36 %). This led to a significant expansion of the overall import volume and a marked increase in market share to 24 % (+5 percentage points).

- (837) During the investigation period, import volumes declined compared to 2024 but remained well above 2022 levels. Imports from India decreased moderately (-5 %), while imports from Taiwan (-13 %) and Türkiye (-20 %) also fell. By contrast, imports from Japan (+1 %) and Vietnam (+19 %) continued to increase. Overall, imports from the countries concerned remained high, with market share only slightly decreasing to 23 % (-1 percentage point).

- (838) Over the period considered, imports from the countries concerned increased significantly, both in volume and in market share. Import volumes rose by 6 % in 2023 and further increased markedly by 31 % in 2024. Although they declined by 8 % during the investigation period compared to 2024, they remained 28 % above 2022 levels. In parallel, the market share of these imports grew steadily from 16 % in 2022 to 19 % in 2023 and reached 24 % in 2024, before slightly decreasing to 23 % in the investigation period. Overall, despite a slight drop in the investigation period, imports from the countries concerned showed a strong upward trend and significantly strengthened their position on the Union market.
