### 7. **UNION INTEREST**

- (1019) Having decided to apply Article 7(2) of the basic Regulation, the Commission examined whether it could clearly conclude that it was not in the Union interest to adopt measures in this case, despite the determination of injurious dumping, in accordance with Article 21 of the basic Regulation. The determination of the Union interest was based on an appreciation of all the various interests involved, including those of the Union industry, importers, users.

#### 7.1.   **Interest of the Union industry**

- (1020) The Union industry is located in several Member States (Austria, Belgium, Czech Republic, Germany, Hungary, Italy, Luxemburg, Netherlands, Portugal, Slovakia, Slovenia, Spain and Sweden) and it employs directly 8 598 FTE in relation to the product under investigation.

- (1021) Eleven producers cooperated during the investigation. None of the known producers opposed the initiation of the investigation. As concluded in section 4.5, the situation of the whole Union industry deteriorated as a result of dumped imports from the countries concerned. Material injury was shown in both the micro and the macro indicators and particularly material when it comes to the profitability, sales volumes and market shares of the Union industry.

- (1022) It is expected that the imposition of the anti-dumping duties will restore fair trading conditions on the Union market, end the price depression and enable the Union industry to recover. This would result in an improvement of the Union industry’s profitability towards levels considered necessary for this capital-intensive industry. The Union industry has suffered material injury caused by imports at dumped prices from the countries concerned. It is recalled that many injury indicators showed a negative trend during the period considered. In particular, market share and sales volume, as well as indicators pertaining to the financial performance of the sampled Union producers, such as profitability and return on investment, were seriously affected. It is therefore important to restore fair prices in order to allow all producers to operate on the Union market on a level playing field.

- (1023) In the absence of measures, a further deterioration of the Union industry’s economic situation is very likely. A bad performance on CRF would impact the downstream activities of Union producers, and in particular their captive production. Union producers rely on the business model of a combination of free market sales and captive production. A deterioration of the free market segment could likewise put at risk the captive production segment, which represents bigger production volumes and employment figures than the former

- (1024) It is therefore concluded that the imposition of anti-dumping duties is in the interest of the Union industry as it would enable recovery from the effects of the injurious dumping established and support the long-term sustainability of Union production.

#### 7.2.   **Interest of unrelated importers**

- (1025) As mentioned in recital (96) no unrelated importer completed a questionnaire or provided the Commission with individual elements showing to what extent importers would be harmed by the imposition of measures.

- (1026) In the absence of cooperation and specific data, the likely effects of the imposition of measures on importers must be assessed based on facts available.

- (1027) It can reasonably be assumed that importers may face some cost increases as a result of the imposition of anti-dumping duties. However, as further developed in Section 7.4.1 below, the investigation confirmed the existence of alternative sources of supply, including from Union producers, and the possibility of continued imports at fair prices. These factors mitigate the potential impact of the measures; thus, any negative impact is expected to be limited.

- (1028) While importers may experience a narrowing of supply options or increased costs in the short term, these effects are likely to be moderate. Maintaining the status quo, on the other hand, would, ultimately threaten the viability of domestic supply and potentially lead to greater dependency on dumped imports in the future. On balance, the benefits to the Union industry outweigh the limited and unsubstantiated potential drawbacks for importers.

#### 7.3.   **Interest of users, consumers or suppliers**

- (1029) No users, consumers or suppliers cooperated in the investigation providing a questionnaire reply that was timely, complete, and in the required form. Consequently, no evidence was submitted indicating that the imposition of measures would have a significant adverse effect on users or downstream industries.

- (1030) Following the initiation of the investigation, BSH submitted comments (see recital (90), arguing that measures would increase input costs for downstream industries, and negatively affect competitiveness, employment and supply chain stability. However, BSH did not cooperate further in the investigation by submitting a questionnaire reply, nor did it substantiate its claims with verifiable data on, the share of the product concerned in its total costs of production, the ability to source the product from alternative suppliers, or the capacity to pass on any cost increase to its customers. In the absence of such substantiation, the claim could not be verified and was therefore rejected.

- (1031) In the absence of cooperation from users, the Commission assessed the likely effects of measures on the basis of the information available. The investigation showed that CRF products generally account for only a limited share of the overall production costs incurred by downstream users, including the automotive, construction, household appliances and industrial manufacturing sectors. Therefore, any potential price increase resulting from the imposition of measures would likely have only a limited impact on the overall cost structure of downstream users.

- (1032) At the same time, the imposition of measures would contribute to restoring fair competition on the Union market and to preserving a viable Union industry capable of ensuring a reliable supply of CRF products to downstream industries in the Union, thereby reducing the risk of supply disruptions and excessive dependence on dumped imports.

- (1033) In view of the above, the Commission concluded that there were no compelling reasons to consider that the imposition of anti-dumping measures would be against the interest of users.

- (1034) Following disclosure, the Japanese Producers and the Government of Japan argued that greater consideration should be given to the impact of the measures on Union downstream users. They submitted that high energy costs were already being passed on to users and that additional anti-dumping duties, together with existing trade restrictions, could further increase CRF prices and restrict its availability. They therefore requested that the interests of downstream users be taken into account in the assessment of the measures.

- (1035) The Commission considered that the arguments raised did not alter the conclusions set out hereabove in which the Commission assessed the likely effects of the measures on downstream users on the basis of the information available and found no evidence indicating that the imposition of measures would have a significant adverse effect on users or downstream industries.

- (1036) In particular, as explained hereabove, CRF generally accounts for only a limited share of the overall production costs of downstream users. The arguments concerning the possible increase in CRF prices therefore did not demonstrate that the imposition of measures would have a significant impact on the overall cost structure or competitiveness of downstream users. Nor did the parties provide evidence establishing that the measures would result in significant supply difficulties.

- (1037) At the same time, as explained above, the imposition of measures would contribute to restoring fair competition on the Union market and preserving a viable Union industry capable of ensuring a reliable supply of CRF to downstream industries. The possible effects on downstream users therefore had to be considered in the context of both the potential costs of the measures and the benefits of maintaining a reliable Union supply.

- (1038) The Commission therefore concluded that there were no compelling reasons to consider that the imposition of anti-dumping measures would be against the Union interest. The claims were therefore rejected.

#### 7.4.   **Other factors**

##### 7.4.1.   *Availability of Supply*

- (1039) Some interested parties claimed that the Union industry’s structural limitations prevent it from supplying the Union market with the necessary volumes and qualities of cold-rolled steel. According to these parties, imposing additional trade measures would further tighten supply conditions, aggravate existing shortages, and lead to higher prices within the Union

- (1040) The Commission noted that the investigation confirmed the existence of alternative sources of supply, including Union producers, as well as the possibility of continued imports at fair prices. Specifically, as described in Table 31, the Union industry is operating at 72 % of its production capacity, leaving an available capacity of approximately 12 million tonnes that could be mobilised to meet additional demand. The combined imports from the countries concerned represent a significantly lower volume than this available capacity. Furthermore, the investigation also identified the possibility of additional imports from other origins, which could contribute to ensuring sufficient supply to the Union market. The claim was therefore rejected.

##### 7.4.2.   *Other steel measures*

- (1041) Some interested parties claimed that the simultaneous application of anti-dumping measures and the new steel measure, in force since 1 July 2026 could unduly restrict access to the Union market. They argued that the combined effect of these measures could create disproportionate constraints on steel supply and raise concerns regarding consistency with the Union’s wider external trade policy objectives.

- (1042) The Commission noted that anti-dumping measures and any prospective horizontal trade policy measures concerning steel served distinct legal purposes and pursued different objectives; one addresses unfair trading practices causing injury to the domestic industry while the other seeks to address the negative trade-related effects of global overcapacity in the Union’s steel market. These instruments are based on different legal bases and required different evidentiary standards. They were therefore not interchangeable, and the existence, or possible future adoption, of one could not be a substitute for the other.

- (1043) The Commission noted that a quota-based measure or similar volume restriction would not address the price injury element established in the investigation. As shown in Section 4 and Section 5, the investigation established undercutting, underselling and price depression caused by dumped imports. These effects materialised regardless of import volumes. By contrast, anti-dumping duties counterbalance the unfair pricing component of the injury suffered by the Union industry.

- (1044) The Commission further noted that the imposition of duties is fully consistent with the Union’s broader steel and trade policy objectives. By contrast, allowing injurious dumping to continue unaddressed would have undermined the rationale of the Union trade policy.

- (1045) In view of the above, the Commission concluded that there were no compelling reasons to consider that the imposition of anti-dumping measures would be against the Union interest. The arguments raised by the interested parties were therefore rejected.

- (1046) The Government of Japan argued after disclosure that the tightening of the safeguard measures in April 2025 should be taken into account when assessing the development of Japanese imports. It referred to a decline in Japanese imports following the tightening and considered that this development was inconsistent with the finding that increasing Japanese imports contributed to injury.

- (1047) The Commission considered that the arguments of the Government of Japan did not alter the conclusions set out in recital (833) to (838) . In this case the findings on injury and causation rested on the verified data for the period considered. Those data show that imports from Japan increased from 126 021 tonnes in 2022 to 250 001 tonnes during the investigation period, corresponding to an increase of 98 %. Their market share also increased from 2 % to 3 %.

- (1048) The increase in Japanese imports over the period considered and their increased market share are not consistent with the assertion that Japanese imports did not increase their presence on the Union market. The Commission therefore considered that the development of Japanese imports during the period considered supported, rather than contradicted, the finding that those imports contributed to the injury suffered by the Union industry. The claim was therefore rejected.

- (1049) Also, the Japanese Producers and CSC argued that the new steel measure applicable from 1 July 2026 already provides substantial protection to the Union industry. They submitted that the Commission should take the interaction between that measure and the anti-dumping measures into account when assessing the necessity and level of the anti-dumping duties. CSC further argued that maintaining a target profit of 9,9 % in addition to the protection provided by the new measure could result in over-protection. CSC reiterated this claim in its comments on the additional final disclosure, despite the fact that the matter was not subject to the additional final disclosure and the company was instructed to only comment on the issues covered by the additional final disclosure.

- (1050) The arguments raised did not alter the conclusions set out in recital (1045). The existence of the new steel measure applicable from 1 July 2026 does not, in itself, affect the assessment of the injurious effects of the dumped imports or the level of the anti-dumping measures required to remove such injury. The two instruments serve distinct legal purposes and pursue different policy objectives. Anti-dumping duties address unfair pricing practices causing injury to the Union industry, whereas the steel measure responds to the negative effects of global overcapacity on the Union market. These instruments rely on different legal bases and different evidentiary standards and therefore cannot be considered interchangeable.

- (1051) The Commission also considered that the target profit of 9,9 % was established on the basis of the CRF-specific evidence described hereabove and reflects the level of profitability that the Union industry could reasonably achieve under normal conditions of competition. It is therefore not a level of protection resulting from, or determined by, the new steel measure. The mere coexistence of the two measures does not demonstrate that the target profit or the resulting anti-dumping measures would provide over-protection.

- (1052) The Commission therefore considered that the arguments concerning the interaction between the new steel measure and the anti-dumping measures did not provide a basis for changing the target profit or the level of the anti-dumping measures. The claims were therefore rejected.

- (1053) In its comments on the additional final disclosure, the Government of Japan reiterated that the tightening of safeguard measures in April 2025 and the subsequent decline in Japanese imports undermined the finding that imports from Japan contributed to the injury suffered by the Union industry. It also maintained that the increase in imports from the countries concerned could reflect a shift in sourcing from other third countries rather than increased import pressure. Finally, Japan argued that the Steel Overcapacity Regulation, which reduces the import quota and introduces a 50 % out-of-quota duty, would further reduce imports and therefore called into question the need for additional anti-dumping measures.

- (1054) Such matters were not subject to the additional final disclosure. In any case, the Commission’s assessment of these arguments is already set out in recitals (1046) to (1048) and (1050) to (1052) respectively. In these recitals the Commission explained why the arguments raised did not undermine its assessment of injury and causation or its conclusions on the target profit and the need for anti-dumping measures. The Commission therefore confirmed its conclusions and dismissed the claims.

#### 7.5.   **Conclusion on Union interest**

- (1055) On the basis of the above, the Commission concluded that there were no compelling reasons that it was not in the Union interest to impose measures on imports of CRF originating in countries concerned.

### 8. **DEFINITIVE ANTI-DUMPING MEASURES**

- (1056) In view of the conclusions reached with regard to dumping, injury, causation, level of measures and Union interest, and in accordance with Article 9(4) of the basic Regulation, definitive anti-dumping measures should be imposed in order to prevent further injury being caused to the Union industry by the dumped imports of the product concerned.

- (1057) On the basis of the above, the definitive anti-dumping duty rates, expressed on the CIF Union border price, customs duty unpaid, should be as follows:

| Country | Company | Dumping margin (%) | Injury margin (%) | Definitive anti-dumping duty (%) |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| India | — JSW Steel Limited — JSW Steel Coated Products Limited | 9,5 | 25,5 | 9,5 |
| India | Other cooperating companies | 9,5 | 25,5 | 9,5 |
| India | All other imports originating in India | 9,5 | 25,5 | 9,5 |
| Japan | — Nippon Steel Corporation — Daido Steel Co., Ltd. | 54,7 | 27,9 | 27,9 |
| Japan | Other cooperating companies | 54,7 | 27,9 | 27,9 |
| Japan | All other imports originating in Japan | 54,7 | 27,9 | 27,9 |
| Taiwan | — China Steel Corporation — Chung Hung Steel Corporation | 33,3 | 20,7 | 20,7 |
| Taiwan | Other cooperating companies | 33,3 | 20,7 | 20,7 |
| Taiwan | All other imports originating in Taiwan | 59,6 | 27,0 | 27,0 |
| Türkiye | Borçelik Çelik Sanayi Ticaret A.Ş. | 7,8 | 12,9 | 7,8 |
| Türkiye | Tatmetal Çelik Sanayi ve Ticaret A. Ş. | 3,0 | 17,8 | 3,0 |
| Türkiye | Other cooperating companies | 4,9 | 16,4 | 4,9 |
| Türkiye | All other imports originating in Türkiye | 7,8 | 17,8 | 7,8 |
| Vietnam | POSCO VIETNAM CO., LTD | 16,0 | 25,8 | 16,0 |
| Vietnam | Other cooperating companies | 16,0 | 25,8 | 16,0 |
| Vietnam | All other imports originating in Vietnam | 16,0 | 25,8 | 16,0 |

- (1058) The individual company anti-dumping duty rates specified in this Regulation were established on the basis of the findings of this investigation. Therefore, they reflect the situation found during this investigation in respect to these companies. These duty rates are thus exclusively applicable to imports of the product under investigation originating in the country concerned and produced by the named legal entities. Imports of the product concerned manufactured by any other company not specifically mentioned in the operative part of this Regulation, including entities related to those specifically mentioned, cannot benefit from these rates and should be subject to the duty rate applicable to ‘all other imports originating in the countries concerned’.

- (1059) A company may request the application of these individual anti-dumping duty rates if it changes subsequently the name of its entity. The request must be addressed to the Commission [^ntr158-L_202602547EN.000101-E0158]. The request must contain all the relevant information enabling to demonstrate that the change does not affect the right of the company to benefit from the duty rate which applies to it. If the change of name of the company does not affect its right to benefit from the duty rate which applies to it, a regulation about the change of name will be published in the *Official Journal of the European Union*.

- (1060) To minimise the risks of circumvention due to the difference in duty rates, special measures are needed to ensure the proper application of the individual anti-dumping duties. The application of individual anti-dumping duties is only applicable upon presentation of a valid commercial invoice to the customs authorities of the Member States. The invoice must conform to the requirements set out in Article 1(3) of this Regulation. Until such invoice is presented, imports should be subject to the anti-dumping duty applicable to ‘all other imports originating in the countries concerned’.

- (1061) While presentation of this invoice is necessary for the customs authorities of the Member States to apply the individual rates of anti-dumping duty to imports, it is not the only element to be taken into account by the customs authorities. Indeed, even if presented with an invoice meeting all the requirements set out in Article 1(3) of this Regulation, the customs authorities of Member States should carry out their usual checks and may, like in all other cases, require additional documents (shipping documents, etc.) for the purpose of verifying the accuracy of the particulars contained in the declaration and ensure that the subsequent application of the rate of duty is justified, in compliance with customs law.

- (1062) Should the exports by one of the companies benefiting from lower individual duty rates increase significantly in volume, in particular after the imposition of the measures concerned, such an increase in volume could be considered as constituting in itself a change in the pattern of trade due to the imposition of measures within the meaning of Article 13(1) of the basic Regulation. In such circumstances, an anti-circumvention investigation may be initiated, provided that the conditions for doing so are met. This investigation may, inter alia, examine the need for the removal of individual duty rate(s) and the consequent imposition of a country-wide duty.

- (1063) To ensure a proper enforcement of the anti-dumping duties, the anti-dumping duty for all other imports originating in the countries concerned should apply not only to the non-cooperating exporting producers in this investigation, but also to the producers which did not have exports to the Union during the investigation period.

- (1064) For the countries concerned where the duty rate for cooperating companies not included in the sample is lower than the duty rate applicable to all other imports originating in that country, exporting producers that did not export the product concerned to the Union during the investigation period should be able to request [^ntr159-L_202602547EN.000101-E0159] the Commission to be made subject to the anti-dumping duty rate for cooperating companies not included in the sample. The Commission should grant such request provided that three conditions are met. The new exporting producer would have to demonstrate that: (i) it did not export the product concerned to the Union during the IP; (ii) it is not related to an exporting producer that did so; and (iii) has exported the product concerned thereafter or has entered into an irrevocable contractual obligation to do so in substantial quantities.

### 9. **RETROACTIVE COLLECTION**

- (1065) As mentioned in recital (3), the Commission made imports of the product under investigation subject to registration.

- (1066) Eurofer claimed that the retroactive application of definitive anti-dumping duties should remain legally available where the conditions set out in Article 10(4) of the basic Regulation are fulfilled and imports have been registered pursuant to Article 14(5), even in the absence of provisional measures. In support of its claim, Eurofer argued that Article 10(4) does not expressly require the prior imposition of provisional measures and referred to case law concerning the objective of preventing stockpiling and preserving the effectiveness of definitive measures.

- (1067) However, the Commission considered that Article 10(4) of the basic Regulation cannot be interpreted in isolation but must be read together with Article 10 as a whole, which governs the relationship between provisional and definitive duties. In this respect, the retroactive application provided for in Article 10(4) constitutes an exceptional extension of the system of provisional measures, allowing definitive duties to be collected on registered imports made prior to the imposition of provisional duties. Therefore, the Commission considered that the retroactive application mechanism under Article 10(4) is intrinsically linked to the prior imposition of provisional measures. The Commission therefore concluded that, as provisional measures were not imposed in the present investigation, the conditions for the retroactive application of definitive anti-dumping duties pursuant to Article 10(4) of the basic Regulation were not met.

- (1068) Following disclosure, Eurofer stated that it disagreed with the Commission's conclusion that the imposition of provisional measures is a prerequisite for the retroactive collection of duties under Article 10(4) of the basic Regulation. Eurofer argued that Article 10(4) provides for a distinct mechanism for the retroactive collection of duties following registration and that the application of that provision is not conditional upon the prior imposition of provisional measures. Eurofer further argued that registration and retroactive collection are separate mechanisms and should not be considered as forming part of a single mechanism linked to provisional measures. Eurofer therefore requested that the Commission reconsider its interpretation of Article 10(4) of the basic Regulation.

- (1069) The Commission noted that registration and retroactive collection are different steps but reached a different conclusion. Registration preserves the possibility of retroactive collection should the legal conditions be met. It does not itself give a right to collect duties retroactively; that right remains conditioned to the requirements of Article 10(4), which limits retroactive collection to products entered for consumption no more than 90 days prior to the date of application of provisional measures. As provisional measures are the express reference point for calculating that period, retroactive collection under Article 10(4) cannot be applied where no provisional measures have been imposed. Furthermore, this interpretation is also consistent with the Commission’s recent practice [^ntr160-L_202602547EN.000101-E0160].

- (1070) The Commission therefore maintained that the conditions for the retroactive application of definitive anti-dumping duties pursuant to Article 10(4) of the basic Regulation were not met. The claim was therefore rejected.

### 10. **FINAL PROVISIONS**

- (1071) In view of Article 109 of Regulation (EU) 2024/2509 of the European Parliament and of the Council [^ntr161-L_202602547EN.000101-E0161], when an amount is to be reimbursed following a judgment of the Court of Justice of the European Union, the interest to be paid should be the rate applied by the European Central Bank to its principal refinancing operations, as published in the C series of the *Official Journal of the European Union* on the first calendar day of each month.

- (1072) The measures provided for in this Regulation are in accordance with the opinion of the Committee established by Article 15(1) of Regulation (EU) 2016/1036,

HAS ADOPTED THIS REGULATION:

#### Article 1

1. A definitive anti-dumping duty is imposed on imports of flat-rolled products of iron or non-alloy steel, or other alloy steel but excluding stainless steel, of all widths, cold-rolled (cold-reduced), not clad, plated or coated and not further worked than cold-rolled (cold-reduced), but not including:

- — flat-rolled products of iron or non-alloy steel, of all width, cold-rolled (cold-reduced), not clad, plated or coated, not further worked than cold-rolled, whether or not in coils, of all thickness, electrical,

- — flat-rolled products of iron or non-alloy steel, of all width, cold-rolled (cold-reduced), not clad, plated or coated, in coils, of a thickness of less than 0,35 mm, annealed (known as ‘black plates’),

- — flat-rolled products of other alloy steel, of all width, of silicon-electrical steel, and

- — flat-rolled products of alloy steel, not further worked than cold-rolled (cold-reduced), of high-speed steel,

currently falling under CN codes ex 7209 15 00 , 7209 16 90 , 7209 17 90 , 7209 18 91 , ex 7209 18 99 , ex 7209 25 00 , 7209 26 90 , 7209 27 90 , 7209 28 90 , 7211 23 30 , ex 7211 23 80 , ex 7211 29 00 , 7225 50 80 , 7226 92 00 (TARIC codes 7209 15 00 90, 7209 18 99 90, 7209 25 00 90, 7211 23 80 19, 7211 23 80 95, 7211 23 80 99, 7211 29 00 19, 7211 29 00 99), and originating in India, Japan, Taiwan, Türkiye and Vietnam.

2. The rates of the definitive anti-dumping duty applicable to the net, free-at-Union-frontier price, before duty, of the product described in paragraph 1 and produced by the companies listed below shall be as follows:

| Country | Company | Definitive anti-dumping duty (%) | TARIC additional code |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| India | — JSW Steel Limited — JSW Steel Coated Products Limited | 9,5 | 88GB |
| India | Other cooperating companies listed in the Annex | 9,5 | See Annex |
| India | All other imports originating in India | 9,5 | 8999 |
| Japan | — Nippon Steel Corporation — Daido Steel Co., Ltd. | 27,9 | 88GD |
| Japan | Other cooperating companies listed in the Annex | 27,9 | See Annex |
| Japan | All other imports originating in Japan | 27,9 | 8999 |
| Taiwan | — China Steel Corporation — Chung Hung Steel Corporation | 20,7 | 88GG |
| Taiwan | Other cooperating companies listed in the Annex | 20,7 | See Annex |
| Taiwan | All other imports originating in Taiwan | 27,0 | 8999 |
| Türkiye | Borçelik Çelik Sanayi Ticaret A.Ş. | 7,8 | 88GI |
| Türkiye | Tatmetal Çelik Sanayi ve Ticaret A. Ş. | 3,0 | 88GJ |
| Türkiye | Other cooperating companies listed in the Annex | 4,9 | See Annex |
| Türkiye | All other imports originating in Türkiye | 7,8 | 8999 |
| Vietnam | POSCO VIETNAM CO., LTD | 16,0 | 88GO |
| Vietnam | Other cooperating companies listed in the Annex | 16,0 | See Annex |
| Vietnam | All other imports originating in Vietnam | 16,0 | 8999 |

3. The application of the individual duty rates specified for the companies mentioned in paragraph 2 shall be conditional upon presentation to the Member States’ customs authorities of a valid commercial invoice, on which shall appear a declaration dated and signed by an official of the entity issuing such invoice, identified by his/her name and function, drafted as follows: *‘I, the undersigned, certify that the (volume in tonnes) of cold rolled flat steel sold for export to the European Union covered by this invoice was manufactured by (company name and address) (TARIC additional code) in (country concerned). I declare that the information provided in this invoice is complete and correct.’* Until such invoice is presented, the duty applicable to all other imports originating in the applicable country concerned, i.e. India, Japan, Taiwan, Türkiye or Vietnam shall apply.

4. Unless otherwise specified, the provisions in force concerning customs duties shall apply.

#### Article 2

Article 1(2) may be amended to add new exporting producers from Taiwan and Türkiye and make them subject to the appropriate weighted average anti-dumping duty rate for cooperating companies not included in the sample. A new exporting producer shall provide evidence that:

- (a) it did not export the goods described in Article 1(1) during the period of investigation (1 July 2024–30 June 2025);

- (b) it is not related to an exporter or producer subject to the measures imposed by this Regulation, and which could have cooperated in the original investigation; and

- (c) it has either actually exported the product concerned or has entered into an irrevocable contractual obligation to export a significant quantity to the Union after the end of the period of investigation.

#### Article 3

This Regulation shall enter into force on the day following that of its publication in the *Official Journal of the European Union*.

This Regulation shall be binding in its entirety and directly applicable in all Member States.

Done at Brussels, 13 November 2026.

***For the Commission***

***The President***

**Ursula VON DER LEYEN**
